# Sacraments & Liturgy · Reference Card

> Open book. This card is meant to be open while you work — including during
> quizzes. Nothing here is something you're expected to recall cold.

This course teaches what the Catholic Church holds about the seven sacraments and the liturgy in which they are celebrated, from within the tradition, with every claim's weight marked. Its machinery is three habits: return three verdicts (valid, licit, fruitful) and name what decides each; keep what an act **defines** apart from how a school **explains** it; and mark every text's level, no higher and no lower. Other traditions are stated as their own confessions state them and left without reply. The card holds the level of every Church text the lessons cite, each sacrament at a glance, the positions and distinctions in the wording the lessons verified, the other traditions' accounts, and the traps. The ladder belongs to [`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md); where that course and this card differ, that course wins.

## Authority and citations

### Levels of authority

**The ladder, as this course uses it.** Rung 1: proposed as divinely revealed, solemnly defined or taught infallibly by the ordinary universal magisterium; owed faith. Rung 2: definitive, to be held ([definitive teaching](#definitive-teaching)). Rung 3: authentic ordinary magisterium, owed religious submission. Rung 4: theologically certain or common teaching of the schools. Rung 5: freely disputed among Catholics. Find the **act**, not the book you read the sentence in; ask what the act proposes the claim **as**; if the level is not manifest, **default down** (can. 749 §3). Overstating and understating are both errors, graded strictly. Reading a genre's weight is [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)'s.

**Phrases this card uses.**

- *Canon against chapter*: in Trent's decrees "the chapter expounds; the canon is where the definition bites". Canons with anathema are rung 1 exactly as worded; chapter teaching no canon repeats is **at least rung 3** (Session VI's chapters are fenced more heavily, but its ch. 4 on desire stays at least rung 3: [baptism of desire](#baptism-of-desire)).
- *Rung 1 as worded*; *rung 1 for what its words reach*: the definition is narrower than its slogan (XIII can. 9 binds that the precept binds, not its terms; XXII can. 6 that the Canon has no error, not that it is old or sole; XXIV can. 7 that the Church does not err, not by itself that indissolubility is revealed; VII can. 13 not reaching the supreme authority).
- *At least rung 3*: conciliar teaching that defined nothing new (Vatican II, Florence's doctrinal statements), or a doctrinal chapter.
- *Practical instruction, changeable*: Florence's matter and form for each sacrament.
- *Law, no rung*; *discipline*; *liturgical law*: canons, rubrics, calendars, indults, motu proprios, instructions. Binding, changeable, no doctrinal rung. Instructions cannot derogate from law (can. 34 §2). Ecclesiastical law can still decide validity (faculties, form, the impediment of orders).
- *Binding for validity, no defining formula*: *Sacramentum Ordinis* (1947) and *Divinae Consortium Naturae* (1971).
- *No magisterial level*: theologians, Fathers as authors, the ITC, commissions, historians, other traditions' texts. Weighed by their arguments.
- *Freely disputed (rung 5)*: every account of *how* a sacrament causes, of how the one sacrifice is present, of what the character is; limbo; the female diaconate; whether attrition needs love; development or rupture in *Amoris Laetitia*; the prudence of particular reforms.

**The levels in this course, in one place.**

- **Rung 1.** Seven sacraments, all instituted by Christ, each truly one; they contain and confer the grace they signify on those who place no obstacle, *ex opere operato*; character in three; the minister's intention required, his holiness not; heretics' baptism true; necessity of baptism; infant baptism; remission of original guilt, concupiscence not truly sin; Firmiter's change and baptism "by anyone"; the real presence, conversion, the term, concomitance's fact, permanence, latria; no divine precept of both kinds, Christ whole under either; the Mass a true, propitiatory sacrifice, derogating nothing from the cross; "Do this" instituted priests; penance's canons; anointing's canons; orders' canons; matrimony's doctrinal canons; indulgences' power and usefulness; suffrages help the dead; temporal punishment can remain; the Roman Canon free from error.
- **Rung 2 (at least).** Only priests anoint validly (2005); *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis* (rung 1 against rung 2 disputed); *Apostolicae Curae* (rung 2); the absolute indissolubility of a ratified, consummated marriage (2000).
- **At least rung 3.** Trent's chapter teaching no canon repeats (the substance untouched, XXI ch. 2; the identity formula, XXII ch. 2; concomitance as reason; "not in his natural mode"); desire and blood supply for baptism; Florence's doctrinal statements; SC's and LG's teaching; PO 16; contract and sacrament inseparable; the seal's admitting no exception; the dependence of fruit on disposition; sacramental grace; sacramentals working by the Church's intercession.
- **Rung 3.** *Mediator Dei*; *Mysterium Fidei*; *Inter Insigniores* as an act; FC 84's practice; AL and the Buenos Aires texts; *Ecclesia de Eucharistia*; *Desiderio Desideravi*; *Gestis verbisque* and the 2008 and 2020 responses; the 2001 Addai-Mari judgment; the treasury; *Cum postquam*; *Samaritanus Bonus*; *Fiducia Supplicans*'s account of blessings at most.
- **Rung 4.** Internal and virtual intention; reviviscence of baptism; the words of institution alone consecrate at the instant; the spouses as ministers.
- **Rung 5.** See *Freely disputed* above.

*Lessons:* every lesson marks levels; see especially [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md), [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md), [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Levels of authority used in this course

Every Church text the lessons cite, with the level the lessons mark, chronological; the ladder itself is [`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)'s. "Cited" means a lesson uses the text without assigning a rung; do not invent one. DH numbers appear only where a lesson verified them.

### The seven at a glance

At reference level, as the lessons state them. Each sacrament's entry has the detail; levels as marked there.

| Sacrament | Matter (remote; proximate) | Form | Minister (validity; liceity) | Recipient | Effects | Character | Defining texts and level |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| [Baptism](#effects-of-baptism) | true and natural water; immersion or pouring | Latin *Ego te baptizo in nomine Patris et Filii et Spiritus Sancti*; Byzantine passive "The servant of God, N., is baptized ..." | validly anyone with the form and the intention, even the unbaptized; ordinarily bishop, presbyter, deacon, others lawfully in necessity (can. 861) | any unbaptized person; an adult must intend to receive | original and personal sin and all punishment remitted; grace and virtues; character; incorporation | yes | Trent V cans. 4-5; VII can. 9; Baptism cans. 2, 4, 5, 12-14; *Firmiter*: **rung 1**. Form's wording at least rung 3 |
| [Confirmation](#confirmation) | chrism consecrated by a bishop; anointing of the forehead by the laying on of the hand | *Accipe signaculum doni Spiritus Sancti* (Latin, 1971); "The seal of the gift of the Holy Spirit" (Byzantine) | bishop the ordinary minister; Latin presbyter validly only with a faculty; every Eastern presbyter | the baptized not yet confirmed | special strength of the Spirit, bound more perfectly to the Church (LG 11) | yes | VII, Confirmation cans. 1, 3; VII can. 9: **rung 1**; the rite: law (1971) |
| [Eucharist](#real-presence) | wheat bread and grape wine (a little water mixed); the consecration within the anaphora | the Saviour's words (Florence; Trent XIII ch. 3: at least rung 3; "alone, at the instant": rung 4); Addai and Mari judged valid (2001) | only a validly ordained priest confects (can. 900 §1) | the baptized; no conscious mortal sin without confession first (can. 916) | the whole Christ present; nourishment; unity of the mystical body; pledge of glory; a true and propitiatory sacrifice | no | XIII cans. 1-4, 6-9, 11; XXI cans. 1-4; XXII cans. 1-4: **rung 1** |
| [Penance](#penance) | the penitent's acts (contrition, confession, satisfaction) "as it were the matter" | "I absolve thee" (*Ego te absolvo ...*): taught (ch. 3) | a priest, with the faculty for validity (can. 966), any priest in danger of death (can. 976) | the baptized who sinned after baptism, sorry (contrition or attrition) with purpose of amendment | reconciliation with God and the Church; guilt and eternal punishment remitted; temporal may remain | no | XIV cans. 1-15: **rung 1** |
| [Anointing of the sick](#anointing-of-the-sick) | olive or other plant oil duly blessed (by a bishop, or any presbyter in necessity within the rite); anointing of forehead and hands | *Per istam sanctam unctionem ...* (1972) | every priest and only a priest (proper minister rung 1; exclusivity at least rung 2) | the baptized with use of reason, beginning to be in danger from sickness or old age; repeatable | grace, remission of sins, comfort; bodily health at times | no | XIV extreme unction cans. 1-4: **rung 1**; CDF 2005: at least rung 2 |
| [Holy orders](#three-degrees-of-orders) | the imposition of hands (for priests the first, silent one) | the consecratory prayer that determines it (1947; 1968) | a consecrated bishop (can. 1012); mandate, co-consecrators, dimissorials for liceity | a baptized male (can. 1024; OS at least rung 2) | power and grace of the degree; bishops and presbyters act *in persona Christi capitis*, deacons serve in the diakonia | yes | XXIII cans. 1, 3, 4, 6, 7: **rung 1**; *Sacramentum Ordinis*: binding for validity |
| [Matrimony](#matrimony) | (matter and form of consent: school analysis, no level) | the words of consent (Aquinas; no level) | Latin: the spouses, "ordinarily understood" (rung 4), before the Church's qualified witness and two witnesses (form: law); East: the priest who crowns (validity) | a baptized man and woman, free of impediments, consenting | the bond, perpetual and exclusive; the grace of the state | no | XXIV doctrinal canons **rung 1** (can. 7 as worded); absolute indissolubility at least rung 2 |

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md); Florence's version of the same table: [Decree for the Armenians](#decree-for-the-armenians)

### Valid, licit, fruitful

Three separate verdicts: whether a sacrament happened, whether it happened lawfully, and whether its grace reached the recipient. Return all three and name what decides each.

| Verdict | Asked of | Decided by | Classic cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Valid** | every celebration | matter, form, minister (with a faculty where the law requires it: penance, Latin presbyteral confirmation, assisting at marriage), the minister's intention, the recipient (baptized for the other six; an adult's consent; a male for orders; capacity and form for marriage) | "we baptize you" (CDF 2020) and "Creator, Redeemer, Sanctifier" (CDF 2008): invalid, baptized again absolutely; completely gluten-free hosts; a deacon's "absolution" or "anointing"; attempted ordination of a woman; a Latin-Eastern wedding with only a deacon (can. 1108 §3) |
| **Licit** | every celebration, valid or not | the rite, the law and any required permission (can. 846 §1); the recipient's canonical condition | a suspended priest's Mass; a rewritten preface with the consecration intact; an infant baptized without founded hope of upbringing (can. 868); a breach of the fast; general absolution outside can. 961; a 1962 Mass without the reserved permission; a bishop consecrated without mandate |
| **Fruitful** | only a valid sacrament | the recipient's disposition: no [obstacle](#obex) (unbelief, attachment to mortal sin) | communion in conscious mortal sin: a true reception, sacramentally only, unfruitful, a sacrilege; an adult baptized insincerely: character without grace, revived on repentance ([reviviscence](#reviviscence)); an infant places no obstacle |

The chain runs one way: validity first, then fruit; liceity beside it. Validity's requirements belong to the supreme authority alone (can. 841). A minister's sin and unbelief touch none of the three for the recipient; his intention touches validity. Sacramentals and indulgences have no such triple: a blessing's validity arises only where it changes a thing's status (can. 1169 §1), and an indulgence is gained in full, in part or not at all.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md); applied in [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Citations and how to read them

| Form | Points at |
|---|---|
| Trent, Session (Roman numeral), ch. or can. | a decree of the Council of Trent, in Waterworth's 1848 translation; a chapter expounds, a canon defines |
| VII, Canons on Baptism can. *n*; XIV, On Extreme Unction can. *n* | the separate series of canons within a session |
| DH *n* | a place in Denzinger-Hünermann, an index, **not** a weight; "old Denzinger" (Dz) is the 1911 Latin numbering |
| CCC *n* | a Catechism paragraph, which keeps the weight of its source |
| can. *n*; CCEO can. *n* | the 1983 Latin Code (as amended) or the 1990 Eastern Code: law, not a rung |
| ST III q. *n* a. *n* ad *n*; Suppl.; II-II | Aquinas, *Summa Theologiae* (English Dominican translation); the *Supplement* is compiled from his *Sentences* commentary |
| SC, LG, UR, OE, PO, GS *n* | a section of a Vatican II document |
| MD, MF, OS, FC, AL, EdE, DD, RS, LA, VL, CLP, SP, TC, GV, FS, DPP *n* | *Mediator Dei*, *Mysterium Fidei*, *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis*, *Familiaris Consortio*, *Amoris Laetitia*, *Ecclesia de Eucharistia*, *Desiderio Desideravi*, *Redemptionis Sacramentum*, *Liturgiam Authenticam*, *Varietates Legitimae*, *Comme le prévoit*, *Summorum Pontificum*, *Traditionis Custodes*, *Gestis verbisque*, *Fiducia Supplicans*, the *Directory on Popular Piety* |
| GIRM, GILH, GNLY, OLM, EM, ED, EI | the General Instructions of the Roman Missal and of the Liturgy of the Hours; the Universal Norms on the Liturgical Year; the Lectionary; *Eucharisticum Mysterium*; the Ecumenical Directory (1993); the *Enchiridion Indulgentiarum* (norms "N.") |
| ANF, NPNF | Schaff's *Ante-Nicene* and *Nicene and Post-Nicene Fathers*, public domain |
| DR | Douay-Rheims (Challoner); its psalms follow the Greek and Vulgate numbering ([psalm numbering](#psalm-numbering)), and John 6 runs one verse ahead from 6:51b |
| (lesson's translation), (lesson's gloss) | the lesson's own rendering of a Latin or Greek original |

*From* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md); the citation conventions are [`fundamental-theology` 4.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-05-reading-a-documents-weight.md)'s

## Terms

Technical vocabulary in first-appearance order. Greek and Latin are transliterated; glosses marked *(lesson's gloss)* are the lessons' own renderings. A term with a full entry links to it.

| Term | Means | First used |
|---|---|---|
| [*mysterion*, *sacramentum*](#mysterion-and-sacramentum) | God's plan hidden and now disclosed (Paul); in Latin also a binding oath of service | [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md) |
| [sacrament](#sacrament) | a sign of a sacred thing that does what it shows | [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md) |
| *rememorativum*, *demonstrativum*, *prognosticum* | a sacrament's threefold signification: reminder of the Passion, indication of present grace, prognostic of glory (ST III q.60 a.3) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md) |
| [*sacramentum tantum*, *res et sacramentum*, *res tantum*](#threefold-analysis) | sign only; a reality that is also a sign; reality only | [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md) |
| [principal and instrumental cause](#instrumental-causality) | one acts by its own form, the other only as moved; joined instrument (hand) and separate instrument (stick) | [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md) |
| [*veluti sacramentum*](#church-as-sacrament) | "like a sacrament" (LG 1): the Church, not an eighth sacrament | [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md) |
| [matter and form](#matter-and-form) | the thing and the words that fix its meaning | [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) |
| [remote and proximate matter](#remote-and-proximate-matter) | the thing itself; its use in the rite | [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) |
| *tanquam* | "as": Florence's things *as* matter, an analogy | [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) |
| [valid, licit, fruitful](#valid-licit-fruitful) | it happened; it happened lawfully; its grace reached the recipient | [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) |
| [intention](#intention-of-the-minister); virtual, habitual | at least to do what the Church does; an intention still at work though the mind wanders | [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) |
| [Donatist principle](#donatist-principle) | validity depending on the minister's holiness: rejected | [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) |

| Consilium | the commission that drafted the reformed books | [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md) |
| unique expression; ordinary and extraordinary form | TC's and SP's legal names for the Roman Rite's books | [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md) |
| *recognitio*; *confirmatio* | Rome's review (now for adaptations); its ratification (for translations) | [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md) |
| dynamic equivalence | the theorists' name, not *Comme le prévoit*'s | [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md) |
| [*ars celebrandi*](#ars-celebrandi) | the art of proper celebration | [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md) |
| *graviora delicta* | the gravest offences, reserved to the doctrinal dicastery | [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md) |
| [sacramentals](#sacramentals) | sacred signs the Church instituted | [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md) |
| constitutive, invocative | the manuals' kinds of blessing; not CCC's terms | [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md) |
| [superstition](#superstition) | worship to whom, or in a manner, it ought not be given | [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md) |

## The sacramental principle

Positions and distinctions from Module 1: what a sacrament is, what makes one happen, what it does and for whom, and why the Church counts seven. Every later module runs this machinery.

### Sacrament

A sign of a sacred thing that does what it shows: Augustine supplied the sign, the schoolmen and Trent the cause.

Augustine on John 15:3 (*Tractates on John* 80.3, NPNF): "Take away the word, and the water is neither more nor less than water. The word is added to the element, and there results the Sacrament"; the Latin as Aquinas quotes it is *accedit verbum ad elementum, et fit sacramentum* (ST III q.60 a.4). The word cleanses "not because it is uttered, but because it is believed": the Church's word of faith, which cleanses even an infant. The Roman Catechism calls "a sign of a sacred thing" Augustine's definition and gives its other common form, "a visible sign of an invisible grace, instituted for our justification"; Trent applies it to the Eucharist (Session XIII ch. 3). Aquinas narrows the genus: a sign of a holy thing "so far as it makes men holy" (q.60 a.2), so creation and holy water signify holy things without being sacraments (ad 1, ad 3). The Catechism's summary (CCC 1131): efficacious signs of grace, instituted by Christ and entrusted to the Church. Why signs at all: human beings are led through bodily things to spiritual ones (q.61 a.1), an argument from fittingness. Each sacrament signifies across three times (q.60 a.3): "a reminder of the past" (the Passion), "an indication" of grace now given, "a prognostic" of glory (*rememorativum, demonstrativum, prognosticum*).

**Level:** that the New Law's sacraments contain and confer the grace they signify, **rung 1** (Trent VII can. 6); the Augustinian definition is the tradition's constant teaching, and catechisms carry the weight of what they restate.

**Does not assert:** a symbol that only reminds (reminding is one of three significations); that every holy sign is a sacrament ([sacramentals](#sacramentals)); *how* the sign causes ([instrumental causality](#instrumental-causality), rung 5).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md); the strict definition that made counting possible is [the number seven](#the-number-seven)

### Mysterion and sacramentum

Paul's word for God's plan disclosed, rendered by a Latin word for a binding oath: the translation gained a rite that does something and lost the plan as a whole.

*Mystērion* in Paul is a purpose hidden for ages and now disclosed (lesson's gloss; Col 1:26, Eph 3:9). The Latin Bible often renders it *sacramentum*: Eph 1:9, 3:9, 1 Tim 3:16, and Eph 5:32, *sacramentum hoc magnum est*, the wording Trent quotes in Session XXIV. Challoner's Douay-Rheims says "mystery" in the first three and keeps "This is a great sacrament" only at Eph 5:32 (Greek *mega mystērion*, "great mystery"). The Roman secular sense: an oath pledging service, the soldier's oath of enlistment the Roman Catechism's example; Tertullian plays on it, "no agreement between the divine and the human sacrament" (*On Idolatry* 19, ANF 3). Augustine's *sacramentum* covers a whole family of sacred signs (the salt given to catechumens is "the sacrament of the catechumen", *On Baptism* IV.21.29); Pseudo-Dionysius's *mysterion* (lesson's gloss: a sacred rite of initiation) is as broad.

**Gained:** a rite that binds and enrols. **Lost** (once "sacrament" narrowed to the seven): Paul's sense of the whole plan revealed, which is why translators went back to "mystery".

**Does not license:** reading Eph 5:32's Latin as proof that matrimony is a sacrament; Trent claims only that Paul "intimates" marriage's grace ([matrimony](#matrimony)).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)

### Instrumental causality

Only God causes grace as principal cause; the sacrament causes it as his instrument, the way the axe shapes the couch by the craftsman's art.

Aquinas, ST III q.62 a.1: a principal cause acts by its own form, an instrument only as moved, so the effect resembles the principal agent (the couch resembles the craftsman's art, not the axe). Grace is a share in the divine nature, so God alone causes it principally. Christ's humanity is a **joined** instrument, like a hand; the sacrament a **separate** one, like a stick the hand moves; the power runs from the Godhead, through the humanity and its Passion, into the rite (q.62 a.5). A visible instrument can signify its hidden effect, hence the "common expression" that the sacraments "effect what they signify" (q.62 a.1 ad 1). The instrumental power is passing (q.62 a.4). Aquinas rejects the rival on which sacraments are tokens, like a leaden coin worth a hundred pounds only by the king's decree: then they would be "mere signs" (q.62 a.1). Other schools proposed "moral" or "intentional" causality (the lessons name no one).

Applied to the minister ([1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)): an instrument's other qualities are accidental, "a pipe ... of silver or lead" carries water either way (q.64 a.5), so the minister's sin and unbelief do not reach the effect (a.9); but a *living* instrument moves his own limbs, so he must subject himself to the principal agent by intending what Christ and the Church do (a.8 ad 1). The same tool serves for Scripture: God the principal author, the writer a living instrument.

**Level:** theology, **no magisterial level**. Trent defined *that* the sacraments confer grace (rung 1), not *how*; the mode of sacramental causality is **rung 5, freely disputed** (Thomist instrumental, "moral", "intentional").

**Assumed from:** [`fundamental-theology` 3.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-01-inspiration.md), [`thomistic-synthesis` 5.2](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/05-02-providence-and-secondary-causes.md), [`christology` 3.4](../christology/lessons/03-04-the-grace-of-the-head.md), [`christology` 5.1](../christology/lessons/05-01-the-paschal-mystery-and-the-threefold-office.md).

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)

### Sacraments of the Old Law

Israel's rites (circumcision, the Paschal lamb) signified faith in the Passion to come; they did not cause grace.

Aquinas, ST III q.62 a.6: the ancients were justified by faith in the Passion to come, and their rites signified that faith. An efficient cause cannot act before it exists, though faith can be moved by a future end. Florence, Decree for the Armenians (1439), says the seven "differ greatly" from them (lesson's translation): "For those did not cause grace, but only prefigured that it would be given through the passion of Christ; these of ours both contain grace and confer it on those who receive them worthily."

**Level:** that the New Law's sacraments differ from the Old Law's by more than ceremonies and outward rites, **rung 1** (Trent VII can. 2); Florence's sentence carries the weight the [Decree for the Armenians](#decree-for-the-armenians) has; Aquinas's reason, no magisterial level.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md)

### Threefold analysis

Every sacrament can be sorted into three layers: the sign only, a reality that is also a sign, and the reality only.

| Sacrament | *sacramentum tantum* (sign only) | *res et sacramentum* (reality and sign) | *res tantum* (reality only) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Baptism (ST III q.66 a.1) | the washing joined to the words (not the water in the font) | the baptismal [character](#sacramental-character) | inward justification |
| Eucharist (q.73 aa.3, 6) | the species of bread and wine | Christ's true body and blood | the unity of the mystical body |
| Penance (q.84 a.1 ad 3) | the outward acts of penitent and priest | inward repentance | forgiveness of sin |
| Matrimony (Suppl. q.42 a.3 ad 2) | the words and acts of consent | the bond | the grace |

Innocent III already sorts the Eucharist this way in *Cum Marthae circa* (29 November 1202, to John, former archbishop of Lyons), answering a question, not defining. Where the inner layers sit (q.73 a.1 ad 3): in baptism both are in the recipient; in the Eucharist the middle layer is in the matter itself, so the sacrament is complete at the consecration and its last layer waits for a communicant. Aquinas sides against Hugh of St Victor, who had called baptism "water" (q.66 a.1).

**Keep apart:** the threefold **signification** of q.60 a.3 (reminder of the past, indication of present grace, prognostic of glory) concerns what one sign signifies across time; the character appears only in the layering.

**Level:** scholastic analysis, **no level**. What the layers name may be defined (baptism's character, rung 1, VII can. 9; the real presence, rung 1, XIII can. 1); that they are the middle layer is analysis.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md); the penance row is [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)'s, the marriage row [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)'s

### Church as sacrament

The Church is "like a sacrament": the visible sign and instrument within which the seven are given, not an eighth.

*Lumen Gentium* 1: the Church is, in Christ, *veluti sacramentum*, a sign and instrument of intimate union with God and of the unity of the whole human race; LG 9 and 48 ("universal sacrament of salvation"). Otto Semmelroth's "primordial sacrament" (*Die Kirche als Ursakrament*, 1953) is a theologian's term. Rahner grounds the [institution](#institution-by-christ) of the seven in the Church as the fundamental sacrament.

**Level:** **at least rung 3**, defined nowhere; "primordial sacrament", no level.

**Does not assert:** an eighth sacrament (Trent's seven, rung 1, untouched).

**Owned by:** [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 1.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/01-04-people-of-god-communion-sacrament.md), whose card has the full entry.

*Lessons:* [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)

### Matter and form

The thing is the matter, the words the form, because the words fix what the thing means: water alone is ambiguous, "I baptize you" makes it a spiritual cleansing.

Washing "may be ordained to bodily cleanliness, to the health of the body, to amusement" (ST III q.64 a.8); the words fix the signification (q.60 a.6), and in a composite the form determines the matter (q.60 a.7). Early thirteenth-century schoolmen named the pair with Aristotle's terms (William of Auxerre usually credited). The pair separates the essential from the ceremonial (the white garment adorns a baptism; water and words make it), makes the form decisive, and gives the **test for changes**: the words' essential sense, not their sound (q.60 a.8), which is why the Byzantine passive form of baptism is valid ([form of baptism](#form-of-baptism)).

Florence, Decree for the Armenians (22 November 1439; the final clause is DH 1312), lesson's translation: "All these sacraments are completed by three things: by things as the matter, by words as the form, and by the person of the minister who confers the sacrament with the intention of doing what the Church does. If any of these is lacking, the sacrament is not completed." Read its shape: an **instruction** for one church, practical, not a definition (the older Denzinger's editors say so; it is almost word for word from a short work of Aquinas); *tanquam*, "as": an analogy, not a claim that a rite is a substance; three elements (intention folded into the minister, the recipient left out); a sanction on completion only. *Gestis verbisque* 12 (DDF, 2024): the Magisterium, in dialogue with theology, designated the essential elements as matter and form, with the minister's intention added; the Church has not always indicated them univocally.

Eucharistic matter as a worked rule: wheat bread and grape wine; completely gluten-free hosts are invalid matter, low-gluten hosts valid, *mustum* valid, with the Ordinary's permission (CDF circular letter, 24 July 2003, as quoted by the CDW circular of 15 June 2017); *Redemptionis Sacramentum* 48 ("commonly considered" wheat bread) and 50.

**Level:** true and natural water for baptism, **rung 1** (VII, Canons on Baptism can. 2). The matter-form vocabulary is the Magisterium's working language, **not defined**; hylomorphism as metaphysics, **no level** ([`metaphysics` 2.3](../metaphysics/lessons/02-03-hylomorphism-and-composition.md)). Florence's clause: instruction, not definition.

**Does not license:** "a school's vocabulary, so the essentials are negotiable": what the supreme authority fixes for validity binds (can. 841).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md); each sacrament's matter and form are in [the seven at a glance](#the-seven-at-a-glance)

### Remote and proximate matter

Remote matter is the thing itself; proximate matter is its use in the rite.

Water, wheat bread, oil are remote matter; the pouring or immersion, the anointing, are proximate matter. In penance the penitent's acts are "as it were the matter" ([acts of the penitent](#acts-of-the-penitent)); in matrimony the matter and form of consent are school analysis with no level ([minister of matrimony](#minister-of-matrimony)).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)

### Minister

Who *can* is validity; who *may* is liceity; and the minister's holiness and faith are neither.

Not all Christians have power over all the sacraments (Trent VII can. 10, rung 1). Only a validly ordained priest confects the Eucharist (can. 900 §1); penance, and confirmation by a Latin presbyter, need a faculty **for validity** (cann. 966, 882; [faculties](#faculties)); anyone can baptize validly, lawfully only in necessity or as a designated catechist (can. 861 §2; [minister of baptism](#minister-of-baptism)). Can. 841: only the supreme authority fixes what validity requires. Holiness and faith are not required ([Donatist principle](#donatist-principle); can. 12). Suspension removes the permission, not the power: a suspended priest's Mass is valid and illicit (ST III q.64 a.9 ad 3).

**Level:** VII can. 10, **rung 1**; the canons cited, law, no rung.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md); each sacrament's minister is in [the seven at a glance](#the-seven-at-a-glance)

### Intention of the minister

The minister must intend at least to do what the Church does: doing, not believing.

Trent VII can. 11 (DH 1611), Waterworth: "If any one saith, that, in ministers, when they effect, and confer the sacraments, there is not required the intention at least of doing what the Church does; let him be anathema." An unbeliever can intend the Church's act while thinking it empty (ST III q.64 a.9 ad 1); *Gestis verbisque* 18 keeps intention apart from faith and moral condition. An intention to **mock** voids (an actor baptizing in a film baptizes no one); a wicked purpose **beyond** the sacrament does not (q.64 a.10). A wandering mind still acts on the intention brought to the font: Aquinas calls it "habitual" (q.64 a.8 ad 3), the later manuals *virtual*, reserving "habitual" for one no longer at work; both hold it sufficient.

**Internal or external.** Ambrosius Catharinus, a Dominican theologian at Trent, held a seriously performed external rite sufficient even if the minister inwardly withholds. On 7 December 1690 the Holy Office under Alexander VIII condemned, among 31 Jansenist propositions, prop. 28 (old Denzinger 1318; lesson's translation): "Baptism is valid when conferred by a minister who observes the whole external rite and form of baptizing, but inwardly in his heart resolves: I do not intend what the Church does." The censures were global. Since then theologians commonly require an internal intention, **presumed** whenever the rite is seriously performed; a positive doubt is remedied by conditional baptism (can. 869 §1). Aquinas's answer to the worry that no one could be sure of his baptism: the minister acts in the person of the whole Church, whose intention the words express, and this suffices "except the contrary be expressed" (q.64 a.8 ad 2). The opposite edge, a correct rite with a corrupted intention, is the Latter-day Saints case ([conditional baptism](#conditional-baptism)).

**The recipient.** An adult must intend to receive: one who never consents and expressly refuses receives neither the grace nor the character (Innocent III, *Maiores Ecclesiae causas*, 1201; ST III q.68 a.7; can. 865 §1).

**Level:** VII can. 11, **rung 1**. The 1690 decree: papal act of the authentic magisterium with global censures; the proposition **excluded, not declared heretical** (as [`moral-theology` 4.1](../moral-theology/lessons/04-01-casuistry-and-the-probabilism-controversy.md) marks it). Internal intention required, virtual sufficient: **common teaching, rung 4**. Innocent III's decretal: **at least rung 3**.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)

### Donatist principle

The claim that a sacrament's validity depends on the minister's holiness: false, and defined false.

A minister in mortal sin who observes the essentials confers the sacrament (Trent VII can. 12, DH 1612); baptism given even by heretics in the Trinity's name, with the Church's intention, is true baptism (VII, Canons on Baptism can. 4, DH 1617). A priest in mortal sin who says Mass correctly: valid, though he sins (ST III q.64 a.6). Augustine's case against the Donatists, and his "having" without "profiting", is [`patristics` 4.4](../patristics/lessons/04-04-augustine-against-the-donatists.md); Cyprian and the rebaptism dispute, [`patristics` 2.4](../patristics/lessons/02-04-cyprian-of-carthage.md).

**The corollary [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md) draws:** one premise does two jobs. The minister is Christ's living instrument, so his holiness and faith are accidental (q.64 aa.5, 9), but he must subject himself by intending (a.8 ad 1). The Donatist principle settles **holiness**, not **intention**.

**Level:** both canons **rung 1**.

**Owned by:** [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-02-holy.md) (as a mark of the Church).

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md)

### Substance of the sacraments

The Church may arrange how the sacraments are given; she cannot touch what Christ fixed in them.

Trent, Session XXI ch. 2 (16 July 1562; DH 1728), Waterworth: in the dispensation of the sacraments the Church may ordain or change what she judges expedient, "their substance being untouched" (*salva illorum substantia*); the chapter is the premise of XXI can. 2. Pius XII, *Sacramentum Ordinis* (1947) §1, glosses the substance as what Christ himself, as revelation's sources show, established to be kept in the sign. *Gestis verbisque* §§11-17 (DDF, 2 February 2024): arbitrary changes endanger validity case by case; any change to a sacramental formula is **gravely illicit** (note 31); the Church has not always indicated the substance univocally (§12). SC 21 and *Mediator Dei* 50: immutable, divinely instituted elements and changeable ones; SC 22 §3: no one, not even a priest, may add, remove or change anything in the liturgy on his own authority; CCC 1125: not even the supreme authority may change the liturgy arbitrarily.

**The power at work (dispensation):** the cup withdrawn and restored ([3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)); Florence's porrection set aside for validity in 1947 ([5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)); Paul VI's 1972 matter, form and anointings, and a presbyter's blessing of the oil in necessity (can. 999) after the Holy Office's 1611 censure ([4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)); the 1971 confirmation formula ([2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md)); celibacy; the 1969 Missal ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)). **The limit (substance):** *Inter Insigniores* §4 and *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis* place the recipient of priestly ordination in the substance, hence "no authority whatsoever", not "forbidden" ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)). Which parts of a given rite are substance is theology until an act like 1947 settles it.

**Level:** XXI ch. 2, a doctrinal chapter and constant teaching, **at least rung 3**; *Gestis verbisque*, **rung 3**; *Sacramentum Ordinis*'s doctrinal ground, at least rung 3.

*Lessons:* [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Ex opere operato

"From the work worked" (lesson's gloss): grace is given by the rite done as Christ instituted it, because Christ acts in it, not by anyone's holiness.

Coined around 1200 (Peter of Poitiers usually credited). Trent VII can. 8: grace is conferred "through the act performed", and faith alone in the divine promise does not suffice to obtain it; can. 6: the sacraments contain and confer grace on those who place no obstacle; can. 7: as far as God's part goes, always and to all who receive rightly. CCC 1127-1128.

**What it does not mean** (the lesson's four):

1. **Not magic.** Magic bends a power to the operator's will; here Christ bound his own action to rites he instituted.
2. **Not a power in the rite apart from God.** The efficacy flows from Christ's Passion (ST III q.62 a.5).
3. **Not "disposition doesn't matter".** The fruit depends on it (CCC 1128; [obex](#obex)).
4. **Not "the minister's intention doesn't matter".** His holiness doesn't; his intention does (VII can. 11).

**Level:** VII cans. 6-8, **rung 1**; *how* the rite causes, rung 5.

**Other side:** the Lutheran confessions reject it as the schools taught it ([Lutheran confessions on the sacraments](#lutheran-confessions-on-the-sacraments)).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md); sacramentals by contrast: [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Ex opere operantis

"From the work of the one working" (lesson's gloss): what the minister's or recipient's own faith and devotion contribute.

Not a rival to *ex opere operato*: *Mediator Dei* (1947) §36 sets the two side by side. Sacramentals act *ex opere operantis Ecclesiae*: their efficacy is "due rather to the action of the church", holy and in closest union with her Head (MD 27; CCC 1670: they prepare to receive grace and dispose to cooperate with it), and reaches the user through his faith; not "merely psychological". The *Enchiridion of Indulgences*' contrast of *opus operantis* with *opus operatum* echoes the same pair ([4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)).

**Level:** MD **rung 3**; that sacramentals act this way, **at least rung 3**; saying they act *ex opere operato* is a strict error.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Obex

The bar (lesson's gloss): the obstacle that blocks a valid sacrament's grace in an adult.

An adult's obstacle is unbelief or attachment to mortal sin (ST III q.69 a.9 and ad 3). Trent VII can. 6: grace is conferred on those "who do not place an obstacle". Conscious mortal sin bars the Eucharist's fruit (ST III q.79 a.3; can. 916, law). The hard edge (q.79 a.3): one in mortal sin "of which he is not conscious, and for which he has no attachment", approaching devoutly, receives the charity that perfects contrition; the obstacle is the will's conscious attachment, and nobody can audit it (explanation, no level; CCC 1395: the Eucharist is not ordered to forgiving mortal sin). An infant places no obstacle. The Apology of the Augsburg Confession, art. XIII, rejects "provided he do not place a hindrance" as insufficient.

**Level:** VII can. 6, **rung 1**; Aquinas's analysis, no level.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md)

### Reviviscence

A valid but unfruitful sacrament takes effect when the obstacle is removed.

Augustine, *On Baptism* I.12.18; Aquinas, ST III q.69 a.10: the character is "like a form", grace its effect; a heavy body held up falls the moment the support goes. Penance removes the obstacle as an indirect cause; baptism then remits the pre-baptismal sins as direct cause; later sins are remitted by penance, not as to the whole debt of punishment. One baptized in deceit who later repents is not baptized again.

| Sacrament | Reviviscence |
|---|---|
| Baptism | common teaching (Augustine, Aquinas) |
| Confirmation, order | commonly held, by analogy |
| Matrimony, anointing | held more tentatively |
| Penance, Eucharist | generally said not to arise (in penance the penitent's acts are "as it were the matter", XIV can. 4) |

**Level:** no council defined it. Baptism, **rung 4**; beyond baptism, **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md)

### Sacramental character

Baptism, confirmation and order mark the soul for good, so they are never repeated.

Trent VII can. 9: these three imprint "a certain spiritual and indelible Sign", on account of which they cannot be repeated; XXIII can. 4: one who has once been a priest cannot become a layman again. Can. 845 §1 draws the consequence; can. 290: sacred ordination once validly received never becomes invalid, only the clerical state is lost. LG 11: the baptismal character destines the faithful for Christian worship; CCC 1121: an indelible configuration to Christ. Character without grace: an adult needs right faith for the grace, not for the character, since "the sacrament is not perfected by the righteousness of the minister or of the recipient of Baptism, but by the power of God" (ST III q.68 a.8). Only the unconfirmed can be confirmed (can. 889 §1); a doubtful baptism is repeated only conditionally (can. 869 §1). The deacon too receives a character ([5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)).

**What it is, on Aquinas's account** (ST III q.63): deputation to worship needs power, active to give and passive to receive, so the character is a spiritual power (a.2), a participation in Christ's priesthood (a.3), in the soul's powers (a.4), indelible because Christ's priesthood is eternal (a.5), imprinted by these three only, the Eucharist being the end of the others (a.6). Warrant: God "hath sealed us" (2 Cor 1:22, DR; *sphragizō*, to set a seal). The syllabus tag *character indelebilis* is not Trent's wording.

**Level:** existence, indelibility and non-repetition, **rung 1**; LG 11 and CCC 1121, **at least rung 3**; *what* the character is, explanation, **rung 5**; the canons, law.

**Does not assert:** that Trent defined Aquinas's account (overstates); that the character is "a scholastic theory" (understates).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)

### Sacramental grace

Each sacrament gives a grace proper to it.

CCC 1129: the Spirit's grace given by Christ and proper to each sacrament. Aquinas: over and above grace and the virtues, "a certain Divine assistance in obtaining the end of the sacrament" (ST III q.62 a.2). Whether it is a new habit, a vigour within sanctifying grace or a claim to actual graces divided the schools (the lesson names none). The grace of the married state is [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)'s; grace itself is [`creation-grace-last-things`](../creation-grace-last-things/syllabus.md) Module 4.

**Level:** **at least rung 3**; its nature, **rung 5**.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md)

### Trent Session VII

3 March 1547: the canons on the sacraments in general, on baptism and on confirmation. The canons define; there are no doctrinal chapters, only a proem grounding them in "the holy Scriptures ... the apostolic traditions, and ... the consent of other councils and of the Fathers" (Waterworth, 1848).

| Canons on the sacraments in general | Anathema on whoever says |
|---|---|
| 1 | the sacraments of the New Law were not all instituted by Christ, or are more or fewer than seven, or any is not "truly and properly a sacrament" ([the number seven](#the-number-seven)) |
| 2 | they differ from the Old Law's only in ceremonies and outward rites |
| 4 | they are not necessary but superfluous, and men obtain grace "without them, or without the desire thereof", through faith alone ([baptism of desire](#baptism-of-desire)) |
| 5 | they were instituted only to nourish faith |
| 6 | they do not contain the grace they signify, nor confer it on those who place no obstacle |
| 7 | grace is not given always and to all, as far as God's part goes |
| 8 | grace is not conferred *ex opere operato* (faith alone suffices) |
| 9 | baptism, confirmation and order imprint no character |
| 10 | all Christians have power over all the sacraments |
| 11 | the intention at least of doing what the Church does is not required |
| 12 | a minister in mortal sin does not confect or confer |
| 13 | the received and approved rites may be despised, omitted or changed "by every pastor" (Latin *per quemcumque ecclesiarum pastorem*; [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)'s gloss, "by any pastor whatever") |

| Canons on baptism | |
|---|---|
| 2 | true and natural water necessary |
| 4 | baptism by heretics in the Trinity's name, with the Church's intention, true baptism |
| 5 | baptism necessary for salvation |
| 12, 13, 14 | infants not to be deferred; counted among the faithful, baptized in the faith of the Church, not rebaptized; no later ratification at adulthood ([infant baptism](#infant-baptism)) |

| Canons on confirmation | |
|---|---|
| 1 | a true sacrament, not an idle ceremony or an adolescents' catechism |
| 2 | the chrism has power |
| 3 | the bishop alone is the ordinary minister |

**Level:** the canons, **rung 1**, exactly as worded. Can. 4 puts "without the desire thereof" **inside** the condemned proposition, so it does not define desire's sufficiency. Can. 13 binds for what its words reach; the Church's practice since Pius V reads it as not reaching the supreme authority. DH numbers: the lessons give DH 1601 (can. 1), 1611 (can. 11), 1612 (can. 12), 1617 (baptism can. 4) only.

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md); individual canons in [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md), [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### The number seven

No one counted seven before the twelfth century, and the Church holds that the seven were always there: the rites are old, the class is new.

**History.** Augustine never counted: the new covenant's sacraments are "in number very few ... and in significance most excellent, as baptism solemnized in the name of the Trinity, the communion of His body and blood, and such other things as are prescribed in the canonical Scriptures" (Letter 54.1, NPNF); fewer than the old (*Reply to Faustus* XIX.13). Pseudo-Dionysius, *On the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy* (around 500): six rites (illumination, synaxis, consecration of the myron, priestly consecrations, monastic consecration, rites over the dead). Hugh of St Victor (*On the Sacraments of the Christian Faith* I.9.2, 1130s) defined a sacrament as a material element that represents a grace by likeness, signifies it by institution and contains it by sanctification, yet called the Old Law's tithes and oblations sacraments (as Aquinas reports, ST III q.65 a.1 obj. 7). Peter Lombard made the cut: a sacrament properly so called "bears its image and exists as its cause" (*Sentences* IV d.1 c.4, lesson's translation), then lists seven (IV d.2 c.1, 1150s); earlier lists existed, his textbook made this one everyone's. A profession proposed by Clement IV to Michael VIII (1267) and offered in the emperor's name at Lyons II (1274): the Roman Church "holds and teaches" seven. Florence's [Decree for the Armenians](#decree-for-the-armenians) (1439). Trent defines.

**The definition.** Trent VII can. 1 (3 March 1547): anathema on whoever says the sacraments of the New Law "were not all instituted by Jesus Christ, our Lord; or, that they are more, or less, than seven", or that any of the seven is not "truly and properly a sacrament".

**Said plainly** ([1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)): the Catechism calls the recognition gradual, like that of the canon of Scripture (CCC 1117). From the later twelfth century the count was taught across the Latin Church, so Florence and Trent stated what the ordinary magisterium already taught: held infallibly before any council said so ([`fundamental-theology` 4.2](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-02-infallibility-and-its-conditions.md)), before the councils, not before Lombard.

**Why seven.** Aquinas's analogy with bodily life (ST III q.65 a.1): birth, growth, food, healing, convalescence, government, propagation; Florence uses it (five for each person's perfection, two for the Church's government and increase); CCC 1210-1211 adopts it and says the order is "not the only one possible". Fittingness, **no level**.

**Level:** the number, that each is truly and properly a sacrament, and Christ's authorship, **rung 1** ([`fundamental-theology` 5.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-01-vincent-of-lerins.md)); Michael VIII's profession, evidence of the ordinary teaching, **defining nothing**; Lombard, Hugh, the analogy, **no magisterial level**.

**Other traditions:** [Lutheran confessions on the sacraments](#lutheran-confessions-on-the-sacraments), [Anglican Articles on the sacraments](#anglican-articles-on-the-sacraments), [Orthodox on the number of mysteries](#orthodox-on-the-number-of-mysteries).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)

### Institution by Christ

All seven are from Christ: the author is defined, the manner is not.

Trent VII can. 1, rung 1, excludes any sacrament being a human or ecclesiastical invention; Trent uses those terms of marriage (XXIV can. 1: not "invented by men in the Church"). It does not say when, where or how. Aquinas: the essentials are from Christ, "not all handed down by the Scriptures" (ST III q.64 a.2 ad 1). Medieval spread: Lombard has anointing "read to have been instituted by the apostles" (IV d.23 c.3); Aquinas reports three opinions on confirmation, instituted by a later council, by the apostles, or by Christ "not by bestowing, but by promising it" (q.72 a.1 ad 1, citing John 16:7); Trent on extreme unction: instituted by Christ, "insinuated indeed in Mark", promulgated by James (Session XIV ch. 1). For the Eucharist the New Testament narrates the act: instituted "at the last supper" (XIII ch. 1; XXII ch. 1).

**The manner, freely disputed:** immediate or mediate; *in specie* (Christ fixed each sign) or *in genere* (Christ fixed the grace and a sign of a given kind, leaving the sign to the Church). Karl Rahner (*Kirche und Sakramente*, 1960): Christ instituted the Church's basic acts at decisive moments of a life in instituting her as the fundamental sacrament. Porrection is the test case ([porrection of the instruments](#porrection-of-the-instruments)).

**Level:** authorship of all seven, **rung 1**; institution "at the last supper" (XIII ch. 1; XXII ch. 1), expository chapters, **at least rung 3**; the manner, **rung 5**. Denying Christ's authorship of any one of the seven is no rung-5 opinion: it contradicts rung 1.

**Does not assert:** a Gospel scene for each sacrament.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)

### Decree for the Armenians

Florence's catechism inside a decree of union: an instruction on the seven, not a definition of each rite.

Council of Florence, bull *Exsultate Deo* (Denzinger spells *Exultate*), 22 November 1439, decree of union with the Armenians; its section on the sacraments is given "in this very brief formula, for the easier teaching of the Armenians, present and future" (lesson's translation). Contents: seven sacraments of the New Law, differing from the Old ([sacraments of the Old Law](#sacraments-of-the-old-law)); five for each person's perfection, two for the Church's government and increase; the general clause (things as matter, words as form, minister with the intention of doing what the Church does: [matter and form](#matter-and-form)); character in three; then each sacrament:

| Sacrament | Florence's instruction |
|---|---|
| Baptism | true natural water; "I baptize thee ..." or the Greek *Baptizatur talis servus Christi* (also *Baptizatur manibus meis talis*), valid if the minister's act is expressed with the Trinity's invocation; the "door of the spiritual life" (*vitae spiritualis ianua*); minister the priest, in necessity anyone, "even a pagan and a heretic"; remits all original and actual guilt and all punishment |
| Confirmation | chrism of oil and balsam blessed by a bishop; "*Signo te signo crucis, et confirmo te chrismate salutis, in nomine Patris* ..."; the bishop the ordinary minister, a simple priest by dispensation of the Apostolic See; given "in place of that imposition of hands" |
| Eucharist | wheat bread and grape wine, a little water mixed; the form the Saviour's words |
| Penance | the penitent's acts as quasi-matter; the priest's "I absolve thee" |
| Extreme unction | olive oil blessed by a bishop; only to a sick person "whose death is feared"; eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, hands, feet, loins anointed; minister the priest; healing of the mind and, as expedient, the body |
| Order | for the priesthood, the handing over of the chalice with wine and the paten with bread ([porrection](#porrection-of-the-instruments)); minister the bishop |
| Matrimony | sign of the union of Christ and the Church; efficient cause "regularly" mutual consent expressed by words of the present; three goods (offspring, fidelity, indivisibility); separation for fornication allowed, another marriage not |

**Level** (assigned by [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)): **defines nothing**. Its doctrinal statements are conciliar teaching, **at least rung 3**, with the weight of what they restate (the seven: rung 1 via Trent; the Trinitarian form: at least rung 3). Its matter-and-form determinations are a **practical instruction, changeable**: Pius XII read it so for orders in 1947. Calling them definitions overstates.

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md); used in [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

## Christian initiation

Module 2: baptism's rite and effects, how it is necessary, what stands in for it, and confirmation, the sacrament the West split off from it.

### Form of baptism

A valid form states the act and names Father, Son and Spirit; Latin and Greek wordings both do.

Latin: *Ego te baptizo in nomine Patris et Filii et Spiritus Sancti*. Byzantine, passive: "The servant of God, N., is baptized, in the name of the Father, Amen. And of the Son, Amen. And of the Holy Spirit, Amen" (Hapgood's *Service Book*, 1906; CCC 1240). Indicative passive, not deprecative. Florence's [Decree for the Armenians](#decree-for-the-armenians) (22 November 1439) accepts the passive form and gives the reason (lesson's translation): "since the principal cause from which baptism has its power is the Holy Trinity, and the instrumental cause is the minister who outwardly confers the sacrament, if the act exercised by the minister himself is expressed, with the invocation of the Holy Trinity, the sacrament is effected." Florence's Latin of the Greek form reads *servus Christi*. The command and the one name for three: Matt 28:19 ([`trinity-and-god` 6.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/06-02-the-trinity-in-the-economy-liturgy-and-prayer.md)).

**"In the name of Jesus" (Acts 2:38).** Nicholas I to the Bulgarians (866) ruled that those baptized "in the name of the Holy Trinity or only in the name of Christ, as we read in the Acts of the Apostles" are not to be rebaptized (lesson's translation): an *obiter* statement defining nothing, Newman's example ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 4.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/04-04-infallibility-in-exercise-and-the-ordinary-universal-magisterium.md)). Pelagius II, as Aquinas quotes him, ordered such people rebaptized. Aquinas (ST III q.66 a.6) requires the Trinity "expressed in sensible words" and explains the apostles' practice as a special dispensation (a school's explanation). The Didache prescribes the threefold name (ch. 7) and calls the baptized those baptized "into the name of the Lord" (ch. 9), so the phrase may say whose baptism it was rather than quote its words. The Church now requires the threefold invocation (Florence; CCC 1240).

**Failed forms:** "we baptize you" (CDF, 24 June 2020) and "Creator, Redeemer, Sanctifier" (CDF, 1 February 2008): invalid, to be given again absolutely ([valid, licit, fruitful](#valid-licit-fruitful)).

**Level:** that the form must state the act and name the three persons, and that the passive form is valid, **at least rung 3** (Florence; CCC 1240): no canon fixes the wording (VII, Canons on Baptism can. 4 defines that the Trinitarian form suffices, not that it is necessary). What the apostles said is a historical question the teaching does not settle.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md); the form principle behind the failed cases is [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)'s

### Minister of baptism

Ordinarily the clergy; in necessity anyone with the form and the intention, even the unbaptized.

The ordinary ministers are bishop, presbyter and deacon (can. 861 §1). Lateran IV's profession *Firmiter* (1215): baptism duly conferred "by anyone" (*a quocunque*) in the Church's form profits infants and adults for salvation. Florence spells out "anyone" (lesson's translation): "In necessity not only a priest or deacon but also a layman or a woman, indeed even a pagan and a heretic, can baptize, provided he keeps the Church's form and intends to do what the Church does"; CCC 1256 repeats it. Can. 861 §2 governs **liceity**: in necessity anyone with the right intention baptizes lawfully, as does a catechist designated by the local ordinary when the clergy are absent or impeded. Trent keeps it an exception: not all Christians have power over all sacraments (VII can. 10). Christ baptizes, so the minister supplies only the act and the intention.

Tertullian, *On Baptism* 17 (about 200; Thelwall, ANF 3), already separates the two questions: "Of giving it, the chief priest (who is the bishop) has the right: in the next place, the presbyters and deacons, yet not without the bishop's authority ... Beside these, even laymen have the right; for what is equally received can be equally given." Two limits: the chapter goes on to deny women the right (the later Church did not follow him), and his ground (the giver has received) is narrower than the later Church's, which extends the power to the unbaptized.

**Level:** baptism by anyone in the Church's form avails, **rung 1** (*Firmiter*); that "anyone" includes the unbaptized, **at least rung 3** (Florence, CCC 1256); can. 861, law.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md); [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)

### Effects of baptism

Baptism takes away all that is sin, gives the life sin blocked, marks the person for good and makes him a member; it leaves concupiscence, suffering and death.

| Effect | Text | Level |
|---|---|---|
| Original guilt remitted; "the whole of that which has the true and proper nature of sin" taken away, not "only rased, or not imputed" | Trent V can. 5 (17 June 1546) | rung 1 |
| All personal sins and all punishment remitted, no satisfaction imposed; "a full and entire remission of all sins" | Florence (1439); Trent XIV ch. 2 | at least rung 3 |
| Justifying grace inheres in the soul | Trent VI can. 11 | rung 1 |
| Faith, hope and charity "infused at once"; baptism justification's instrumental cause | Trent VI ch. 7; CCC 1266 adds the moral virtues and the gifts | at least rung 3 |
| Infants receive grace and virtues, not remission alone | Vienne (1312) chose it only "as more probable" (*tanquam probabiliorem*); Roman Catechism and CCC 1266 now teach it unqualified | at least rung 3 (a level that moved) |
| The [character](#sacramental-character); never repeated | Trent VII can. 9; can. 845 §1 | rung 1 |
| Incorporation into Christ and the Church | LG 11; can. 204 §1 | at least rung 3 |
| A sacramental bond of unity among all the validly reborn, only a beginning, ordered to full faith and eucharistic communion | *Unitatis Redintegratio* 22 | at least rung 3 |

**What stays.** Concupiscence, the "incentive (to sin)": the Church has never understood it as "truly and properly sin" in the reborn; it is called sin "because it is of sin, and inclines to sin" (V can. 5, rung 1). Suffering and death (CCC 1264). Aquinas explains why (ST III q.69 a.3): explanation, no level. Original sin itself is [`creation-grace-last-things`](../creation-grace-last-things/syllabus.md) 3.3's. Confession is for sins after baptism; penance is "a laborious kind of baptism", as Trent reports the Fathers (XIV ch. 2).

The New Testament minimum ([2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md)): Matt 28:19 (command and formula), John 3:5 (water and the Spirit, one birth, *ex hydatos kai pneumatos*), Rom 6:3-4 ("buried together with him by baptism into death"), Titus 3:5 (*loutron palingenesias*, "a bath of rebirth", lesson's gloss; DR "laver of regeneration"), Acts 2:38, 1 Pet 3:21 (*eperōtēma*, "appeal" or "pledge", lesson's gloss; DR "examination").

**Does not assert:** that concupiscence is original sin left over (defined the opposite).

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md)

### Catechumenate

The staged formation of adults before baptism, shrunk by infant baptism into rites over infants and restored after Vatican II.

Alcuin's plea of about 796 that preaching come before baptism ([`church-history` 3.1](../church-history/lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)) asked for what the early catechumenate gave. *Sacrosanctum Concilium* 64 ordered it restored in distinct steps; the *Ordo initiationis christianae adultorum* (1972) did so. Adults receive baptism, confirmation and the Eucharist together (can. 866); can. 856 recommends Sunday or, where possible, the Easter Vigil for any baptism. An adult must ask, be instructed and tested, and be urged to sorrow for sin (can. 865 §1; §2 in danger of death). Justin's sequence of instruction, fasting and prayer before the washing (*First Apology* 61) is read in [`patristics` 1.4](../patristics/lessons/01-04-justin-martyr.md). Catechumens are joined to the Church by explicit desire (LG 14; [baptism of desire](#baptism-of-desire)); the salt given them is Augustine's "sacrament of the catechumen".

**Level:** SC 64, conciliar norm; the rite and canons, law, no rung.

*Lessons:* [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md)

### Necessity of means and of precept

Baptism is necessary as a means, not only as a command, but as a means by Christ's institution, so God who bound salvation to it is not bound by it.

What is commanded is necessary by **precept**: ignorance or impossibility excuses. What the end cannot be reached without is necessary as a **means**: ignorance does not excuse ("no one crosses without the bridge, whatever he knows about bridges"). Baptism is a means for all, infants included, and commanded to adults besides. A means by divine institution, not intrinsic, so its effect can be given by [blood](#baptism-of-blood) or [desire](#baptism-of-desire) (the 1949 Holy Office letter's vocabulary; [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-01-outside-the-church-there-is-no-salvation.md)). CCC 1257: God has bound salvation to baptism, but he himself is not bound by his sacraments. Can. 849: necessary *in re vel saltem in voto*, in fact or at least in desire.

Two questions get run together: is baptism necessary (defined: yes), and is everyone who dies unbaptized lost (the tradition says not).

**Level:** necessity itself **rung 1** (Trent VII, Canons on Baptism can. 5, against calling it "free, that is, not necessary unto salvation"; can. 2 holds John 3:5's water to true water); the means/precept classification is the manuals', **no level**; can. 849, law.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)

### Infant baptism

Infants are baptized, truly for the remission of sins, in the faith of the Church.

Trent V can. 4 (17 June 1546) takes over almost verbatim Carthage (418) can. 2 (African Code 110): infants, even of baptized parents, are baptized truly to remit what they contract from Adam. Trent VII, Canons on Baptism: can. 12 (baptism not to be deferred to Christ's age or the deathbed), can. 13 (the baptized infant counted among the faithful, not rebaptized; better not unbaptized than baptized "in the faith alone of the Church"), can. 14 (not asked at adulthood to ratify). Whose faith: the Church's; the child is offered by "the whole company of the saints and the faithful" (Augustine, Letter 98, as ST III q.68 a.9 ad 2 adopts it; ad 1, the child in the Church's womb).

**The evidence, graded:** household baptisms (Acts 16:15, 33; 1 Cor 1:16) neither name nor exclude infants; Irenaeus (c. 180) counts infants among those "born again to God" (*Against Heresies* II.22.4), possibly baptism; Tertullian (c. 200) urges delaying little children's baptism (*On Baptism* 18), so the practice existed; Cyprian's council to Fidus (early 250s; ANF Ep. 58 = Oxford 64) disputed only whether to wait for the eighth day. Trent's "tradition of the apostles" is the Church's reading of her tradition, not a first-century text; CCC 1252 claims explicit testimony from the second century on.

**Liceity, not validity:** can. 868 §1 2°, as amended by *De concordia inter Codices* (31 May 2016): there must be a founded hope of a Catholic upbringing; if altogether lacking, baptism is deferred with an explanation to the parents. §2: in danger of death licit even against the parents' will. §3 (2016): infants of non-Catholic Christians, at their parents' request, when their own minister is physically or morally inaccessible. The hope is a condition of liceity only: an infant baptized without it is validly baptized, and fruitfully, since an infant places no obstacle.

**Level:** cans. 4 (V), 12-14 (VII), **rung 1**; Augustine's and Aquinas's accounts, no level; can. 868, law.

**Other side:** [Anabaptist and Baptist on infant baptism](#anabaptist-and-baptist-on-infant-baptism), stated, not answered.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)

### Baptism of blood

Death for Christ before baptism gives baptism's grace without the sacrament or its character.

Tertullian calls blood a second baptism that stands in for the font (*On Baptism* 16); Cyprian, the catechumen baptized in his blood (Ep. 72.22; [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-01-outside-the-church-there-is-no-salvation.md)). Aquinas, ST III q.66 a.11 (three baptisms: water, blood, the Spirit) and a.12: blood is the most excellent, since it imitates Christ's act and contains charity, without which shed blood is no baptism (ad 2); it is not a sacrament and imprints no character (a.11 ad 2, a.12 ad 1). The liturgy venerates the unbaptized Holy Innocents as martyrs (ITC 2007 §5; Aquinas's articles do not mention them). CCC 1258. Infants can be martyrs too.

**Level:** **at least rung 3** (LG 14 and CCC 1258, as E&M 5.1 grades it); Aquinas's ranking, explanation.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)

### Baptism of desire

Where the font is impossible, the will that would go to it, formed by charity, receives what it gives: explicitly in the catechumen, implicitly in the invincibly ignorant.

**Explicit:** catechumens joined by explicit intention (LG 14; CCC 1259); the Roman Catechism (1566) credits an adult kept from the font with his "intention and determination to receive Baptism", with repentance. **Implicit:** in the invincibly ignorant, informed by supernatural faith and perfect charity (the 1949 Holy Office letter, rung 3 light; CCC 1260; [`apologetics-foundations` 8.2](../apologetics-foundations/lessons/08-02-salvation-outside-the-visible-church.md)). Aquinas, ST III q.68 a.2: desire as the outcome of faith working by charity (with Ambrose on Valentinian).

**Trent, read closely.** Session VI ch. 4 (13 January 1547): "And this translation, since the promulgation of the Gospel, cannot be effected, without the laver of regeneration, or the desire thereof" (*aut eius voto*). Session VII, Canons on the Sacraments in General, can. 4 (3 March 1547): anathema on whoever says the sacraments are "not necessary unto salvation, but superfluous; and that, without them, or without the desire thereof, men obtain of God, through faith alone, the grace of justification".

**[2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)'s verdict:** the chapter states a **necessary** condition (no justification without the laver or its desire), not that desire suffices or what it must contain; it is a chapter, though Session VI's chapters are fenced ("which whoso receiveth not faithfully and firmly can not be justified"). The canon puts desire **inside** the condemned proposition: the anathema falls on "superfluous" and "faith alone", and one who denied that desire ever suffices would escape it. So Trent **presupposes and teaches** that desire can stand in for the laver and **does not define** it.

**Level:** **at least rung 3** (LG 14; CCC 1258-1259), Trent's chapter its weightiest witness, **not rung 1**; implicit desire, **rung 3, light** (1949 letter); whether this constant teaching is definitive, no act has said. An infant cannot make the adult's desire (ITC 2007 §29); a desire supplied by parents or the Church, **rung 5**.

**Does not assert:** a modern loophole (it is Trent's and Aquinas's), or a definition.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md); shared with the ecclesiology card's entry

### Limbo

The medieval theory that infants dying unbaptized lack the vision of God without suffering: never defined, never abolished.

Augustine taught such infants condemned, if to the "mildest" punishment; the schools softened this to limbo (ITC 2007 §§18, 22). Pius VI's *Auctorem Fidei* (1794) defended the schools' freedom to teach it, not its truth (ITC §38). Until after Vatican II the Latin Church gave such infants no funeral rite and buried them in unconsecrated ground (ITC §100). The ITC, *The Hope of Salvation for Infants Who Die Without Being Baptised* (approved *in forma specifica* by the Commission's own members; publication approved by Benedict XVI in audience 19 January 2007; dated 19 April 2007): limbo a possible theological hypothesis; "reasons for prayerful hope, rather than grounds for sure knowledge" (§102); the Church has never ruled out a desire supplied by parents or Church (§94). CCC 1261 entrusts them to God's mercy, as the funeral rites do, and hopes. Can. 1183 §2: a Church funeral by permission where the parents intended baptism.

**Level:** **rung 5, freely disputed**, before and after 2007; the ITC study is advisory, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not assert:** that limbo was dogma, or that it was abolished in 2007.

**Owned by:** [`creation-grace-last-things`](../creation-grace-last-things/syllabus.md) 7.3.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)

### Conditional baptism

Baptism is never repeated; a real doubt is met by baptizing conditionally, and baptism in other communities is not doubted without a serious reason.

Can. 869 §1: if after serious investigation a doubt remains whether or how validly someone was baptized, baptism is conferred conditionally; §3 privately and with explanation (Ecumenical Directory 1993 §99d). §2: those baptized in non-Catholic communities are not baptized even conditionally unless examining the matter, form and intention of minister and adult recipient gives a serious reason to doubt (Directory §99c). Baptism given even by heretics in the Trinitarian form with the Church's intention is true baptism (Trent VII, Canons on Baptism can. 4, rung 1). A minister's insufficient faith about baptism never of itself invalidates (Directory §95b). Eastern baptism and chrismation are beyond doubt (§99a); converts from Reformation communities are confirmed before communion (§101).

**The limit case.** CDF response of 5 June 2001, approved by John Paul II: on baptism in the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints, "Negative", so such converts are baptized absolutely. The response gives no reasons; Luis Ladaria's signed commentary (*L'Osservatore Romano*, English ed., 1 August 2001) does: water and words are right, but the names invoke three gods, not the Trinity, so the form is no true invocation and the minister cannot intend what the Church does. *Gestis verbisque* note 39 treats it as a failure of intention. The rule ([2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md)): error about what baptism does leaves it valid; error about whose name it is given in does not.
