**Level:** can. 4 (VII, Baptism), **rung 1**; the 2001 response binds practice, defining nothing; Ladaria's reasons, **no magisterial level**; can. 869 and the Directory, law and norms.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md); [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)

### Confirmation

A true sacrament distinct from baptism, imprinting a character: baptism's grace deepened and turned outward.

Trent VII, Canons on Confirmation can. 1: anathema on calling it "an idle ceremony", or "nothing more than a kind of catechism" in which those near adolescence gave an account of their faith; it imprints a character (VII can. 9). **Latin matter and form** (Paul VI, *Divinae Consortium Naturae*, 15 August 1971): anointing with [chrism](#chrism) on the forehead, done by the laying on of the hand, with *Accipe signaculum doni Spiritus Sancti* ("Receive the seal of the gift of the Holy Spirit", lesson's translation; current English "Be sealed with the Gift of the Holy Spirit"). The earlier extension of hands over the group is not essential, though highly esteemed. Paul VI took the Byzantine formula ("The seal of the gift of the Holy Spirit", Hapgood 1906), tracing it to the fourth and fifth centuries, "almost word for word". **East:** chrismation by the baptizing priest with bishop-consecrated *myron*, then communion, in one rite (CCC 1312, 1318; CCEO cann. 695 §1, 697).

**Effects:** the confirmed are more perfectly bound to the Church, enriched with a special strength of the Spirit, and more strictly obliged to spread and defend the faith as true witnesses (LG 11); an increase and deepening of baptismal grace (CCC 1302-1303). Reception at the proper time obligatory (can. 890). Baptismal grace needs no ratification (CCC 1308); the renewal of promises belongs to the Latin rite (SC 71).

**Scripture:** Acts 8:14-17 and 19:5-6: baptism first, then a second act by an apostle, prayer, hands, the Spirit given; no oil; not uniform (Acts 10:44-48 the Spirit before baptism; Acts 2:38 no hands); Heb 6:2 "imposition of hands" unspecified. Paul VI calls the apostolic hand-laying the sacrament's beginning according to Catholic tradition. Cyril of Jerusalem: the post-baptismal anointing is the antitype of Christ's own, "and this is the Holy Ghost" (*Mystagogical Catecheses* 3.1).

**Why the West separated it** (CCC 1290): two goods, the unity of the rite and the bishop's own hand, could not both be kept once infant baptism was the norm and dioceses grew; the East kept the unity, the West the hand. Innocent I to Decentius of Gubbio (19 March 416; lesson's translation): "As for signing infants, it is plain that this may lawfully be done by no one but the bishop"; presbyters anoint with the bishop's chrism but do not sign the forehead. That double anointing survives (CCC 1291). Aquinas: the clerk writes, the employer signs (ST III q.72 a.11).

**Said plainly:** the West has been explaining the separation ever since. Its classic explanation came from a fifth-century Gallic Pentecost homily usually ascribed to Faustus of Riez (disputed); the ninth-century Pseudo-Isidorean forgers put it in the mouth of Pope Melchiades (311-314); through Ivo and Gratian it reached Lombard and Aquinas, who thought they quoted a fourth-century pope (L. A. van Buchem, 1967; Eugene Finnegan, *CTSA Proceedings* 62, 2007). "Strengthened for the combat" has that pedigree (ST III q.72 a.1).

**Level:** true sacrament, character, bishop the *ordinary* minister, **rung 1**; LG 11 on the effects, **rung 3**; CCC 1302-1305 carry their sources' weight; *Divinae Consortium Naturae*, binding law fixing the essential rite, **no doctrinal rung**; Florence's matter and form, an instruction ([Decree for the Armenians](#decree-for-the-armenians)); the soldier-and-maturity explanation, a school's, **no level**.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md)

### Chrism

Perfumed oil a bishop consecrates; its consecration by a bishop is required even when a presbyter confirms.

Can. 880 §2: chrism consecrated by a bishop, even when a presbyter confers the sacrament; in the East *myron* consecrated by a bishop or patriarch (CCEO can. 693), so "the bishop is present through the oil". Florence's instruction: chrism of oil and balsam blessed by a bishop. Innocent I (416): presbyters anoint the baptized with the bishop's chrism but do not sign the forehead. Consecrated at the Chrism Mass ([2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md) Ex 1). Basil counts the blessing of the chrism among the unwritten traditions ([`patristics` 3.2](../patristics/lessons/03-02-basil-the-great.md)). Distinct from the oil of the sick, which a bishop or, in necessity, any presbyter within the rite blesses ([anointing of the sick](#anointing-of-the-sick)).

**Level:** law, no rung; Florence, instruction.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md)

### Minister of confirmation

The bishop by right, the Latin presbyter by the Church's grant, the Eastern presbyter always.

Trent VII, Canons on Confirmation can. 3: anathema on saying the ordinary minister "is not the bishop alone, but any simple priest soever"; XXIII can. 7 denies that bishops' power of confirming is common to them and priests. LG 26: bishops are its "original" ministers. A Latin presbyter confers it validly only with a faculty, from universal law or by grant (can. 882; can. 887); the law gives it to the presbyter who by office or mandate baptizes someone past infancy or receives a baptized Christian into full communion (can. 883 2°) and to any presbyter for those in danger of death (can. 883 3°); the Church supplies it in common error or positive and probable doubt (can. 144 §2). Every Eastern presbyter chrismates validly faithful of any church *sui iuris*, Latin included (CCEO can. 696 §1); Eastern faithful receive validly from Latin presbyters per their faculties (§2). Florence had allowed a simple priest by papal dispensation. Benedict XIV called the controversy over a presbyter's power to confirm "difficult and very tangled" (*De Synodo dioecesana* VII.8.7, lesson's translation).

**Level:** the bishop the *ordinary* minister, **rung 1** ("ordinary" leaves an extraordinary minister open); a presbyter with the faculty, or any Eastern presbyter, confers validly, **at least rung 3** (both Codes; CCC 1312-1313); faculties, law; how a faculty can decide validity, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not assert:** that only a bishop confirms validly (overstates Trent), or that any priest can (a Latin presbyter without the faculty confirms invalidly).

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md); faculties in general: [faculties](#faculties)

### Order of the sacraments of initiation

East: baptism, chrismation, Eucharist in one rite. Latin: for infants, baptism, first communion about seven, confirmation later; for adults all three at once. The order is practice, not dogma.

Early centuries: water, anointing, a hand and the Eucharist in one night. *Quam Singulari* (S.C. of the Discipline of the Sacraments, approved by Pius X, 8 August 1910) lowered the age of first communion to about seven; it never mentions confirmation, so the inversion was an effect wherever confirmation stayed late ([3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)). Can. 891: about the age of discretion, unless the conference fixes another age, or danger of death or grave cause intervenes. The ancient order survives for adults (CCC 1298), in restored-order dioceses, and for the dying. Benedict XVI: the variations are "not properly of the dogmatic order, but pastoral" (*Sacramentum Caritatis* 18, 2007). Aquinas: the body's age does not decide the soul's, and the dying should be confirmed (ST III q.72 a.8 ad 2, ad 4).

**Level:** age, law (can. 891); the order, **freely disputed** as pastoral practice.

*Lessons:* [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md); the first-communion side is [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)'s

## The Eucharist

Module 3: the texts, the controversies that forced precision, what Trent's Sessions XIII, XXI and XXII define, and the discipline around communion, reservation and adoration. The rule throughout: the canon defines *that*; *how* is theology.

### Institution narratives

The Supper is told four times, in two families; the earliest written is Paul's.

| Family | Texts | Cup-word | "Do this" |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mark-Matthew | Mark 14:22-24; Matt 26:26-28 | "This is my blood of the new testament", echoing Sinai's "blood of the covenant" (Exod 24:8); Matthew adds "unto remission of sins" | absent |
| Luke-Paul | Luke 22:19-20; 1 Cor 11:23-25 | "the new testament in my blood", Jeremiah's new covenant (Jer 31:31) | once in Luke, twice in Paul |

Paul (mid-50s) is the earliest written; John has no institution words at the supper (the dating dispute is [`new-testament` 2.4](../new-testament/lessons/02-04-john-and-the-synoptics.md)'s). The frame is paschal either way: "Christ our pasch is sacrificed" (1 Cor 5:7). 1 Cor 11:23-29 (DR), read for its shape: **received**, not composed; words that **identify** and a command that is a **rite** ("This do", twice; eating and drinking "shew the death of the Lord, until he come"); a **consequence**: the unworthy eater is "guilty of the body and of the blood of the Lord". **Open:** the oldest Greek of 11:29 reads only "not discerning the body"; the Douay's "unworthily" and "of the Lord" follow the Vulgate, and exegetes divide over whether the body to be discerned is the sacrament, the community or both.

**What Trent did with them:** instituted "at the last supper" (XIII ch. 1; XXII ch. 1); the words carry "that proper and most manifest meaning in which they were understood by the Fathers", and it is a crime to wrest them "to fictitious and imaginary tropes" (XIII ch. 1); by "Do this" Christ instituted the apostles priests and ordained that they offer (XXII can. 2).

**Level:** XXII can. 2, **rung 1** (an anathema binding the sense of one verse); XIII ch. 1, an expository chapter, **at least rung 3**; the sense of "the body" in 1 Cor 11:29, **open**.

**What the earliest witness does not establish:** a fixed text (the families differ; Didache 9-10 has no narrative; Justin's president prays "according to his ability"); a theory of how; the later discipline; Trent's doctrine of the propitiatory sacrifice; any later rite. "The texts are realist in their language and silent about mechanism" ([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md)): reading Trent's vocabulary into them is one anachronism, a bare symbol the other.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md)

### Anamnesis

"Do this for a commemoration of me": a memorial in the Passover sense, a rite in which a past saving act reaches those who keep it.

*Anamnēsis* (Luke 22:19; 1 Cor 11:24-25): remembrance, memorial (lesson's gloss), read with the Passover's *zikkaron*, "for a memorial" (Exod 12:14), where the father tells his son, "This is what the Lord did to me when I came forth out of Egypt" (Exod 13:8, DR): the generation keeping the rite says *me* (the Mishnah's later rule: [`old-testament` 2.5](../old-testament/lessons/02-05-exodus-and-sinai.md)). Trent: Christ instituted the sacrament "making a remembrance of his wonderful works" (XIII ch. 2), and having kept the old Passover instituted the new (XXII ch. 1); the bloody sacrifice is "represented", "the memory thereof" remaining to the end of the world (XXII ch. 1). The Catechism: memorial in Scripture's sense is not mere recollection; in the Eucharist Christ's Passover is made present, as the Exodus is for Israel (CCC 1362-1364, paraphrased). *Transiturus de mundo* (1264) calls the sacrament a *memoriale* again and again and says Christ is present "in his own true substance": memorial and substance are not rivals. In the anaphora, the anamnesis is the prayer that remembers and offers (the Roman *Unde et memores*: [Roman Canon](#roman-canon)).

**Level:** CCC 1362-1364 and Trent's chapters, **at least rung 3**; XXII can. 2 binds the sense of "Do this", **rung 1**; the exegesis of *zikkaron*, scholarship, no level. How the sacrifice is present is [how the one sacrifice is present](#how-the-one-sacrifice-is-present).

**Does not mean:** a mental recollection.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Bread of life discourse

John 6, read eucharistically by the Church's teaching, but its exegesis left undefined.

The discourse moves from believing in the bread of life (6:35) to eating "my flesh, for the life of the world" (6:51b; DR 6:52), "except you eat" (6:53; DR 6:54), and ends with many who "walked no more with him" (6:66; DR 6:67). The Douay runs one verse ahead from 6:51b. CCC 1336, 1384 read it eucharistically; John 6:54 (DR 6:55) with Irenaeus makes the "pledge of our glory to come" (XIII ch. 2) a bodily rising. But Trent, ruling on the chapter in Session XXI (ch. 1), left it "however ... it be understood" to the differing interpretations of the Fathers and Doctors, and rested the real presence on the institution words. Zwingli's "the flesh profiteth nothing" (6:63; DR 6:64) is his ground for *est* = *significat*. The text and its three readings: [`new-testament` 2.5](../new-testament/lessons/02-05-the-theology-of-john.md).

**Level:** the eucharistic reading is the Church's use, **not a defined exegesis**; "defined" overstates.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md); [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md); Year B's five Sundays of John 6: [lectionary](#lectionary)

### Breaking of bread

Acts' name for a regular practice beside teaching and prayer; whether every instance is eucharistic is debated.

Acts 2:42: "the doctrine of the apostles and ... the communication of the breaking of bread and ... prayers" (DR). The Douay, after the Vulgate, joins two nouns the Greek sets side by side (*koinōnia*, glossed in [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 1.4](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/01-04-people-of-god-communion-sacrament.md), and the breaking of bread). Also Acts 2:46 ("from house to house"), 20:7 (Troas, "the first day of the week"), Luke 24:35 (Emmaus). CCC 1329 lists "the Breaking of Bread" among the sacrament's names.

**Level:** catechesis using the verse, **not a definition** of it; the eucharistic reading of every instance, **debated**.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md)

### Real presence

Christ himself, body, blood, soul and divinity, truly, really and substantially contained under the species: not a picture, a reminder, or a power working through bread.

Trent XIII ch. 1: after the consecration Christ, true God and man, is "truly, really, and substantially contained under the species". Can. 1 anathematizes denying that the body and blood, with soul and divinity, "and consequently the whole Christ", are so contained, and saying he is there "only ... as in a sign, or in figure, or virtue". The usual pairing, *truly* against figure, *really* against mere sign, *substantially* against presence by power alone (as Christ acts through baptism's water), is the theologians' reading; the canon lists both triples. "Real" marks presence *par excellence* because substantial, without making Christ's other presences unreal (*Mysterium Fidei* 39; CCC 1374; the list of presences is SC 7: [liturgy](#liturgy)). Permanence: not "only during the use" (can. 4); latria (ch. 5, can. 6). Not eaten "spiritually only" (can. 8); corporeally present, though "not in the manner in which bodies are in a place" (*Mysterium Fidei* 46).

**The earliest witness** ([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md)): 1 Cor 11:27 "guilty of the body and of the blood"; Ignatius, *Smyrnaeans* 7, the docetists "confess not the Eucharist to be the flesh of our Saviour"; Justin, *First Apology* 66, "not as common bread"; Irenaeus, *Against Heresies* IV.18.5. Realist language, silent about mechanism; the Fathers are witnesses, no rung: the doctrine is dogma because Trent defined it, not because Ignatius wrote it.

**What Trent did not do:** say how the species exist once the bread's substance is gone, or how one body is on many altars; only that this is a manner of existing which, "though we can scarcely express it in words", faith holds possible to God, distinct from Christ's natural mode at the Father's right hand (ch. 1). A mystery strictly so called ([`fundamental-theology` 1.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/01-03-supernatural-revelation.md)): defining it fixes what must be said, not how it is possible.

**Level:** cans. 1, 4, 6, 8, **rung 1**; "not in his natural mode" (ch. 1) and "not as bodies are in a place" (MF 46), **at least rung 3** (taught, no canon defines it); Aquinas's account of how (q.76 a.5, related to place only through the bread's dimensions), explanation, no level.

**Does not mean:** "only spiritually" (can. 8 condemns it); that other presences are unreal.

*Lessons:* [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Paschasius and Ratramnus

Two monks of Corbie in the ninth century, one sacrament called figure and truth at once, the other "in a figure" and yet "truly": the crux is the word *veritas*.

**Paschasius Radbertus**, *On the Body and Blood of the Lord* (early 830s; revised for Charles the Bald about 844): after the consecration the sacrament is *non alia plane caro quam quae nata est de Maria et passa in cruce et resurrexit de sepulchro*, "no other flesh at all than that which was born of Mary, suffered on the cross and rose from the tomb" (lesson's translation); figure in what the senses meet, truth in what faith rightly understands. He echoes Ambrose's "body born of the Virgin" (*On the Mysteries* 9.53). **Ratramnus** (840s), answering Charles the Bald's two questions (ch. 5; Oxford translation, 1838): is the body received "in a mystery or in truth", and is it "the very same Body which was born of Mary"? A figure shows its meaning "under some sort of veil", truth is "veiled under no shadowy images" (chs. 7-8); the eye meets bread, so the body is there "in a figure"; yet in respect of "their invisible substance, that is, the power of the Word of God, they are truly the Body and Blood of Christ" (ch. 49); and between the body in the mystery and the body that suffered and rose the difference "is not small" (ch. 97). He quotes Ambrose (chs. 51-54).

**The crux:** for Paschasius truth is what the thing is, seen or unseen, so one sacrament is figure and truth at once; for Ratramnus truth is what is shown unveiled, so figure and truth exclude each other. How far apart they stood on the reality is still argued; Reformed and Catholic editors have both claimed Ratramnus (Schaff, *History of the Christian Church* IV §124). A textbook crux of definition ([`philosophical-method` 4.3](../philosophical-method/lessons/04-03-finding-the-crux.md)).

**Level:** theologians, **no magisterial level**. Ratramnus was neither a Zwinglian nor a Thomist before his time.

**Argued in:** [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md) 6.4.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)

### Berengar

The eleventh-century master of Tours who held the bread remains bread and becomes the sign of Christ's body in heaven, and the two oaths Rome made him swear.

Berengar of Tours (d. 1088), master of the school of Saint-Martin, revived Ratramnus's side: the bread stays bread in substance and becomes by consecration the sign of Christ's body, which is in heaven; how real a presence he allowed is debated. Synods condemned him from 1050 on. **1059** (Roman synod under Nicholas II; formula drafted by Cardinal Humbert, preserved by Lanfranc, *De corpore et sanguine Domini* 2, carried into Gratian's *Decretum* as *Ego Berengarius*): the body "sensibly (*sensualiter*), not only in sacrament but in truth, handled and broken by the hands of priests and ground by the teeth of the faithful" (lesson's translation). **1079** (Roman synod under Gregory VII): a new oath dropping the hands and teeth, confessing the bread and wine "substantially changed" into Christ's flesh and blood, "not only through the sign and power of the sacrament, but in the property of nature and the truth of substance". He died in communion with the Church.

**Said plainly** ([3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)): the tradition refined the 1059 formula from inside. The gloss on Gratian warned careless readers of a worse heresy than Berengar's (as Calvin reports, *Institutes* IV.17.12), and Aquinas: "the breaking and the crushing with the teeth is to be referred to the sacramental species" (ST III q.77 a.7 ad 3). So "the priest breaks the body of Christ" passes as loose speech about the host and fails as a claim about Christ's body; the whole Christ is under every separated part (XIII can. 3).

**Level:** professions a pope and his synod imposed on one man, **not definitions** for the universal Church; what they confess stands at **rung 1** only because Lateran IV and Trent defined it; the 1059 sensory predicates were **never defined** of the body; Aquinas's reading of them as said of the species, **explanation**.

**Does not license:** "the 1059 oath is dogma" or "a buried scandal": its realism was defined, its sensory predicates corrected openly.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)

### Firmiter

Lateran IV's profession of faith (1215), constitution 1: the source of two rung-1 clauses this course uses, the eucharistic change and baptism "by anyone".

**The eucharistic clause** (lesson's translation, from the Latin, old Denzinger 430): in the one Church, in which Jesus Christ is priest and sacrifice, "whose body and blood are truly contained in the sacrament of the altar under the species of bread and wine, the bread having been transubstantiated into the body and the wine into the blood by divine power". The first conciliar use of the term, as a participle. **The baptismal clause:** baptism duly conferred "by anyone" (*a quocunque*) in the Church's form profits infants and adults for salvation ([minister of baptism](#minister-of-baptism)). Its necessity clause (no salvation outside the one universal Church) is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-01-outside-the-church-there-is-no-salvation.md)'s. Lateran IV's **canon 21** (*Omnis utriusque sexus*) is a different text: law on yearly confession and Easter communion, with the seal ([penance](#penance), [seal of confession](#seal-of-confession)).

**Level:** an ecumenical council's profession of faith, **rung 1 for what it professes**: the true containment, the conversion by divine power, baptism by anyone in the Church's form. It **does not** define substance, mention accidents, or declare the word apt (Trent XIII ch. 4 and can. 2 do).

**Does not license:** "Lateran IV defined transubstantiation" (it professed the presence and the change, using the participle) or "invented the real presence" (confessed for a millennium before).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md), [2.1](lessons/02-01-baptism-the-rite-and-its-effects.md)

### Transubstantiation

The conversion of the whole substance of bread into Christ's body and of wine into his blood, only the species remaining; the word is defined as the right word, Aristotle is not.

**History** ([3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)): the term in school use by the mid-twelfth century; first in a conciliar profession at Lateran IV, [*Firmiter*](#firmiter) (1215), as a participle; declared apt by Trent XIII (1551). **Trent XIII ch. 4** (Waterworth): "by the consecration ... a conversion is made of the whole substance of the bread into the substance of the body of Christ ... which conversion is, by the holy Catholic Church, suitably and properly called Transubstantiation" (*convenienter et proprie ... est appellata*). **Can. 2:** anathema on saying the substance of bread and wine "remains conjointly with" the body and blood, or denying the conversion "of the whole substance", "the species only of the bread and wine remaining", which conversion the Church "most aptly calls" (*aptissime appellat*) transubstantiation. The council declares the conversion and endorses the Church's existing name for it.

**Neither accidental nor substantial change** (in [`metaphysics` 2.2](../metaphysics/lessons/02-02-change-act-and-potency.md)'s sense): in accidental change one thing gains new properties; in substantial change a new thing comes to be, its appearances change, and matter persists underneath. Here what the thing *is* changes wholly, how it appears not at all, and nothing of the bread persists to become the body. Aquinas: "not a formal, but a substantial conversion" which, "with a name of its own", can be called transubstantiation (ST III q.75 a.4); only infinite power converts whole being; not by local motion, so by conversion (q.75 a.2).

**Guarding the word.** Pius VI, *Auctorem fidei* (28 August 1794), prop. 29, censured the Pistoia synod's omission of the word as if the question were merely scholastic (a censure below heresy): the *how* may go unsaid, not the word guarding the *what*. *Mysterium Fidei* (1965) §§24-25: Trent's formulas are tied to no culture, stage of science or school, and may be clarified only with their meaning kept ([transignification](#transignification)).

**Level:** the change and the term, **rung 1** (XIII can. 2); "rung 1 for the change and the term, **not** for Aristotle" ([`fundamental-theology` 5.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-01-vincent-of-lerins.md)); whether the doctrine needs an Aristotelian account of substance, **freely disputed** inside the line the canons draw ([substance and accident](#substance-and-accident)).

**Does not license:** "one theory among several" (understates; the move *Auctorem fidei* censured), or "Trent defined the Thomist machinery" (overstates).

**Orthodox counterpart:** [Orthodox on metousiosis](#orthodox-on-metousiosis).

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Corpus Christi

The feast of the sacrament, instituted for the Latin Church by Urban IV in 1264.

Urban IV, bull *Transiturus de mundo* (Orvieto, August 1264; vatican.va heads it 11 August; the 1908 Catholic Encyclopedia's 8 September is wrong), on the Thursday after the octave of Pentecost. Reasons given: Holy Thursday was crowded (penitents, chrism, the washing of feet), and to confound "the faithlessness and madness of heretics". The bull calls the sacrament a *memoriale* again and again and says Christ is present "under another form indeed, but in his own true substance". Most scholars accept the attribution of its Office to Aquinas. Trent defends the feast, processions and public exposition (XIII ch. 5, can. 6). Today a solemnity on Thursday E+60 or the Sunday after ([date of Easter](#date-of-easter)).

**Level:** a bull instituting a feast is **liturgical law, no rung**; its doctrinal sentences weigh what the defining acts make them weigh.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md); its devotional descendants (processions, exposition): [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Remanence

Historians' name for Wyclif's thesis that the substance of bread and wine remains after the consecration.

John Wyclif (d. 1384), at Oxford. Constance, Session VIII (4 May 1415), condemned 45 of his articles, the first three eucharistic: the material substance of bread and wine remains; the accidents of bread do not remain without a subject; Christ is not there identically and really in his own bodily presence. The censure was **global**: Martin V's *Inter cunctas* (1418, interrogation 11) says only that some of the 45 are heretical, some erroneous, some rash, some offensive, without saying which. Remanence contradicts what *Firmiter* professed, and Trent anathematizes it by name (XIII can. 2: the substance "remains conjointly"). Luther's sacramental union keeps the bread's substance in a different sense ([Luther Zwingli Calvin on the Supper](#luther-zwingli-calvin-on-the-supper)).

**Level:** at Constance, a global censure (so "Constance declared remanence heretical" says more than Constance said); against Trent XIII can. 2, **rung 1**.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)

### Trent Session XIII

11 October 1551, the decree on the Eucharist: eight chapters that expound and eleven canons where the definition bites. It never says "accident".

| Chapter | Teaches |
|---|---|
| 1 | the real presence, "truly, really, and substantially"; institution at the Supper; the words' "proper and most manifest meaning", not "fictitious and imaginary tropes"; a manner of existing distinct from Christ's natural mode, scarcely expressible |
| 2 | the reason of institution: "making a remembrance of his wonderful works"; spiritual food; "a pledge of our glory to come"; a symbol of the one body |
| 3 | the Eucharist's excellence (the Author of sanctity present "before being used"); [concomitance](#concomitance) |
| 4 | the conversion "suitably and properly called Transubstantiation" |
| 5 | latria; the feast and processions |
| 6 | reservation and carrying to the sick |
| 7 | preparation: "let a man prove himself"; no one conscious of mortal sin approaches without sacramental confession |
| 8 | the use: sacramentally only, spiritually only, or both |

| Canon | Anathema on whoever says or denies |
|---|---|
| 1 | denies the body and blood, with soul and divinity, the whole Christ, truly, really, substantially contained; says only "as in a sign, or in figure, or virtue" |
| 2 | says the bread's substance remains conjointly; denies the conversion of the whole substance, "the species only ... remaining", "most aptly" called transubstantiation |
| 3 | denies the whole Christ under each species and every separated part |
| 4 | says the presence is only during the use, not in hosts reserved |
| 5 | says the chief fruit is remission of sins, or that other effects do not arise |
| 6 | denies latria, the feast, processions, public exposition; calls adorers idolaters |
| 7 | denies that reservation and carrying to the sick are lawful |
| 8 | says Christ is eaten spiritually only |
| 9 | denies that the faithful are bound to Easter communion "in accordance with the precept of holy Mother Church" |
| 10 | denies that the celebrating priest may communicate himself |
| 11 | says faith alone is sufficient preparation; an ordinance attached requires confession first "when a confessor may be had", under excommunication |

**Level:** the canons, **rung 1**, as worded; two are narrower than they look: can. 9 defines that the precept binds, **not its terms** (can. 920 is law); can. 11's anathema strikes "faith alone", while confession first is the attached ordinance, constant teaching (CCC 1385, **at least rung 3**) and law (can. 916). Chapter teaching no canon repeats (concomitance as the reason; the contrast of modes; 1 Cor 11:28 as precept; the memorial; the pledge), **at least rung 3**.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md), [3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md); the events: [`church-history` 6.4](../church-history/lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Concomitance

The words aim at body and at blood, and reach the living Christ, who cannot be had in pieces.

Trent XIII ch. 3: by "the force of the words" the body is under the bread and the blood under the wine; by the "natural connexion and concomitancy" of the risen Christ's parts, the blood is under the bread, the body under the wine, the soul under both; the divinity by the hypostatic union. Can. 3: the whole Christ under each species "and under every part of each species, when separated". Aquinas: present after the manner of substance, whole in each part (ST III q.76 aa.1-3). Consequences: the fraction breaks the species, not Christ; a crumb that keeps the species of bread is Christ; nothing necessary is lost under one kind ([communion under both kinds](#communion-under-both-kinds)). Constance (1415) already reasoned so.

**Level:** the fact, the whole Christ under each species and part, **rung 1** (can. 3); concomitance as the reason, **at least rung 3** (chapter teaching no canon repeats); Aquinas's account, explanation.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md), [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)

### Substance and accident

What a thing is, against how it is: the vocabulary the doctrine borrows, which the doctrine is not tied to.

From [`metaphysics` 2.1](../metaphysics/lessons/02-01-substance-and-accident.md) (the *Categories*: what is "present in a subject" cannot exist apart from it, that lesson's P2), [`metaphysics` 2.2](../metaphysics/lessons/02-02-change-act-and-potency.md) (substantial against accidental change) and [`thomistic-synthesis` 2.1](../thomistic-synthesis/lessons/02-01-act-and-potency-extended.md). The Eucharist is neither change ([transubstantiation](#transubstantiation)). Trent says *species* (appearances), never "accident"; the Synod of Jerusalem (1672) says the body is under the bread's accidents (*sumbebēkota*). **Is the doctrine committed to Aristotle?** For the Thomist, "substance" in can. 2 is the *Categories*' substance. On the other reading it means what a thing is as against how it appears; its defenders cite Trent's "species" and *Mysterium Fidei* 24. A bundle theory, on which a thing just is its properties, leaves no room for the *what* to change while every property stays: that is why the question is real. Either way no account may let the bread's substance remain, or reduce the change to one of meaning.

**Level:** the vocabulary, borrowed; "a Catholic must be a Thomist" overstates (MF 24); the Aristotle question, **freely disputed**. The argument belongs to the module's boss problem and to [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Accidents without a subject

Aquinas's account of what remains: the appearances of bread held in being by God, inhering in nothing.

ST III q.77 a.1: the accidents are not in the bread (gone), not in Christ's body (not its subject), not in the air; God as first cause sustains them. Ad 2: existing in a subject belongs to what an accident is, but these stand "through the Divine power sustaining them". He borrows Aristotle's categories, then asks God for what Aristotle's definition rules out by nature. a.2: dimensive quantity bears the other accidents. a.4: the presence ends when the change would have corrupted bread (the working criterion for a decayed host; CCC 1377: as long as the species subsist). Not in Trent. Constance (1415) condemned Wyclif's thesis that the accidents of bread do *not* remain without a subject, within a global censure (*Inter cunctas*, 1418).

**Level:** **not defined**; explanation, no magisterial level; Constance's weight here is the theologians' question.

**Does not license:** "Trent defined accidents without a subject, as Aristotle teaches".

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Transignification

With transfinalization: early-1960s proposals to restate the change as a new meaning or purpose, rejected as substitutes, accepted as consequences.

Some theologians, chiefly in the Netherlands (the lessons name no one), restated the change as *transignification* (a new meaning) or *transfinalization* (a new purpose). Paul VI, *Mysterium Fidei* (encyclical, 3 September 1965): rejects treating the change as if it involved "nothing more than" these (§11); accepts that the species take on a new signification and finality **because** they contain a new reality rightly called ontological (§46); Trent's formulas are tied to no culture, science or school and may be clarified only with their meaning kept (§§24-25); "real" is presence par excellence (§39, rejecting a "pneumatic" body present everywhere). Not to be confused with de la Taille's *Mysterium Fidei* (1921) on the sacrifice.

**Level:** *Mysterium Fidei*, **rung 3**, restating and applying Trent.

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Sacrifice of the Mass

The one sacrifice of the cross made present under signs, with the same victim and the same living priest: defined that it is a true and propitiatory sacrifice; how, left open.

Trent XXII ch. 1: Christ was "about to offer Himself once on the altar of the cross"; since his priesthood was not to end at his death, at the Supper he left the Church a visible sacrifice "whereby that bloody sacrifice ... might be represented, and the memory thereof remain even unto the end of the world, and its salutary virtue be applied to the remission of those sins which we daily commit". *Repraesentaretur*: made present again, not depicted (lesson's gloss; CCC 1366 "re-presented"). Ch. 2: in the Mass "that same Christ is contained and immolated in an unbloody manner"; "For the victim is one and the same, the same now offering by the ministry of priests, who then offered Himself on the cross, the manner alone of offering being different." The bloody oblation's fruits come through the unbloody, "so far is this (latter) from derogating in any way from that". The offerer is not absent: Christ is "always living to make intercession for us" (Heb 7:25, DR), "the centre and the principal actor" of the liturgy that honours the Father (CCC 662). CCC 1366-1368 restate.

**Propitiatory**, read in [`christology` 5.1](../christology/lessons/05-01-the-paschal-mystery-and-the-threefold-office.md)'s vocabulary: propitiation acts on God, and God provides it; it is not the Son changing the Father's mind. The Lord, "appeased by the oblation", grants "the grace and gift of penitence" and forgives even grave sins to those who come "contrite and penitent" (ch. 2). Offered for the living's sins, punishments, satisfactions and needs, and for the dead "not as yet fully purified".

**Who offers:** Christ, through priests [in the person of Christ the Head](#in-persona-christi-capitis); and the Church, in the Roman Canon's *nos servi tui, sed et plebs tua sancta ... offerimus* ("we your servants, and your holy people too, ... offer", lesson's translation); the faithful offer by the priest's hands and with him (*Mediator Dei* 92-93; LG 10).

**Level:** cans. 1-4 ([Trent Session XXII](#trent-session-xxii)), **rung 1**; the once-for-all premise (ch. 1) and the identity formula (ch. 2), on which can. 4 rests, chapter teaching no canon repeats, **at least rung 3 and not open to denial**; *Mediator Dei* 70, 115 (the separate species signify the separated body and blood; communion belongs to the Mass's integrity), **rung 3**; every account of [how the one sacrifice is present](#how-the-one-sacrifice-is-present), **freely disputed**.

**Does not mean:** a second sacrifice beside Calvary; a reminder only; forgiveness without repentance; that "represented" or "unbloody" means only symbolic (*unbloody* denies a new death, not a real offering).

**Other side:** [Lutheran and Reformed on the sacrifice of the Mass](#lutheran-and-reformed-on-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Trent Session XXII

17 September 1562: the Doctrine on the Sacrifice of the Mass, nine chapters and nine canons, plus a reform decree and the decree referring the chalice to the pope.

| Chapter | Teaches | Canon |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | institution: the once-for-all premise; a visible sacrifice "represented", remembered, applied; the new Passover; Malachi's clean oblation foretold (*praedixit*, *futurum esset*) | 1 (a true and proper sacrifice, not merely Christ given to eat); 2 ("Do this" made the apostles priests to offer) |
| 2 | the visible sacrifice propitiatory for the living and the dead; one victim, the same offerer, the manner alone different | 3 (propitiatory, not only praise and thanksgiving or bare commemoration, not profiting only the communicant, offered for living and dead); 4 (no derogation from the cross) |
| 3 | Masses in honour of the saints offered to God alone | 5 |
| 4 | the Canon, free from error, composed "out of the very words of the Lord, the traditions of the apostles, and the pious institutions also of holy pontiffs" ([Roman Canon](#roman-canon)) | 6 |
| 5 | the ceremonies | 7 |
| 6 | Masses where only the priest communicates "truly common"; the council wishes the faithful to communicate at every Mass | 8 |
| 7 | water mixed with the wine | 9 (in part) |
| 8 | Mass not to be celebrated everywhere in the vernacular ("not expedient") | 9 (the low-voiced Canon may not be condemned, nor the vernacular required "only") |
| 9 | preliminary remark on the canons | |

**The reform decree** "on things to be observed and avoided" (same day) told bishops to prohibit "all manner of conditions and bargains for recompenses", demands "little removed from a simonical taint", and "the observance of a fixed number of certain masses and of candles" as superstition. **Said plainly** ([3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)): Masses were bargained over for money; the Augsburg Confession had complained of Masses "used for gain" thirty-two years earlier.

**Level:** cans. 1-4, **rung 1**; can. 6, **rung 1** for "contains no error" only (not the Canon's age, sole use or unchangeability); can. 9, **rung 1**, read narrowly ([7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)); ch. 8's "not expedient", prudential, **no rung**; chapter teaching no canon repeats, **at least rung 3**; the reform decree, **no rung**.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Ephapax

"Once for all": Christ's single offering, a datum for the doctrine of the Mass, not an objection to defuse.

*Ephapax* at Heb 7:27; 9:12; 10:10 (*hapax* 9:26, 28); "by one oblation" he has perfected the sanctified (Heb 10:14). Exegesis: [`new-testament` 6.2](../new-testament/lessons/06-02-hebrews.md); the one offering made to God: [`christology` 5.2](../christology/lessons/05-02-ransom-christus-victor-and-sacrifice.md). Trent XXII ch. 1 takes it as the decree's premise ("about to offer Himself once"), and Heb 7:25's living high priest is how Trent can say "the same now offering" without unsaying "once".

**Level:** the once-for-all premise, **at least rung 3, not open to denial**.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### In persona Christi capitis

The ordained minister acts in the person of Christ the Head: since 2009 the Code gives this capacity to bishops and presbyters, and service to deacons.

At Mass Christ is the principal offerer, "the same now offering by the ministry of priests" (Trent XXII ch. 2); the priest "bears Christ's image, in Whose person and by Whose power" he consecrates (ST III q.83 a.1 ad 3); CCC 1548. **Can. 1009 §3**, added by Benedict XVI's *Omnium in mentem* (26 October 2009): bishops and presbyters receive the mission and capacity to act in the person of Christ the Head, whereas deacons are empowered to serve the People of God in the diakonia of liturgy, word and charity. The canon states two positive clauses; the contrast is implicit, and it states **no negative** about deacons. The 1983 can. 1008 had put all the ordained "in the person of Christ the Head", and the ITC's 2002 diaconate study was written under it. *Inter Insigniores* §5 builds the reservation's fittingness argument on it (the priest as Christ's image at the consecration). The faithful offer by the priest's hands and with him, but have no priestly power (*Mediator Dei* 82-84, 92-93).

**Level:** can. 1009 §3, **law, no rung**, restating LG 28-29 (**at least rung 3**).

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### Mass stipends

An offering given to have a Mass applied for an intention: it buys nothing; it fixes an intention.

A priest may apply the Mass for anyone, living or dead (can. 901), and may accept an offering to apply it for a definite intention (can. 945 §1), and is urged to celebrate for the needy without one (§2); the donor shares in supporting the Church (can. 946); any appearance of trade is excluded (can. 947); separate Masses for separate offerings (can. 948); obligations and registers (cann. 949-958). Trent XXII's reform decree banned bargains and superstitious fixed numbers. How much one Mass avails for the dead Trent does not say; Aquinas: the offering "suffices of its own quantity" for all punishment but avails "according to the measure of their devotion" (ST III q.79 a.5), explanation. The Code presupposes many Masses for one intention (cann. 948, 950) on an account never defined.

**Level:** **law, no rung**; that the Mass may be offered for the dead, **rung 1** (XXII can. 3).

**Does not mean:** a price.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### How the one sacrifice is present

Trent defined that the Mass is the one sacrifice made present; every account of how is theology, freely disputed.

| Account | Claim |
|---|---|
| Destruction theories (post-Tridentine: Bellarmine, de Lugo, Vasquez) | sacrifice needs a real or mystical change in the victim, sought in the double consecration or the priest's communion |
| Maurice de la Taille, *Mysterium Fidei* (1921; not Paul VI's encyclical) | Supper (oblation) and cross (immolation) are one sacrifice; the Mass is the Church's offering of the victim once immolated |
| Anscar Vonier, *A Key to the Doctrine of the Eucharist* | a sacramental sacrifice: the cross present in the sign of the separate consecration, after Aquinas (ST III q.83 a.1: an image of the Passion and an application of its fruit) |
| Odo Casel, *Das christliche Kultmysterium* | mystery-presence: the saving act itself made present in the rite |

**Constraints:** *Mediator Dei* 70 and 115 (rung 3): the separate species signify the separation of body and blood; communion belongs to the integrity of the sacrifice. What "unbloody" consists in, no act says. Casel's mystery theology also stands behind SC 102's "in some way made present" ([liturgical year](#liturgical-year)), and *Mediator Dei* 165 warns against a "vague and uncertain" presence of the mysteries without naming anyone ([liturgical movement](#liturgical-movement)).

**Level:** all **freely disputed (rung 5)**, none magisterial. Treating one as Church teaching (on the strength of MD 115 or the Catechism's "re-presents") overstates; inferring from the open *how* that the sacrifice is opinion understates.

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md)

### Eucharistic fast

One hour before communion, not before Mass, water and medicine excepted: reverence in the body, wholly the Church's to set.

Can. 919 §1: abstain for at least an hour before Holy Communion from food and drink, water and medicine excepted; §2 a priest celebrating two or three times the same day may eat before the second or third; §3 the elderly, the infirm and those who care for them are exempt. **History:** the fast from midnight; Pius XII, *Christus Dominus* (6 January 1953), water does not break it; *Sacram Communionem* (19 March 1957), three hours from solid food and alcohol, one hour from non-alcoholic drink; Paul VI one hour (1964). Each Church its own fast (CCC 1387). A breach makes the reception illicit, not invalid.

**Level:** **discipline, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)

### Communion under both kinds

The cup withdrawn from the laity for most of seven centuries and restored from 1963: the doctrine did not move, the discipline moved twice.

Until about the twelfth century Western laity normally received both kinds; the chalice was withdrawn chiefly for fear of spilling, and unevenly (Fortescue, *The Mass*, 1912; Aquinas, "a prudent custom in some churches", ST III q.80 a.12). Jacob of Mies proposed the lay cup in Prague (1414), whence the Utraquists (*sub utraque*). **Constance**, Session XIII (15 June 1415), granted that "in the primitive Church this sacrament was received by the faithful under both kinds" and kept the custom because "the whole body and blood of Christ is truly contained as much under the species of bread as under the species of wine" (lesson's translation); its own standing in 1415 is [`church-history` 5.4](../church-history/lessons/05-04-avignon-the-great-schism-and-the-councils.md)'s question. **Trent XXI** (1562): no divine precept binds the laity to both; Christ whole and a true sacrament under either; just causes for one kind; the concession of the cup referred to the pope (Session XXII). **SC 55** (1963) reopened it, Trent's dogmatic principles intact; GIRM 281-283: both kinds the fuller sign, norms from the diocesan bishop; can. 925. The usual form in the Eastern rites (CCC 1390).

**Level:** doctrine **rung 1** (XXI cans. 1, 3: nothing necessary to salvation lost under one kind, not pastoral reassurance); distribution, **discipline, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md); the power behind it: [substance of the sacraments](#substance-of-the-sacraments)

### Trent Session XXI

16 July 1562: communion under both species and of infants, and the chapter that states the Church's power over the sacraments' dispensation.

| | Chapter | Canon |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | no divine precept binds laity and non-consecrating clerics to both kinds; John 6, "however ... it be understood", is no command of both, its interpretation left to the Fathers and Doctors | anathema on saying both kinds are required by God's precept |
| 2 | the Church's power in the dispensation: "their substance being untouched" (*salva illorum substantia*), it may ordain or change "what things soever it may judge most expedient, for the profit of those who receive, or for the veneration of the said sacraments" | anathema on denying just causes for one kind |
| 3 | Christ whole and entire, and a true sacrament, under either species; no grace necessary to salvation lost | anathema on denying it |
| 4 | communion not necessary for little children before the age of reason | anathema on saying it is |

A reform decree of the same day abolished the name and use of the questors of alms ([4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)).

**Level:** cans. 1-4, **rung 1**; ch. 2, the premise of can. 2, restated in GIRM 282, **at least rung 3** ([substance of the sacraments](#substance-of-the-sacraments)).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md); ch. 2 in [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Admission to communion

Communion expresses full communion, so the rule follows it; the exceptions serve an individual's grave need, never intercommunion.

Who belongs, and how fully, is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-02-subsistit-in-and-the-status-of-other-churches.md)'s. UR 8 sets two principles for worship in common: witness to unity, which generally forbids it, and sharing in grace, which sometimes commends it. Eucharistic communion is inseparably linked to full ecclesial communion (Ecumenical Directory 1993 §129). **Can. 844:** §2 Catholics may receive penance, the Eucharist and anointing from non-Catholic ministers in whose Churches these are valid, when a Catholic minister is physically or morally impossible; §3 members of Eastern Churches not in full communion receive licitly if they ask of their own accord and are properly disposed (OE 27; UR 15), their own Churches' stricter discipline respected (ED 122, 124); §4 other Christians only in danger of death or grave necessity judged by the bishop or conference, if they cannot approach their own minister, ask of their own accord, manifest Catholic faith in the sacrament and are properly disposed (ED 130-131): the conditions are **cumulative**. For the Reformation communities the reason given is UR 22's defect of Orders (CCC 1400). Concelebration without full communion is never lawful (*Ecclesia de Eucharistia* 44); the exception meets individual need, not intercommunion (EdE 45); EdE 46 glosses "Catholic faith" only as the faith the Church professes about the sacrament, and whether that must include the Mass as sacrifice is where readings part. Can. 915 (manifest grave sin) is [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)'s; Aquinas: receiving "expresses" incorporation, so receiving in mortal sin is "lying to this sacrament" (ST III q.80 a.4).

**Level:** can. 844 and the Directory, **law, no rung**; UR 8, 15, **rung 3**; EdE where it teaches, rung 3.

**Does not mean:** that any Christian who believes in the real presence is admitted.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)

### Viaticum

Communion given to the dying, the last sacrament and the first reason the Eucharist is reserved.

Nicaea can. 13: the dying are not to be deprived of "the last and most indispensable Viaticum" (Percival). Trent XIII ch. 6, can. 7: reserving the Eucharist and carrying it to the sick is lawful. Viaticum is reservation's primary and original purpose (*Eucharisticum Mysterium* 49, 1967, quoting the 1949 instruction *Quam plurimum*). In danger of death from any cause (can. 921 §1); not delayed, while fully conscious (can. 922); in necessity any minister of communion brings it (can. 911 §2). SC 74: the continuous rite of penance, anointing and viaticum; CCC 1517, 1524-1525: the Eucharist the last sacrament, with penance and anointing the sacraments preparing for the homeland. A condemned prisoner receives penance and viaticum, not anointing (danger of death not from sickness). Withheld from someone persisting in a request for euthanasia (*Samaritanus Bonus* V.11, 2020; cann. 915, 843 §1).

**Level:** XIII can. 7, **rung 1**; the canons and instructions, law; *Samaritanus Bonus*, rung 3.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)

### Reservation and adoration

Kept first for the dying, and adored because kept; the cult points back to the altar.

Trent XIII chs. 5-6, cans. 6-7: latria; the feast, processions and public exposition legitimate; adorers not idolaters; reservation and carrying to the sick lawful. Ends of reservation: viaticum primary, communion outside Mass and adoration secondary (*Eucharisticum Mysterium* 49). **Tabernacle:** one per church, prominent, immovable, solid, opaque, locked, a lamp before it (cann. 938, 940). **Exposition and benediction** (cann. 941-944; EM 60-66): no Mass in the same space before the exposed sacrament (EM 61; can. 941 §2); no exposition merely to give benediction after Mass (EM 66); the Mass the origin and consummation of worship outside it (EM 3e, 50). The forms are second-millennium: the elevation after the words of institution (France, about 1200, per Fortescue), Corpus Christi (1264), later exposition and benediction. Latria's ground: one adoration of the Word with his flesh ([`christology` 3.4](../christology/lessons/03-04-the-grace-of-the-head.md)).

**Level:** XIII cans. 6-7, **rung 1**; the norms, **discipline, no rung**; benediction and processions judged as devotions in [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md).

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md), [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Eucharistic ecclesiology

"We, being many, are one bread, one body": the Eucharist builds the Church, so who approaches it is an ecclesial question.

1 Cor 10:17 (DR). The Eucharist's *res tantum* is the unity of the mystical body (ST III q.73 a.3; [threefold analysis](#threefold-analysis)); to receive "expresses" that one is made one with Christ and incorporated in his members, done by living faith, "which no one has who is in mortal sin" (q.80 a.4). Henri de Lubac, *Corpus Mysticum* (1944): the early medieval West called the Eucharist the "mystical body" and the Church the "true body" until, after Berengar, the terms changed places. John Paul II: the Eucharist builds the Church and the Church makes the Eucharist (*Ecclesia de Eucharistia* 26). Trent: the sacrament is a symbol of the one body whose head Christ is (XIII ch. 2). The Orthodox eucharistic ecclesiology of Afanasiev and Zizioulas, and LG 26, are [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-02-the-local-and-the-universal-church.md)'s; that card states them.

**Level:** EdE, **rung 3**; Aquinas and de Lubac, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)

## The sacraments of healing

Module 4: penance under its changing disciplines, indulgences as a practice, and the anointing of the sick. The doctrine of the debt of punishment and the treasury is `theology-of-debt`'s; this module takes the sacrament and the practice.

### Penance

Baptism forgives once and cannot be repeated; this sacrament forgives by judgement, as often as needed.

Trent, Session XIV (25 November 1551), chs. 1-9 and cans. 1-15 on penance (Waterworth). Had the baptized kept their justice, no second sacrament of forgiveness would be needed (ch. 1). A true sacrament instituted by Christ for reconciling the faithful "as often as they fall into sin after baptism" (can. 1), distinct from baptism (can. 2), instituted when, risen, he breathed on the apostles: "Whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven them: and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained" (John 20:23, DR; can. 3); *aphēte*, "you release", *kratēte*, "you hold fast" (lesson's gloss). Releasing or holding is a judgement, and no one judges a cause he does not know, so grave sins must be told (ch. 5). Matt 18:18 (the plural "whatsoever you shall bind") serves for the minister (ch. 6, can. 10); 16:19 is only alluded to in "the keys" (chs. 5, 8). Trent does not cite James 5:16. Penance is "a laborious kind of baptism", as Trent reports the Fathers (XIV ch. 2).

| Element | Text |
|---|---|
| Quasi-matter | contrition, confession, satisfaction, "as it were the matter" (can. 4; [acts of the penitent](#acts-of-the-penitent)) |
| Form | "I absolve thee" (ch. 3): *Ego te absolvo a peccatis tuis in nomine Patris, et Filii, et Spiritus Sancti* after a prayer for pardon and peace (CCC 1449); Byzantine formulas are invocations (CCC 1481) |
| Minister | priests alone, even in mortal sin (can. 10), with the faculty ([absolution](#absolution)) |
| Confession | of divine right (can. 6); every mortal sin remembered after diligent examination, by kind and number, with circumstances that change the kind (can. 7; can. 988 §1); Lateran IV fixed only the yearly minimum (ch. 5; can. 8; can. 989) |
| Effects | reconciliation with God (ch. 3) and the Church (can. 959); guilt and eternal punishment remitted, [temporal punishment](#temporal-punishment) can remain (can. 15), answered by satisfaction through Christ's merits (can. 13) |

**Lateran IV, canon 21** (*Omnis utriusque sexus*, 1215): everyone past the age of discretion confesses yearly to his own priest and receives at Easter; the priest may not betray the sinner "by word, sign or any other means", under deposition and lifelong penance in a strict monastery ([seal of confession](#seal-of-confession)). Law, protected by XIV can. 8 and XIII can. 9.

**Leo the Great**, Letter 168 (*Magna indignatione*, 6 March 459, to the bishops of Campania, Samnium and Picenum; lesson's translation from Denzinger): no written list of sins is to be read out in public; "it suffices that the guilt of consciences be made known to priests alone by secret confession"; the priest "comes as intercessor". A disciplinary decree, not a definition: what was public was the penance, not the list of sins; absolution still a prayer. Trent agrees: secret confession was in use from the beginning, and no divine precept commands public confession (XIV ch. 5).

**History, in brief:** [canonical penance](#canonical-penance) (public, once in a life), [tariff penance](#tariff-penance) (private, repeatable), then the scholastic settlement (three acts, absolution as form, the indicative "I absolve thee", defended in ST III q.84 a.3), Lateran IV (1215), Trent (1551), the 1983 Code and *Misericordia Dei* (2002).

**Level:** XIV cans. 1-4, 6-7, 9-11, 13, 15, **rung 1** (can. 7 as [`moral-theology` 1.1](../moral-theology/lessons/01-01-what-moral-theology-is.md) grades it); taught in the chapters, **not defined**: the form (ch. 3), perfect contrition reconciling with the desire of the sacrament (ch. 4), jurisdiction (ch. 7); the yearly precept, faculties, penalties, general absolution, Leo's decree, **law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "the Lateran precept makes confession obligatory" (confessing grave sin is of divine right; Lateran fixed the yearly minimum).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### Canonical penance

The ancient public discipline for grave sin after baptism: years in an order of penitents, reconciled by the bishop, in some regions once in a lifetime.

The grave sinner did public penance for years in an order of penitents, barred from communion until the bishop reconciled him, in some regions once in a lifetime (CCC 1447), so many waited for their deathbed. Hermas allowed one repentance after baptism (*Shepherd*, Mandate 4.3); Tertullian, still a Catholic (*On Repentance* 7, ANF 3): "In the vestibule He has stationed the second repentance for opening to such as knock: but now once for all, because now for the second time; but never more". Heb 6:4-6 ("impossible ... to be renewed again to penance") and the early fight over it: [`new-testament` 6.2](../new-testament/lessons/06-02-hebrews.md). Novatian denied the Church could reconcile apostates; Carthage (251) graded their penance ([`patristics` 2.4](../patristics/lessons/02-04-cyprian-of-carthage.md)). Confession of the sins themselves was secret to priests (Leo, Letter 168, 459). Penitents could not be anointed, since anointing is "a kind of sacrament" (Innocent I, 416; [extreme unction](#extreme-unction)).

**Level:** the once-only rule, a **discipline, later dropped**; the power to bind and loose, the Church holds, did not change.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### Tariff penance

Private, repeatable penance, carried onto the continent by Irish missionaries from the seventh century, with books pricing each sin.

Private and repeatable (CCC 1447), with penitential books fixing a penance per sin and commutations ([`theology-of-debt` 3.5](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-05-the-christian-jubilee-and-the-treasury.md)); in time absolution came at the confession, satisfaction after; formulas were prayers (deprecative) into the twelfth century, then the indicative "I absolve thee", defended in ST III q.84 a.3 (the form taken from Christ's words, "because sins are fetters").

**Level:** discipline, no rung.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### Acts of the penitent

Contrition, confession and satisfaction are "as it were the matter": the penitent supplies the sign's stuff by what he does, the priest's sentence makes it a sacrament.

Trent XIV can. 4 (*quasi materia*). The Roman Catechism: real matter, but not applied from outside like water or chrism. Scotus made the absolution alone the sacrament, the acts required for worthy reception: a school position. The penitent's acts belonging to the sacrament is why reviviscence hardly arises in penance ([reviviscence](#reviviscence)). Contrition: [contrition and attrition](#contrition-and-attrition); confession by kind and number: [penance](#penance); satisfaction answers the remaining [temporal punishment](#temporal-punishment). The purpose of amendment is required for absolution (can. 987).

**Level:** XIV can. 4, **rung 1**; Thomist and Scotist accounts of the matter, **school theology, no level**. Trent's "quasi" is not shown to have been meant to leave the dispute open.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### Absolution

"After the manner of a judicial act": a tribunal of mercy and a healing, by a priest with the faculty.

Trent XIV ch. 6 and can. 9: absolution is a judicial act, not a bare ministry of declaring sins forgiven; *Reconciliatio et Paenitentia* (post-synodal apostolic exhortation, 2 December 1984) §31: a "tribunal of mercy" and a healing; the confessor judge and physician (can. 978). Priests alone absolve, even priests in mortal sin (can. 10; can. 965). **Orders are not enough:** absolution without jurisdiction is void (ch. 7); the Code requires the faculty for validity (can. 966 §1), granted by the local ordinary (can. 969) or by office (can. 968 §1), save in danger of death, when any priest, even one lacking the faculty, absolves validly and licitly from every sin and censure (can. 976; [faculties](#faculties)). Bishops may reserve cases with effect before God (can. 11). The 1983 Code reserves censures, not sins; a confessor may remit an undeclared *latae sententiae* excommunication or interdict when waiting would be hard, imposing recourse within a month (can. 1357). A deacon's "absolution" is no sacrament (can. 10). Absolution of an accomplice in a sin against the sixth commandment: can. 1384 (2021).

**Level:** XIV cans. 9-11, **rung 1**; ch. 7 on jurisdiction, **taught, not defined**; the canons, law.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md); [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md) (the laicized priest at the roadside, can. 976)

### Contrition and attrition

Contrition is sorrow from love that reconciles even before the sacrament, with its desire; attrition is sorrow from fear, a graced start that disposes within the sacrament. Whether attrition needs some love is open.

Trent XIV ch. 4: contrition is sorrow and detestation of sin with the purpose of sinning no more; perfected by charity, it reconciles before the sacrament, though not without the desire of it (CCC 1452). Attrition, from sin's ugliness or fear of hell, is a gift of God if it excludes the will to sin with hope of pardon; it cannot justify without the sacrament but disposes for grace within it (ch. 4; CCC 1453). Can. 5: such sorrow is true and prepares for grace, not hypocrisy. Perfect contrition with the resolve to confess is can. 916's exception for communion ([3.5](lessons/03-05-communion-reservation-adoration.md)) and the hope where no priest comes ([4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)). **The open question:** whether attrition needs some beginning of love of God divided contritionists from attritionists; the Holy Office decree of 5 May 1667 under Alexander VII forbade either side to censure the other until the Holy See decides (the attritionist view then "more common"). None has come.

**Level:** can. 5, **rung 1**; ch. 4 on perfect contrition, **taught, not defined**; attrition and love, **rung 5, by decree**.

**Does not license:** "Trent merely tolerates attrition".

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### Seal of confession

The confessor may not betray a penitent in any way, for any reason: law, taught as exceptionless and grounded in divine law, never defined.

Can. 983 §1: the sacramental seal is "inviolable"; the confessor may not betray the penitent by words or in any manner or for any reason; §2 binds interpreters and anyone who learns of sins from confession. Can. 984 §1: no use of knowledge from confession to the penitent's detriment, even without danger of revelation (the Holy Office condemned the contrary on 18 November 1682). **Penalty:** direct violation incurs automatic excommunication reserved to the Apostolic See, **can. 1386 §1** as revised in 2021 (formerly can. 1388, still cited in CCC 1467's footnote); §3 punishes recording or divulging confessions. Lateran IV c. 21 (1215): deposition and lifelong penance in a strict monastery. CCC 1467: the secret "admits of no exceptions". The Apostolic Penitentiary's note on the internal forum (published 1 July 2019): the seal rests on divine law, no human power has jurisdiction over it, not comparable to professional secrecy; civil compulsion meets the same answer. Aquinas: the priest knows it "not as man, but as God knows it" (Suppl. q.11 a.1 ad 1); secrecy draws penitents to confess. Inside the confession a priest may urge a penitent to prevent an injustice and judge whether he has the purpose of amendment absolution requires (can. 987).

**Level:** **law, and at least rung 3** for its admitting no exception (CCC 1467; the 2019 note); **never defined**.

**Does not license:** "a defined dogma", or "a rule that bends in hard cases".

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### General absolution

Absolving many at once without individual confession: an emergency exit that cancels no confession owed.

Individual, integral confession is the only ordinary way (can. 960; *Reconciliatio et Paenitentia* §§32-33, the third rite "exceptional"). Can. 961: only in imminent danger of death without time for confessors, or in grave necessity (too few confessors, so that penitents would long be without grace or communion through no fault of their own), as the diocesan bishop judges; a feast-day or pilgrimage crowd is not enough. Can. 962 §1: received validly only by one suitably disposed and intending to confess each grave sin individually in due time; can. 963: before any further general absolution. *Misericordia Dei* (apostolic letter motu proprio, 7 April 2002) §§4-7 tightened the practice (§7: one in habitual grave sin who does not intend to change cannot validly receive). **Validity turns on the penitent:** can. 961 has no invalidating clause (can. 10), so absolution outside its conditions is illicit, and valid and fruitful for a disposed penitent intending individual confession, invalid for one who intends never to confess.

**Level:** **law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md)

### Temporal punishment

What can remain after guilt and eternal punishment are forgiven: the debt of punishment indulgences and satisfaction address.

Trent VI can. 30 (anathema on saying that after the grace of justification no debt of temporal punishment remains); XIV cans. 12, 15 (God does not always remit the whole punishment with the guilt; the keys bind as well as loose). Chapter 2 of XXII's "sins, punishments, satisfactions" is the same ledger ([`theology-of-debt` 3.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-04-aquinas-satisfaction-and-the-debt-of-punishment.md)). Baptism, by contrast, remits all punishment ([effects of baptism](#effects-of-baptism)).

**Level:** **rung 1**.

**Owned by:** [`theology-of-debt` 3.4](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-04-aquinas-satisfaction-and-the-debt-of-punishment.md)-3.6; purgatory, [`creation-grace-last-things`](../creation-grace-last-things/syllabus.md).

*Lessons:* [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md), [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)

### Indulgence

A remission before God of temporal punishment for sins already forgiven as to guilt, gained by the duly disposed through the Church dispensing the treasury: a grant, not a sacrament.

*Indulgentiarum Doctrina* (Paul VI, 1 January 1967) norm 1 = can. 992 = CCC 1471: a remission before God of the temporal punishment due to sins whose guilt is already forgiven, which a properly disposed believer gains under defined conditions through the Church, dispensing with authority the treasury of the satisfactions of Christ and the saints. It never remits guilt. Not a sacrament: no matter and form, no grace conferred, none of the three sacramental verdicts; gained in full, in part, or not at all. Who can gain one (can. 996): baptized, not excommunicated, in the state of grace at least when the work is finished, with at least a general intention, doing the work at the time and in the manner the grant specifies. Who can grant: only the supreme authority and those given the power by law or the pope (can. 995). The 1967 reform was built so that the disposition decides: the *Enchiridion* gives more weight to the believer's act (*opus operantis*) than to the work done (*opus operatum*) (Praenotanda 4). To the living by way of absolution, to the dead [by way of suffrage](#by-way-of-suffrage) (Leo X, *Cum postquam*, 1518). Treasury: Clement VI, *Unigenitus* (1343); ID §5. Bound: that the Church can grant them and that they are useful. Free: whether to seek any (ID 11, the "holy and free liberty of the sons of God", ranking the Mass and the sacraments ahead of them).

**Said plainly** ([4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)): indulgences were sold. Alms were a lawful indulgenced work (Aquinas defends grants for building churches and bridges, Suppl. q.25 a.3), and late-medieval collectors preached them for money and promised more than the grants said; Boniface IX condemned such sellers in 1392 ([`theology-of-debt` 3.5](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-05-the-christian-jubilee-and-the-treasury.md)); the St Peter's campaign of 1517 is [`church-history` 6.1](../church-history/lessons/06-01-the-late-medieval-church-and-luthers-protest.md)'s. Session XXI's reform decree (ch. 9, 16 July 1562) abolished the alms-collectors (*quaestores*); Session XXV's decree (December 1563) orders "that all evil gains for the obtaining thereof,—whence a most prolific cause of abuses amongst the Christian people has been derived,—be wholly abolished". Paul VI names the misuse again (ID §8). Giving to the poor is still an indulgenced work.

**Level:** the Church's power to grant and their usefulness, **rung 1** (Trent XXV, with anathema); the treasury, **rung 3** (authentic papal teaching Trent never names; ID's doctrinal chapters restate; CCC 1471 and can. 992 add no weight); every norm, **discipline, no rung**.

**Owned by:** the theology, [`theology-of-debt` 3.5](../theology-of-debt/lessons/03-05-the-christian-jubilee-and-the-treasury.md)-3.6; this card takes the practice. Luther's objection is stated there and argued in [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md).

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)

### Plenary and partial indulgences

A plenary removes all the temporal punishment, a partial some; since 1967 a partial matches what one's own act earns.

1967 norm 2; can. 993. Before 1967 partials carried figures such as "300 days", reckoned as the remission so many days of the old canonical penance would have won, never as days in purgatory (the standard explanation; *Catholic Encyclopedia*, "Indulgences", 1910, as a witness to it, not authority; no magisterial text says what the figures meant). The 1967 norm 4 abolished the figures; norm 5 (= *Enchiridion* norm 4) replaced them: an indulgenced act done with at least a contrite heart receives, besides the remission it earns by itself, an equal remission through the Church. A plenary lacking full disposition or a condition becomes partial ([conditions for a plenary indulgence](#conditions-for-a-plenary-indulgence)). Real and local indulgences were abolished (1967 norm 12): indulgences attach to acts, objects and places being only their occasion. Numbering: the current numbers are the *Enchiridion*'s (1967 n. 5 = EI N. 4; n. 6 = N. 18; n. 7 = N. 20).

**Level:** **discipline, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)

### Conditions for a plenary indulgence

The work, three conditions with a generous calendar, and one disposition with none: no affection for any sin, even venial.

*Enchiridion* norm 20 (from 1967 norm 7): (1) the work; (2) sacramental confession; (3) eucharistic Communion; (4) prayer for the pope's intentions (one Our Father and one Hail Mary suffice); (5) the exclusion of all affection (*affectus*) for any sin, even venial. Lacking full disposition or a condition, the indulgence is partial (§4). The conditions may fall several days before or after the work, same-day Communion and prayer being fitting (§3); the Apostolic Penitentiary's note *The Gift of the Indulgence* (29 January 2000, ¶5) reads "several" as about twenty, a note, not a norm. One confession serves several plenaries; each needs its own Communion and prayer (§2). One plenary a day, except at the point of death (norm 18), when it is granted even if no priest can come; the 2025 Jubilee decree (Apostolic Penitentiary, 13 May 2024) allowed a second plenary the same day, for the dead only, with a second Communion at Mass. State of grace at least at the end of the work (can. 996). A confessor may commute the work and the other conditions for the legitimately impeded (norm 24), never the detachment (*The Gift of the Indulgence* ¶6). Plenaries were cut back so that they would be gained with the proper dispositions (ID 12).

**Level:** **discipline, no rung**: it binds, it can change, as 2025 showed.

**Does not license:** "the usual conditions are a checklist" (three are; the fourth no one can check from outside), or calling the twenty-day window or one-a-day "Church teaching".

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)

### Enchiridion of Indulgences

The book of norms and grants: *Enchiridion Indulgentiarum*, 4th edition, Apostolic Penitentiary decree of 16 July 1999 (Latin typical text).

Norms N. 1-26; four **general grants**, all partial: raising the mind to God amid duties and troubles with a short invocation; serving people in need; freely giving up something lawful and pleasant in a spirit of penance; open witness to the faith. Then **specific grants**, e.g. grant 7 (adoring the Blessed Sacrament at least half an hour, plenary; a shorter visit, partial), grant 29 (a devout cemetery visit with prayer for the dead, 1-8 November, plenary applicable only to the souls in purgatory), grant 30 (reading Scripture in an approved text at least half an hour, plenary; less, partial; hearing it if reasonably impeded). Mass and the sacraments are not themselves indulgenced, since they already sanctify supremely (Praenotanda 3). Devout use of a blessed object of piety: partial (1967 norm 17; EI N. 15). Diocesan bishops: partial indulgences in their territory and the papal blessing with plenary three times a year (EI N. 7); grants take effect only after authentic copies are reviewed by the Penitentiary (EI N. 12).

**Reading an entry:** who may gain it; the exact work (place, day, duration); plenary or partial; for whom. For a plenary, "the usual conditions" means norm 20, printed or not.

**Level:** **discipline, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)

### By way of suffrage
