To the living the Church applies the remission by her authority; for the dead she offers it to God as intercession, with no receipt.

*Per modum suffragii* against *per modum absolutionis* (Leo X, *Cum postquam*, 1518). Any indulgence may be gained for oneself or applied to the dead by way of suffrage (can. 994; EI N. 3), never to another person living on earth (*The Gift of the Indulgence* ¶7; CCC 1471's "may be applied to the living or the dead" is loose and not the rule). Some grants are for the dead only (grant 29). None of the texts promises that a given suffrage reached a given soul. That the suffrages of the living help the souls in purgatory is defined at Florence (*Laetentur caeli*, 1439) and restated by Trent (Session XXV, Decree concerning Purgatory), which puts the Mass first among them.

**Level:** that suffrages help the dead, **rung 1** (Florence); that indulgences reach the dead by way of suffrage, **rung 3** (*Cum postquam*); accounts of how suffrage reaches a soul, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)

### Anointing of the sick

The sacrament of the seriously sick, from the start of danger, given by a priest alone; the sacrament of the dying is viaticum.

**James 5:14-15:** the sick man calls the *presbyterous tēs ekklēsias*, the church's elders, a body with a standing; they pray over him, "anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord" (DR); the prayer of faith *sōsei*, will save (rescue and healing alike), the Lord *egerei*, will raise him (from the bed; also the verb of resurrection), and if he has sinned he will be forgiven (lesson's glosses; [`new-testament` 6.3](../new-testament/lessons/06-03-james-faith-and-works.md) glosses *presbyterous*). Mark 6:13: "insinuated", hinted at, not instituted (Trent XIV ch. 1).

**Trent, Session XIV, On Extreme Unction** (25 November 1551): a true sacrament instituted by Christ, promulgated by James (ch. 1, can. 1); it confers grace, remits sins and comforts the sick, and has not ceased (can. 2); the Roman rite is not repugnant to James (can. 3, not thereby unchangeable); James's presbyters are priests ordained by a bishop, and the priest alone is the *proper* minister (can. 4). Ch. 2: the Spirit's grace cleanses sins still to be expiated and the remains of sin, strengthens by confidence in God's mercy, and gives bodily health "at times", when that serves the soul. Ch. 3: given "to the sick, but to those especially who lie in such danger as to seem to be about to depart this life: whence also it is called the sacrament of the departing"; repeatable in another like danger.

**The rite since 1972** (Paul VI, *Sacram Unctionem Infirmorum*, 30 November 1972): forehead and hands; olive or another plant oil, duly blessed (by a bishop, those equivalent in law, or any presbyter in necessity within the celebration, can. 999); one anointing in necessity (can. 1000 §1); the form, said once: *Per istam sanctam unctionem et suam piissimam misericordiam adiuvet te Dominus gratia Spiritus Sancti, ut a peccatis liberatum te salvet atque propitius allevet* ("Through this holy anointing and his most loving mercy may the Lord help you by the grace of the Holy Spirit, so that, freed from sins, he may save you and kindly raise you up", lesson's translation). The old form asked pardon for each sense's sins; the new prays James's promises.

**Minister.** Every priest and only a priest anoints validly (can. 1003 §1). *Ecclesiae de mysterio* (1997, approved *in forma specifica*) art. 9: no ordinary or extraordinary non-priest minister; the non-ordained may perform no anointing with the oil of the sick or any other oil. The CDF's note of 11 February 2005 calls the doctrine *definitive tenenda* and any attempt a simulation ([definitive teaching](#definitive-teaching)).

**Recipient.** SC 73: the time has certainly come once a believer begins to be in danger of death from sickness or old age. Can. 1004: use of reason; repeatable (§2); CCC 1515 names a serious operation; in doubt the priest anoints (can. 1005); the unconscious who at least implicitly asked (can. 1006); not the obstinate in manifest grave sin (can. 1007; *Samaritanus Bonus* V.11, 2020, on persisting in a request for euthanasia, with anointing *sub condicione* for an unconscious person if an earlier sign allows the presumption of repentance). Not one in danger of death from another cause (battle, a voyage, execution: the Roman Catechism), who receives penance and viaticum.

**Effects:** CCC 1520-1523 (strength, union with Christ's passion, LG 11, preparation for the final journey); CCC 1532 (forgiveness where the sick person could not confess). Aquinas: the principal effect is the remnants of sin, a spiritual weakness, guilt going only consequently (Suppl. q.30 a.1); God heals the body only when the spiritual healing needs it (a.2): explanation.

**Level:** XIV cans. 1-4, **rung 1**; only priests anoint validly, **at least rung 2** (whether can. 4 already defined it is not settled, and on neither reading is it open); SC 73-74, LG 11, *Samaritanus Bonus* V.11, **rung 3**; Trent's chapters on the bishop's oil and the dying, taught, not canons, within the Church's power over the rite; Paul VI's constitution, the canons, *Ecclesiae de mysterio*, **law, no rung**; Aquinas, **no magisterial level**.

**Other side:** Calvin tied James's oil to a temporary gift of healing, now ceased (*Institutes* IV.19.18-19), stated and left standing ([Calvin on anointing](#calvin-on-anointing)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)

### Extreme unction

The West's name for the anointing, from the centuries when it was given almost only to the dying.

Technical in the West by the late twelfth century (the Roman Catechism: the last of the Church's anointings to be given). **Innocent I** to Decentius, *Si instituta ecclesiastica* (19 March 416), ch. 8 (lesson's translation): James concerns "the faithful who are sick, who can be anointed with the holy oil of chrism"; once the bishop has prepared it, "it is lawful not only for priests but for all Christians to use it for anointing, in their own need or that of their own"; it "cannot be poured on penitents, because it is a kind of sacrament" (*genus sacramenti*, not yet the technical term). It fixes no oil, form or deathbed. The lay clause has two readings: lay application was part of anointing the sick before the presbyters' rite was set apart; or (the older Catholic reading, the 1912 *Catholic Encyclopedia*) a separate devotional use of the blessed oil. **Pavia** (850): a great mystery that forgives sins and restores bodily health. The usual historians' account: Carolingian reformers bound the anointing to the priest's visit with penance and viaticum; penitents could not be anointed and penance was put off toward the deathbed, so the anointing followed it there. Aquinas: the last remedy, an immediate preparation for glory, for those whose sickness puts them in a state of departure (Suppl. q.32 a.2). **Florence** (1439, instruction): olive oil blessed by a bishop, only to the sick "whose death is feared", each of the senses anointed. **Trent** XIV ch. 3: "especially" the dying, "the sacrament of the departing". The Roman Catechism: "a very grievous sin" to defer it until life ebbs. **SC 73** (1963): "also and more fittingly" called anointing of the sick. CCC 1512: in the course of centuries given more and more exclusively to the dying; CCC 1523 still calls it the sacrament of those departing.

**Said plainly** ([4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)): for centuries the Latin Church gave it almost only to the dying, though James wrote of the sick and Trent wrote "especially". In 1611 the Holy Office judged "rash and next to error" the claim that oil not blessed by a bishop could validly serve; in 1842 it refused a parish priest the use of oil he blessed himself, even in necessity; can. 999 now lets any presbyter bless it in necessity. The usual account: validity needs oil blessed by one with power to bless, a power the Church can grant, as the Byzantine office of Holy Unction (sung by seven priests, who bless the oil within it; Hapgood 1906) assumes ([substance of the sacraments](#substance-of-the-sacraments)).

**Level:** Innocent's lay permission, **discipline**; Florence, instruction; "especially" (Trent ch. 3), taught, not a canon.

**Does not license:** "Vatican II abolished extreme unction" (it renamed it as more fitting; the doctrine stood), or "anointing is for the dying only" (over-correcting fails too: it is given even more rightly to those about to depart).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)

### Definitive teaching

Rung 2: truths the Church proposes to be held definitively, though not as divinely revealed. This course meets three such labels.

The ladder is [`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md)'s ([levels of authority](#levels-of-authority)). The CDF's *Doctrinal Commentary on the Concluding Formula of the Professio fidei* (29 June 1998) §11 illustrates the second paragraph with examples, without exhausting it. In this course:

| Teaching | Act | What the lessons say |
|---|---|---|
| Only priests (bishops and presbyters) anoint validly | CDF, Note on the Minister of the Sacrament of the Anointing of the Sick (11 February 2005), *definitive tenenda*; *Ecclesiae de mysterio* (1997) had said "theologically certain" | **at least rung 2**; whether Trent XIV can. 4 already defined it at rung 1 is not settled ([4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)) |
| Priestly ordination reserved to men | *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis* (1994) and the CDF responsum (1995) | **at least rung 2**; rung 1 against rung 2 disputed ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)) |
| Anglican orders null | *Apostolicae Curae* (1896), listed by the 1998 commentary among truths connected to revelation by historical necessity, held definitively | **rung 2** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)) |
| A ratified and consummated marriage cannot be dissolved by any human power | John Paul II, Address to the Roman Rota (21 January 2000) §8: to be held definitively, though not solemnly declared by a defining act | **at least rung 2** ([5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)) |

**Not the same as** a "dogmatic fact" (the 1998 commentary attaches that label to canonizations, not to *Apostolicae Curae*). Definitive is never "settled as rung 1" and never "open".

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md), [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

## The sacraments at the service of communion

Module 5: Holy Orders in three degrees, the reservation of priestly ordination and the separate diaconate question, matrimony, and what the Church can and cannot say about a failed marriage.

### Three degrees of orders

One sacrament in three degrees: the bishop has its fullness, the presbyter a share, the deacon is ordained not unto priesthood but unto ministry.

Every Christian priesthood is a share in Christ's ([`christology` 5.1](../christology/lessons/05-01-the-paschal-mystery-and-the-threefold-office.md)); the ministerial priesthood differs from the common "in essence and not in degree only" (LG 10; [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-03-the-laity-charisms-and-the-sensus-fidei.md)). **Trent, Session XXIII** (15 July 1563), ch. 1 argues from a visible sacrifice to a visible priesthood empowered to consecrate, offer and forgive. Its canons anathematize whoever reduces the priesthood to preaching (can. 1), denies that order is truly a sacrament instituted by Christ (can. 3), denies the character or says "that he who has once been a priest, can again become a layman" (can. 4), denies a hierarchy of bishops, priests and ministers by divine ordination (can. 6), or denies that bishops are superior to priests (can. 7). Ch. 2 lists the orders, greater and minor.

| Degree | Teaching |
|---|---|
| Bishop | the fullness of the sacrament (LG 21; [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-01-bishops-and-apostolic-succession.md)) |
| Presbyter | true priests who lack its highest degree (LG 28) |
| Deacon | hands laid *non ad sacerdotium, sed ad ministerium*, "not unto priesthood but unto ministry" (LG 29, quoting an ancient church order; lesson's translation), for the diakonia of liturgy, word and charity; a character (CCC 1554, 1570) |

*Sacerdos* names bishops and priests only, yet all three degrees are conferred by the one sacrament. Can. 1009 §3 ([in persona Christi capitis](#in-persona-christi-capitis)) gives the capacity to act in the person of Christ the Head to bishops and presbyters and the diakonia to deacons. The schoolmen's seven orders, each with a character (Suppl. q.35 a.2), are gone from the Latin Church: *Ministeria quaedam* (15 August 1972) suppressed the minor orders and the subdiaconate; *Spiritus Domini* (10 January 2021) opened the lector and acolyte to women as lay ministries rooted in baptism. **The permanent diaconate:** LG 29 allowed its restoration, open to married men of mature age; Paul VI's *Sacrum Diaconatus Ordinem* (18 June 1967) set the norms (conferences decide, with papal approval); a married candidate at thirty-five with his wife's consent (can. 1031 §2). The minister of orders is a consecrated bishop (can. 1012; CCC 1576).

**Three joints** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)): **order** (what the sacrament makes a man; permanent; what he *can* do), **office and faculty** (what he *may* do; for absolution and marriage validity too: [faculties](#faculties)), **discipline** (celibacy, ages, mandates; changeable law, which can still attach invalidity, as in the impediment of orders to marriage, can. 1087).

**Unresolved tension** (flagged, not adjudicated by any lesson): Trent XXIII can. 2 anathematizes denying "other orders, both greater and minor" as steps to the priesthood; *Ministeria quaedam* suppressed the minor orders, and Francis's letter to Ladaria (10 January 2021) calls 1972 an adaptation that does not surpass prior doctrine.

**Level:** XXIII cans. 1, 3, 4, 6, 7, **rung 1** (they do **not** define the episcopate as a sacramental degree); LG 10, 21, 28, 29 (the deacon's sacramental grace included), **at least rung 3**; can. 1009 §3, *Ministeria quaedam*, *Spiritus Domini*, *Sacrum Diaconatus Ordinem*, **law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "a deacon is a junior priest" (he cannot consecrate, absolve or anoint), or "the diaconate is a lay ministry" (it is a degree of the sacrament, with a character).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md); the diaconate question: [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### Sacramentum Ordinis

Pius XII's constitution of 1947: for all three degrees the only matter is the imposition of hands, the only form the words that determine it; the porrection is not needed "at least for the future".

Pius XII, apostolic constitution, 30 November 1947. Declared and decreed, for diaconate, presbyterate and episcopate, that the only matter is the imposition of hands and the only form the words determining it, which signify the power of order and the grace of the Spirit (§4). For priests the matter is the first, silent imposition of hands, not the final one with *Accipe Spiritum Sanctum: quorum remiseris*. The handing over of the instruments is not necessary for validity *saltem in posterum*, "at least for the future"; no retroactive force. **Reason** (§3): Rome always held Greek ordinations valid without the porrection, and Florence never required the Greeks to add it, so Christ did not require it; if the Church once required it, she may change what she established. §1 restates that the Church has no power over the sacraments' substance, which it glosses as what Christ himself, as revelation's sources show, established to be kept in the sign ([substance of the sacraments](#substance-of-the-sacraments)). **Paul VI, *Pontificalis Romani*** (18 June 1968): the same matter, and a new episcopal consecration prayer from the *Apostolic Tradition*, which it ascribes to Hippolytus (liturgical law). The anointing of hands and the handing over of paten and chalice remain as rites that express what was done (CCC 1573-1574).

**Said plainly** ([5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)): for centuries the Latin schools taught, and a council's instruction stated, that a priest is made by the handing over of chalice and paten, while Rome accepted Eastern ordinations that never used that rite. Pius XII did not say the schools had been wrong: he decided "at least for the future" and left the past unsettled.

**Level:** **no defining formula**; it fixes with supreme authority what validity requires, **binding law for validity, no doctrinal rung** (graded like *Divinae Consortium Naturae*); its doctrinal ground, that Christ did not require the porrection, **at least rung 3**. Calling it a rubric understates (validity turns on it); calling it a dogma, or a condemnation of the medieval rite, overstates.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)

### Porrection of the instruments

The handing over of the chalice with wine and the paten with bread, once named the priesthood's matter, set aside for validity in 1947, kept as an explanatory rite.

Florence, *Exsultate Deo* (22 November 1439; lesson's translation): "The sixth sacrament is that of order, whose matter is that by whose handing over the order is conferred: thus the presbyterate is handed on by the offering of the chalice with wine and of the paten with bread." Aquinas: "the priestly character is imprinted at the very giving of the chalice" (Suppl. q.37 a.5), with the blessing, imposition of hands and anointing as preparation; matter shows rather than causes the power (q.34 a.5). [*Sacramentum Ordinis*](#sacramentum-ordinis) (1947): not necessary for validity "at least for the future". It survives as an explanatory rite (CCC 1574): forget the chalice and the man is still a priest.

**The test case for the manner of institution** ([institution by Christ](#institution-by-christ)): read *in specie*, Christ fixed the imposition of hands, which never left the Latin rite, and the porrection was an addition; read *in genere*, Christ fixed the grace and power and left the sign to the Church. Trent's canon fits both.

**Level:** Florence's matter, a **practical instruction, changeable** ([1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)); Aquinas, **no magisterial level**. "Ordination valid only with chalice and paten" is false since 1947.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)

### Faculties

Order gives the power; a faculty authorizes its use, and for absolution and marriage it decides validity.

Valid absolution needs the faculty besides the power of orders (can. 966 §1), granted by the local ordinary (can. 969) or held by office (can. 968 §1), except in danger of death, when any priest absolves (can. 976). A Latin presbyter confirms validly only with a faculty (can. 882; [minister of confirmation](#minister-of-confirmation)). Delegation decides validity of assisting at marriage (cann. 1108, 1111; [canonical form](#canonical-form)). The Church supplies the faculty in common error or positive and probable doubt (can. 144 §2). Every cleric is incardinated (can. 265) and owes reverence and obedience to the pope and his own ordinary (can. 273; CCC 1567). A cleric who loses the clerical state may not exercise orders except under can. 976 (cann. 290-292), yet his ordination never becomes invalid (can. 290). Francis provided that those who confess to the Society of St Pius X's priests are absolved validly and licitly (*Misericordia et misera* 12, 20 November 2016): a grant presupposing their orders and supplying only the faculty; the DDF's note of 2 July 2026 judges confessions heard by Society priests invalid and marriages they assist invalid ([Ecclesia Dei](#ecclesia-dei)).

**Level:** **law, no rung**; the capacity-against-exercise line is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.1](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-01-bishops-and-apostolic-succession.md)'s.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md), [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md), [2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md), [1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)

### Celibacy

Latin discipline, not dogma: the priesthood's nature does not demand it, and the Church commends it as fitting.

*Presbyterorum Ordinis* 16: not demanded by the very nature of the priesthood (the early Church; Eastern married priests), commended as fitting in many ways. Can. 277 §1 makes it law. A married man is "simply impeded" from orders except the permanent diaconate (can. 1042 1°): liceity, not validity; dispensation reserved to the Apostolic See (can. 1047 §2 3°). Those in sacred orders invalidly attempt marriage (can. 1087), an impediment the Apostolic See dispenses (can. 1078 §2 1°). The East ordains married deacons and priests, takes bishops from celibates, and allows no marriage after ordination (CCC 1580). *Anglicanorum Coetibus* (4 November 2009, art. VI): an ordinariate may petition, case by case, the priestly ordination of married former Anglican ministers by derogation from can. 277 §1, celibacy remaining the rule. Celibacy as an ecclesiastical tradition: [`fundamental-theology` 3.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/03-04-tradition.md).

**Level:** the law, **no rung**; PO 16's teaching that it is fitting, **at least rung 3**.

**Does not license:** "celibacy is dogma" (overstates) or "a bare rule" (understates PO 16).

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)

### Apostolicae Curae

Leo XIII's apostolic letter of 1896 declaring Anglican ordinations "utterly void and wholly null", for defect of form and of intention.

Leo XIII, 13 September 1896: ordinations by the Anglican rite *irritas prorsus fuisse et esse, omninoque nullas*, "utterly void and wholly null" (lesson's translation), for defect of form and of intention in the Edwardine Ordinal. Its genre is an **apostolic letter**, not a bull (so the 1998 CDF commentary; Schaff's summary and older cards say "bull"). The archbishops of Canterbury and York replied in *Saepius Officio* (1897; Schaff misprints 1877), pointing to the Ordinal's language of sacrifice and to changes in Rome's own rites (Schaff's summary, *Creeds* vol. 2). The married former Anglican ministers ordained under *Anglicanorum Coetibus* are ordained, not received as priests ([celibacy](#celibacy)).

**Level:** the CDF's *Doctrinal Commentary on the Concluding Formula of the Professio fidei* (29 June 1998) §11 lists it among truths connected with revelation by historical necessity, to be held definitively: **rung 2**. Not a "dogmatic fact" (§11 attaches that label to canonizations).

**Argued in:** [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md) 4.3 (*Apostolicae Curae* against *Saepius Officio*); not argued here.

*Lessons:* [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)

### Ordinatio Sacerdotalis

John Paul II's apostolic letter of 1994: the Church has no authority whatsoever to confer priestly ordination on women, to be definitively held.

22 May 1994, §4: the Church has no authority whatsoever to confer priestly ordination on women, and this judgment is to be definitively held by all the Church's faithful; it concerns "the Church's divine constitution". The 1998 commentary (¶11): among truths to be held definitively; the pope did not intend a dogmatic definition; a future definition as revealed is not foreclosed. "No authority whatsoever", not "forbidden": who can receive priestly ordination is placed in the [substance](#substance-of-the-sacraments). The law: "A baptized male alone receives sacred ordination validly" (can. 1024); attempting to confer a sacred order on a woman, or to receive one, incurs automatic excommunication reserved to the Apostolic See, and a cleric may be dismissed (can. 1379 §3, revised 2021). Both canons cover every grade: for the priesthood they restate teaching, for the diaconate they are the law in force. An attempted ordination of a woman with the exact rite is **invalid by the recipient alone**.

**Level:** **at least rung 2**; rung 1 against rung 2 disputed ([`fundamental-theology` 4.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-04-the-ladder-of-doctrinal-authority.md) Ex 2), and whether the 1995 response's claim is established disputed ([CDF responsum of 1995](#cdf-responsum-of-1995)): never "settled", never "open".

**Does not license:** "a solemn definition" (overstates) or "a discipline the next pope could lift" (understates); "refuting the nuptial symbol refutes the teaching" (the symbol is fittingness; the teaching rests on Christ's act).

**Other side:** [communions that ordain women](#communions-that-ordain-women).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### CDF responsum of 1995

The CDF's reply that *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis*'s teaching belongs to the deposit of faith and was set forth infallibly by the ordinary and universal magisterium.

*Responsum ad dubium*, 28 October 1995 (signed Ratzinger and Bertone; approved by John Paul II): the teaching requires definitive assent, since, founded on the written word of God and constantly kept in Tradition, it was set forth infallibly by the ordinary and universal magisterium (LG 25). The unsigned CDF reflection: the papal letter, itself not infallible, witnesses to an already infallible teaching; reasons of fittingness (*ex convenientia*) are not strictly logical proofs.

**The two level objections** ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md), stated at reference level): (i) **rung 1 or rung 2**: rung 1 requires that the ordinary universal magisterium proposed it *as revealed*, which "written word of God" and "deposit of faith" suggest; rung 2 requires only that it is held definitively as *connected with* revelation, which OS's "held" and the 1998 placement suggest. (ii) **whether the response's claim is established**: a dicastery's reply is not itself infallible; Francis Sullivan (*Creative Fidelity*, 1996; [`fundamental-theology` 4.1](../fundamental-theology/lessons/04-01-the-magisterium.md)) held that the bishops' agreement in teaching it as definitive had not been shown, with can. 749 §3 asking that infallibility be manifest. Neither objection makes the teaching rung 3 or open; if (ii) were right, the 1995 claim would be unproven, not refuted, and whether that would lower the assent owed is itself disputed.

**Level:** a dicastery response with papal approval, **at least rung 2**.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### Inter Insigniores

The CDF's 1976 declaration on the question of admitting women to the priesthood: four arguments, then fittingness, with what it declines to claim.

CDF declaration dated 15 October 1976, approved by Paul VI. **Arguments:** (a) constant tradition, East and West (§1); (b) Christ called no woman to the Twelve, though he broke boldly with custom about women elsewhere, so the choice was not cultural conformity (§2); (c) the apostles, who broke with Mosaic practice, ordained none of Paul's women co-workers (§3); (d) the Church's power over the sacraments stops at their substance (§4). **Fittingness** (§5): the priest acts *in persona Christi*, Christ's image at the consecration; sacramental signs signify by natural resemblance; Christ is Bridegroom and the Church his Bride, so his role "must be taken by a man". Gal 3:28 concerns divine sonship, not ministries (§6). **Declines:** that §5 is a demonstration (it is by the analogy of faith); any superiority of men (function, not worth); that the old reasons were good (it concedes the Fathers' prejudices and scholastic arguments modern thought would "rightly reject", §1); that the Gospel facts are self-evident (they "do not make the matter immediately obvious", §2). It **says nothing of deaconesses**.

**Said plainly** ([5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)): some reasons the tradition once gave were bad; the Church holds the practice was faithful without defending every reason given for it.

**Level:** as an act, **rung 3**; its conclusion now held at least at rung 2 through OS; its reasons of fittingness are illustration, so rejecting them is not rejecting the teaching.

**Catholic objections, as their makers state them:** Phyllis Zagano (member of the 2016 commission): the ancient deaconesses were truly ordained; the 1976 Pontifical Biblical Commission report (never officially published; printed in *Origins* after a leak) reportedly found unanimously that the New Testament alone does not clearly settle the question; Karl Rahner: constant is not unchangeable, since a practice may stay unbroken because no one questioned the social order it mirrored; "the symbol proves too much" (why maleness, not other features?). Sara Butler (*The Catholic Priesthood and Women*) answers that the teaching stands on Christ's act as received.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### Deaconesses

Women deacons existed in parts of the early Church; what they were is the question.

Rom 16:1: "Phebe, our sister, who is in the ministry of the church" (DR); *diakonos*, servant or minister (lesson's gloss), for the ITC a recognized service, not the deacon's office. 1 Tim 3:11's "women": deaconesses or deacons' wives, disputed. The *Didascalia* (about 240): deaconesses anointed women at baptism, instructed and visited them. *Apostolic Constitutions* (about 380) VIII.19-20: the bishop lays hands on a deaconess with a prayer for the Spirit; VIII.28 denies her a deacon's functions. Nicaea can. 19: Paulianist deaconesses have "no imposition of hands" and are reckoned among the laity (text disputed; Percival notes a variant and calls the clause "most difficult of translation"). Chalcedon can. 15: "receive the laying on of hands as a deaconess" at forty, no marriage after (Percival; the ITC reads it as *cheirotonia*). Eighth-century Byzantium: hands laid on in the sanctuary during the liturgy, with the stole, as for a deacon (ITC 2002). The West shows none for five centuries. ITC 2002: deaconesses were "not purely and simply equivalent" to deacons.

**Level:** history, **no magisterial level**; the question it feeds is open ([female diaconate](#female-diaconate)).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### Female diaconate

Whether women can be ordained deacons is open: no act answers it, and the arguments about *Ordinatio Sacerdotalis* do not reach it.

ITC, *From the Diakonia of Christ to the Diakonia of the Apostles* (approved 30 September 2002): two indications, deaconesses not simply equivalent to deacons, and the unity of the sacrament of orders; the decision left to the magisterium. Francis set up commissions (2 August 2016, under Ladaria; 8 April 2020, under Petrocchi) and reserved the question to himself. Cardinal Petrocchi's summary letter to Leo XIV (18 September 2025, published 4 December 2025) reports a 2022 thesis passed 7-1: the state of research rules out moving toward women deacons as a grade of orders, a strong assessment that does not permit a definitive judgment like the one on priestly ordination. The arguments: "not unto priesthood but unto ministry" (LG 29) and can. 1009 §3 (the deacon's diakonia, not *in persona Christi capitis*) against the unity of the sacrament of orders. *Inter Insigniores* §5's symbol, as stated, does not reach deacons; the unity of orders may. Can. 1024 and can. 1379 §3 are the law in force for every grade. No deacon anoints, so the question does not touch anointing ([4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)).

**Level:** **open, rung 5**, its decision reserved to the magisterium; commissions and the 2025 letter, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not license:** "the 2025 letter closed it" (a commission is not magisterium, and its thesis declines a definitive judgment), or "OS settles it" (OS concerns priestly ordination).

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md)

### Matrimony

The one sacrament older than the Church: between two baptized people the marriage itself, the covenant they make, is the sign that causes.

Trent XXIV's doctrine opens with Adam pronouncing the bond perpetual and indissoluble (Gen 2:24) and Christ confirming it; Christ added grace "which might perfect that natural love", merited by his passion; Paul "intimates" it (Eph 5:32, "This is a great sacrament: but I speak in Christ and in the church", DR; Greek *mega mystērion*; the verse alone settles nothing: [mysterion and sacramentum](#mysterion-and-sacramentum)). Florence names the sign, the union of Christ and the Church, and lists Augustine's three goods (offspring, fidelity, and indivisibility where Augustine has *sacramentum*). **Contract and sacrament are one thing between the baptized:** Pius IX's *Syllabus* (prop. 73) condemned the separation; Leo XIII, *Arcanum* (10 February 1880) §23 (lesson's translation): "marriage is the contract itself, if only it is lawfully made. … Every lawful marriage between Christians is in itself and by itself a sacrament"; can. 1055 §2; CCEO can. 776 §2. So two baptized Protestants marrying before a registrar, not bound to form (can. 1117), receive the sacrament. **Effects:** a bond, perpetual and exclusive by nature; in Christian marriage a special sacrament strengthens and as it were consecrates the spouses for their state (can. 1134). The bond is the *res et sacramentum* (Suppl. q.42 a.3 ad 2; [threefold analysis](#threefold-analysis)).

**The exception clause** (Matt 19:9, "except it be for fornication", DR; *porneia*, sexual wrongdoing, lesson's gloss), three readings: **unlawful union** within forbidden degrees, never a marriage (Joseph Fitzmyer, *Theological Studies*, 1976, from Qumran texts); **separation without remarriage** (Jerome, Letter 77.3: the wife put away "must remain unmarried"; Augustine, *On Adulterous Marriages*; Florence: separation of bed lawful for fornication, another marriage not, "since the bond of a lawfully contracted marriage is perpetual"); **dissolution** (the Reformed confessions; Orthodox practice by economy).

**Level:** one of the seven, conferring grace, **rung 1** (XXIV can. 1); inseparability of contract and sacrament, **at least rung 3**; the doctrinal chapter and the grace of state, **at least rung 3**. Details: [Trent Session XXIV](#trent-session-xxiv), [ends and properties of marriage](#ends-and-properties-of-marriage), [minister of matrimony](#minister-of-matrimony), [canonical form](#canonical-form), [impediments](#impediments), [mixed marriages](#mixed-marriages). Sexual ethics: [`moral-theology` 6.1](../moral-theology/lessons/06-01-the-theology-of-the-body-and-chastity.md).

**Other side:** [Orthodox crowning](#orthodox-crowning), [Westminster Confession on marriage](#westminster-confession-on-marriage), [oikonomia](#oikonomia).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md)

### Trent Session XXIV

11 November 1563: the doctrine on matrimony, twelve canons, and the reform decree *Tametsi*.

| Canon | Anathema on whoever says |
|---|---|
| 1 | matrimony is not truly and properly one of the seven, instituted by Christ, but "invented by men in the Church", conferring no grace |
| 2 | Christians may have several wives at once |
| 3, 4 | only the degrees in Leviticus impede; the Church could not establish nullifying impediments or dispense in some |
| 5 | the bond can be dissolved for heresy, irksome cohabitation or desertion |
| 6 | a ratified, non-consummated marriage is not dissolved by solemn religious profession |
| 7 | the Church errs in teaching that the bond is not dissolved by adultery, and that neither party, not even the innocent, may marry again while the other lives |
| 8 | the Church errs in decreeing separation for many causes |
| 9 | clerics in sacred orders or the solemnly professed can marry |
| 10 | marriage is to be preferred to virginity or celibacy |
| 11 | the prohibition of solemnities at certain times is superstition |
| 12 | matrimonial causes do not belong to ecclesiastical judges |

**Reform decree, ch. 1 (*Tametsi*):** marriage before the parish priest and two or three witnesses a condition of validity where published; clandestine marriages before it were true marriages ([canonical form](#canonical-form)).

**Said plainly** ([5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)): canon 7 was reworded at a secular government's request. The July 1563 draft anathematized whoever said adultery dissolves a marriage; in August Venice's ambassadors pleaded for the Greeks of its territories, who allowed remarriage after adultery; the canon as passed anathematizes whoever says the Church errs. What the change meant is disputed: Piet Fransen's 1947 thesis, followed by Giancarlo Pani (*La Civiltà Cattolica*, 2014), holds that the Fathers declined to define indissolubility as of faith; E. Christian Brugger (*Public Discourse*, 2014) holds that they held it so and spared the Greeks as a pastoral accommodation.

**Level:** the doctrinal canons (1, 2, 3-4, 5, 10), **rung 1**; can. 6, **rung 1** for what it defines, though the 1983 Code names only papal dispensation for dissolving a non-consummated marriage (can. 1142); can. 7, **rung 1 as worded**, whether it defined indissolubility as revealed disputed; cans. 8, 9, 11, 12 define the Church's authority (separation, vowed persons' marriages, rite and times, marriage cases), not the permanence of any law; the doctrinal chapter, **at least rung 3**; *Tametsi*, **law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "canon 7 solemnly defined indissolubility" (overstates what is settled about the canon) or "its indirect wording left remarriage after adultery open" (understates).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md); *Tametsi* close-read in [`church-history` 6.4](../church-history/lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)

### Ends and properties of marriage

Ordered to the good of the spouses and to children; one and indissoluble, with a special firmness in Christian marriage.

Can. 1055 §1 (after GS 48): a partnership of the whole of life, ordered by its nature to the good of the spouses and to the procreation and education of children. Can. 1056: unity and indissolubility, which in Christian marriage obtain a special firmness by reason of the sacrament. The 1917 Code (can. 1013 §1) ranked procreation and education primary, mutual help and the remedy of concupiscence secondary (Pius XI, *Casti Connubii*, 1930, §17); GS 48-50 and the 1983 Code do not rank and repudiate nothing; how much changed is argued. Indissolubility belongs to every marriage, natural ones included, and is **absolute** for a ratified and consummated marriage: no human power, the pope's included, and no cause except death dissolves it (can. 1141; CCC 1640). John Paul II, Address to the Roman Rota (21 January 2000): §6, "not even by the power of the Roman Pontiff"; §8, to be held definitively, though not solemnly declared by a defining act.

**Level:** the ends, **at least rung 3**; absolute indissolubility of *ratum et consummatum*, **at least rung 2**, never "solemnly defined", never open ([definitive teaching](#definitive-teaching)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Minister of matrimony

Consent makes the marriage; in the Latin Church the spouses are commonly understood as its ministers, in the East the priest who crowns them.

Consent lawfully manifested makes marriage, and no human power can supply it (can. 1057 §1); Florence calls mutual consent expressed by words of the present the efficient cause "as a rule" (*regulariter*), and the Roman Catechism repeats it. Latin: it is "ordinarily understood" that the spouses confer the sacrament on each other (CCC 1623); the cleric asks for and receives their consent in the Church's name (can. 1108 §2). East: the minister is the priest who crowns them after receiving their consent (CCC 1623); Eastern Catholic law makes the rite sacred "by the very intervention of a priest who assists and blesses" (CCEO can. 828 §2, lesson's translation), required for validity. Aquinas: the words of consent are the form, "not the priest's blessing, which is a sacramental" (Suppl. q.42 a.1 ad 1). One Church, two received theologies of the minister: the covenant itself as the sign, or the joining as God's act through the Church's prayer. The law decides validity, not which theology is right: since 2016 only a priest validly assists where a party is Eastern (can. 1108 §3).

**Level:** the spouses as ministers, **rung 4**, Latin common teaching, reported beside the Eastern view; matter and form of consent, **no magisterial level**; the canons, law.

**Does not license:** "the priest marries the couple" (in the Latin Church he is the qualified witness), or forgetting that in the Eastern churches, Catholic included, the priest's blessing is required for validity.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)

### Canonical form

The Church cannot supply consent, but she sets the conditions under which her members' consent is valid: before a qualified cleric and two witnesses.

Before 1563 clandestine marriages were true marriages. *Tametsi* (Trent XXIV, reform decree ch. 1): the parish priest and two or three witnesses for validity, where published; *Ne temere* (1907, in force 1908) made the form universal ([`church-history` 6.4](../church-history/lessons/06-04-trent-and-catholic-reform.md)). Now: can. 1108 §1, the local ordinary, pastor, or a delegated priest or deacon assists, with two witnesses; form binds whenever one party was baptized in or received into the Catholic Church (can. 1117); dispensable in a mixed marriage (can. 1127 §2, extended to disparity of cult by can. 1129); witnesses alone in danger of death or a month's foreseen absence of an assistant (can. 1116). **Since *De concordia inter Codices* (31 May 2016):** only a priest validly assists at a marriage between Eastern parties or between a Latin and an Eastern party (can. 1108 §3), and delegation is subject to it (can. 1111 §1); a Catholic marrying an Eastern non-Catholic is bound to form for liceity only, a priest's intervention sufficing for validity (can. 1127 §1). Place is a matter of liceity (can. 1118). Defect of form is a ground of nullity ([grounds of nullity](#grounds-of-nullity)).

**Level:** **ecclesiastical law, no rung**, universal only from 1908, and dispensable; that the Church can make such conditions, **rung 1** (XXIV cans. 3-4, 12).

**Does not license:** "canonical form is divine law".

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Impediments

Conditions that make a person unable to marry validly: some of divine law and never dispensed, most ecclesiastical and dispensable.

Trent XXIV cans. 3-4: the Church can establish diriment impediments and dispense in some. Divine-law impediments, such as a prior bond or consanguinity in the direct line, are never dispensed, and only the supreme authority declares what divine law forbids (can. 1075). Ecclesiastical impediments are dispensable by the local ordinary except reserved ones (can. 1078; sacred orders, public perpetual vow of chastity in a pontifical institute, and crime reserved to the Apostolic See, §2). The list (cann. 1083-1094): age, impotence, prior bond, disparity of cult, sacred orders, public perpetual vow, abduction, crime, consanguinity, affinity, public propriety, adoption. Ecclesiastical impediments bind Catholics; disparity of cult binds only when one party is Catholic (can. 1086 §1). The Orthodox absolute impediments (an undissolved marriage, a third marriage) are [oikonomia](#oikonomia)'s.

**Level:** the Church's power, **rung 1**; the particular impediments, **law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md), [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Mixed marriages

Permission decides liceity, dispensation validity, baptism sacramentality.

Catholic and baptized non-Catholic: express permission of the competent authority, for liceity (can. 1124), with the conditions and promises of can. 1125; a sacrament (can. 1055 §2). **Disparity of cult** (Catholic and unbaptized): a dispensation for validity (can. 1086), after the promises, of which the other party is informed (can. 1125); a valid **natural** marriage, not a sacrament, since the covenant is raised "between the baptized" (can. 1055 §1; common teaching); still a perpetual and exclusive bond that neither spouse can end (cann. 1056, 1134). Catholic and Eastern non-Catholic: form for liceity only, a priest's intervention for validity (can. 1127 §1). CCC 1633-1637. Dissolution in favour of the faith is possible for a non-sacramental bond ([privilege of the faith](#privilege-of-the-faith)).

**Level:** **law, no rung**; that a marriage with an unbaptized party is no sacrament, common teaching.

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)

### Declaration of nullity

A judgement that no marriage ever came to be: it ends nothing.

CCC 1629; validity is presumed until the contrary is proven (can. 1060), with moral certainty. An invalid marriage entered in good faith by at least one party is **putative** (can. 1061 §3), and its children are legitimate (can. 1137). Keep apart: **nullity** (there never was a bond), **dissolution** (a real bond ended, possible only for one not both sacramental and consummated), **civil divorce** (civil effects only, can. 1059; a valid bond untouched, CCC 2382; tolerable for legal rights, children or inheritance, CCC 2383). *Mitis Iudex Dominus Iesus* (motu proprio, 15 August 2015, in force 8 December 2015) rewrote the process, not the grounds: one sentence for nullity suffices without a second conforming one (criterion I); the diocesan bishop judges a **briefer process** when both spouses petition, or one with the other's consent, and the nullity is manifest (can. 1683); the text favours "not the nullity of marriages, but the speed of processes" and admits the briefer process could endanger indissolubility (criterion IV); defenders cite access and cost, critics the lost second review and the risk of reading Art. 14's examples as grounds. Henry VIII sought a nullity, not a divorce; Clement VII ruled the marriage valid in March 1534 ([`church-history` 6.3](../church-history/lessons/06-03-the-english-reformations.md)).

**Level:** **law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "annulment is a Catholic divorce"; "a declaration says the marriage failed" (it says something was missing on the day); "*Mitis Iudex* added grounds" (it changed who judges and how fast).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Grounds of nullity

Three heads, all about the moment of consent; later breakdown is at most evidence.

| Head | Canons | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Impediment | 1083-1094 | age, impotence, prior bond, disparity of cult, sacred orders, public perpetual vow, abduction, crime, consanguinity, affinity, public propriety, adoption |
| Defective consent | 1095-1103 | lack of sufficient use of reason; grave defect of discretion; inability to assume the essential obligations for causes of a psychic nature (1095); error of person (1097); fraud (1098); simulation, excluding marriage or an essential element or property by a positive act of will (1101 §2); a future condition (1102); force or grave fear (1103) |
| Defect of form | 1108 (as amended 2016), 1117 | no qualified assistant or witnesses where form binds |

A ground is a proved fact about the wedding; consent is presumed to match the words (can. 1101 §1).

**Level:** **law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Pauline privilege

A marriage of two unbaptized persons is dissolved in favour of the one baptized if the other departs: a real marriage ended, not annulled.

1 Cor 7:15: "But if the unbeliever depart, let him depart. For a brother or sister is not under servitude in such cases" (DR). Cann. 1143-1146: the unbaptized party departs (will not cohabit peaceably), established by the interpellations; the bond ends **when the baptized party contracts a new marriage** (can. 1143 §1), not at baptism, by the law itself with no recourse to higher authority (CDF 2001 norms, preface). It needs two unbaptized spouses at the wedding; where one was baptized, the [privilege of the faith](#privilege-of-the-faith) is the route.

**Level:** **law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "the Pauline privilege annuls the first marriage" or "baptism dissolves it".

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Privilege of the faith

The pope may dissolve a marriage with at least one unbaptized party, in favour of the faith; the absolute limit is a ratified and consummated marriage.

CDF norms *Potestas Ecclesiae* (approved 16 February 2001, dated 30 April 2001): art. 1, the Roman Pontiff may dissolve a marriage in which at least one party is unbaptized, provided it was not consummated after both were baptized; art. 4, common life cannot be restored and the petitioner was not exclusively or predominantly to blame; art. 5, declarations. **Ratified, non-consummated:** the pope dissolves for a just cause (can. 1142); Trent XXIV can. 6's route, solemn religious profession, is not in the 1983 Code. **The limit:** a marriage *ratum et consummatum* "can be dissolved by no human power and by no cause, except death" (can. 1141; CCC 1640). One baptism moves the line: once both are baptized the marriage is a sacrament, consummation after it is presumed (can. 1061 §2), and the bond is beyond any power.

**Said plainly** ([5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)): the Church dissolves real marriages, by her law or the pope's act; the 2001 preface records that the Holy Office kept its 1934 instruction on such cases out of the *Acta Apostolicae Sedis* for fear of seeming to favour divorce.

**Level:** the norms, **law, no rung**; the absolute limit, **at least rung 2** (John Paul II to the Rota, 2000, §8).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Familiaris Consortio 84

John Paul II on the divorced and civilly remarried: reaffirmed the practice of not admitting them to communion, because their state objectively contradicts what the Eucharist signifies.

*Familiaris Consortio* (apostolic exhortation, 22 November 1981) §84: pastors are to discern situations; the Church reaffirms her practice, "based upon Sacred Scripture", of not admitting the divorced and remarried to communion, because their state and condition objectively contradict the union of love between Christ and the Church signified and effected by the Eucharist, and because admission would lead the faithful into confusion about indissolubility; absolution is open to those who, unable to separate for serious reasons, undertake complete continence. FC 83: the divorced not remarried face no obstacle. **CDF letter** (14 September 1994, published by papal order): conscience cannot settle the existence of a prior marriage, a public, external-forum question (§§7-9). **Pontifical Council for Legislative Texts** (24 June 2000) on can. 915: "manifest grave sin" read objectively; the prohibition derives from divine law. The bar rested on the public state, not a verdict on the soul.

**Level:** FC and FC 84's practice, **rung 3**, resting on higher doctrine (indissolubility; worthiness for communion); the 1994 letter, **rung 3**; the 2000 declaration interprets a canon, **no rung of its own**.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Amoris Laetitia chapter 8

Francis's chapter on discernment of particular cases: no new general rule, but a rule's consequences need not always be the same, "also … with regard to sacramental discipline".

*Amoris Laetitia* (post-synodal apostolic exhortation, 19 March 2016), after the synods of October 2014 and October 2015 (the 2015 final report, §§84-86, never names communion). Ch. 8: no new general rules, discernment of particular cases (300); responsibility differs, so a rule's consequences need not always be the same, "also … with regard to sacramental discipline" (n. 336); the Church's help can in certain cases include the sacraments (305, n. 351); indissolubility untouched (298). Four cardinals' *dubia* (19 September 2016, made public 14 November 2016) asked first whether this is now possible without FC 84's conditions; no public reply. Application: the [Buenos Aires criteria](#buenos-aires-criteria).

**Two readings, each at its strength** ([5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)): *development* (the Buenos Aires bishops, Francis, the DDF): FC 84 already admitted some, and AL applies the old distinction between an objective situation and its imputability (301-302; CCC 1735) to the discipline that applies the rule; *rupture* (the four cardinals' explanatory note): FC 84 rested on a public state contradicting what the Eucharist signifies, which diminished imputability leaves unchanged, and the 1994 and 2000 texts call the rule unmodifiable and of divine law. Both keep can. 1141; they divide over whether FC 84's rule follows from the objective state or applies discipline to persons.

**Level:** AL, **rung 3** (as [`moral-theology` 2.5](../moral-theology/lessons/02-05-the-primacy-of-conscience-rightly-understood.md) grades it; the AL 303 conscience crux is that lesson's); development against rupture, **freely disputed (rung 5)**; the *dubia*, petitions, no level.

**Does not license:** "AL defined a new doctrine" or "AL is only advice".

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Buenos Aires criteria

The Argentine bishops' application of *Amoris Laetitia* ch. 8, which Francis called the only interpretation and a rescript made authentic magisterium.

Bishops of the Buenos Aires pastoral region, *Criterios básicos para la aplicación del capítulo VIII de Amoris laetitia* (5 September 2016): no "permissions" (criterion 1); continence proposed where possible (5); where it is not feasible and nullity cannot be obtained, a discernment finding diminished responsibility "opens the possibility" of reconciliation and the Eucharist (6, citing AL nn. 336, 351); no unrestricted access (7). Francis's letter of the same day: *no hay otras interpretaciones*, "there are no other interpretations" (lesson's gloss). A rescript *ex audientia* of 5 June 2017 (Parolin) ordered both published *velut Magisterium authenticum*, "as authentic magisterium" (lesson's translation); printed in *Acta Apostolicae Sedis* 108 (2016) 1071-1074. The DDF's reply to Cardinal Duka, approved 25 September 2023, repeats it.

**Level:** **rung 3**, by the rescript; defines nothing, leaves can. 1141 intact.

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

## The liturgy

Module 6: what the liturgy is before what it looks like; the year, the week and the day; and what a rite is, Roman and Eastern.

### Leitourgia

A public work: the word carries both a service done for the people and a priestly service, and applies first to Christ.

*Laos*, people, and *ergon*, work: "public work" (lesson's gloss; CCC 1069). In classical Athens a service a wealthy citizen performed for the city at his own cost, such as equipping a warship. The Greek Old Testament uses it for the service of priests and Levites at the sanctuary; Luke for Zachary's "days of his office" (Luke 1:23, DR). Hebrews gives it to Christ: "a minister of the holies" (*leitourgos*, Heb 8:2, DR), who "hath obtained a better ministry" (*leitourgia*, 8:6). CCC 1069-1070. An Athenian liturgy was a rich man's gift; the Christian one is the service of the high priest "always living to make intercession" (Heb 7:25), which his people share.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Liturgy

The public worship Christ as Head renders the Father with his Body: an exercise of his priestly office in signs that signify and effect sanctification.

*Mediator Dei* (1947) §20: the public worship our Redeemer as Head renders the Father, together with the worship the community renders through him: the worship of the Mystical Body "in the entirety of its Head and members". *Sacrosanctum Concilium* 7 takes it over: an exercise of Christ's priestly office, in which signs perceptible to the senses signify and effect sanctification, each in its own way; the whole public worship carried out by Head and members. **Christ's presences** (SC 7): in the sacrifice of the Mass, in his minister and "especially" under the eucharistic species; by his power in the sacraments, so that "when anyone baptizes, Christ baptizes"; in his word read in the Church; when the Church prays and sings. MD 20 had listed altar, sacraments and prayer; the council added the word. So no other action of the Church equals a liturgical celebration's efficacy by the same title and degree (SC 7). Can. 834 §1 restates it as law. The liturgy does not exhaust the Church's activity (SC 9) and is its summit and font (SC 10: [source and summit](#source-and-summit)); regulation belongs to the Church's authority alone ([substance of the sacraments](#substance-of-the-sacraments)). [1.1](lessons/01-01-what-a-sacrament-is.md)'s sign that causes, widened from seven rites to the whole of public worship.

**Level:** where SC 7 restates defined doctrine it carries that weight (the substantial presence, **rung 1**); its doctrinal teaching, **at least rung 3**; MD 20, **rung 3**; can. 834, law. SC adopted no account of how the mysteries are present.

**Not the same as:** Durkheim's rite, which periodically re-creates a group's unity ([`social-theory` 3.3](../social-theory/lessons/03-03-religion-as-social-the-elementary-forms.md)): a description from outside answering a different question.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Sacrosanctum Concilium

Vatican II's Constitution on the Sacred Liturgy (1963): doctrine at least rung 3, norms that are law.

Promulgated 4 December 1963 (2,147 to 4; the event is [`church-history` 9.2](../church-history/lessons/09-02-the-second-vatican-council.md)'s). A plain **Constitution**, neither "dogmatic" nor "pastoral"; it defines nothing new and left the revision itself to others ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)).

| Articles | Content |
|---|---|
| 4 | all lawfully recognized rites "of equal right and dignity" ([rite](#rite)) |
| 5-13 | doctrine: Christ's priestly act (7); the liturgy does not exhaust the Church's activity (9); summit and font (10); dispositions (11); spiritual life not confined to it (12); devotions commended if they harmonize with the seasons, derive from and lead to the liturgy (13) |
| 14 | [participation](#active-participation), *plenam, consciam atque actuosam*, the aim "before all else" |
| 19, 28, 30, 48 | participation internal and external; each his own part; acclamations, gestures, silence; offering the Victim through and with the priest |
| 21-23 | general restoration: immutable, divinely instituted elements and changeable ones (21); regulation by the Apostolic See and, as law determines, bishops and conferences, and no one else, "not even a priest" (22 §3); sound tradition kept, legitimate progress, no innovations unless the Church's good genuinely and certainly requires them, new forms to "grow organically from forms already existing" (23) |
| 34, 36, 37-40 | noble simplicity (34); Latin preserved, the vernacular may be extended (36); adaptation ([inculturation](#inculturation)) |
| 43 | the liturgical movement a sign of providence and a movement of the Spirit |
| 50, 51, 54, 55 | the Order of Mass revised, substance kept, duplications and low-value additions discarded, lost elements restored "to the norm of the holy Fathers" (50); Scripture "more lavishly" over a cycle of years (51); the vernacular first for readings and common prayer, the faithful also able to say or sing their parts of the Ordinary in Latin (54); the cup reopened (55) |
| 64, 71, 73-74 | the adult catechumenate restored; confirmation's renewal of promises; anointing of the sick and the continuous rite |
| 83-84, 89, 100, 102-111 | the Hours; the year, Sunday, the calendar |
| 112-121 | [sacred music](#sacred-music) |
| 128 | revision of the laws on building altars and churches; nothing on the celebrant's orientation |
| appendix | no objection to a fixed Sunday for Easter if the separated Churches assent |

SC 14's Latin has three adjectives; the vatican.va English, "fully conscious, and active", drops "full". SC 106's vatican.va English says Sunday is kept "every eighth day"; CCC 1166's English quotes it as "every seventh day".

**Level:** doctrinal teaching (SC 5-13: Christ's priestly act, summit and font, participation as a baptismal right and duty), **at least rung 3**, asking religious submission; restated defined points keep their own level; the general norms (SC 21-40 and the rest), **law, no rung, reformable**.

**Does not license:** "SC binds as dogma" (its norms are law) or "SC is only pastoral, so optional" (it is not a pastoral constitution, and its doctrine is at least rung 3).

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md), [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Mediator Dei

Pius XII's encyclical on the liturgy (1947): the first papal document on its theology, welcoming the liturgical movement and fencing it.

20 November 1947. §20 the definition ([liturgy](#liturgy)); §4 credits the movement particularly to Benedictine monasteries. **Corrections:** the liturgy is neither ornamental ceremonial nor a list of rubrics (§25); "objective" piety may not belittle personal devotion (§§28-32); antiquity is no warrant by itself, and insisting on a table-form altar or banning black vestments revives Pistoia's "exaggerated and senseless antiquarianism" (§§61-64); the faithful offer with the priest, by his hands, but hold no priestly power (§§82-84, 92-93); Christ's mysteries are present in the year, but not "in a vague and uncertain way as some modern writers hold" (§165; it names no one). **Teachings used across the course:** *ex opere operato* and *ex opere operantis* not rivals (§36); sacramentals *ex opere operantis Ecclesiae* (§27); fruit depends on disposition (§31); human and divine elements (§50); authority over the liturgy (§58); the vernacular only by the Holy See's permission (§§59-60, a discipline since changed); participation the faithful's chief duty (§80); other methods for those unable to follow the rite (§108); the separate species signify the separation (§70) and communion belongs to the sacrifice's integrity (§115); the laity not obliged to the Office (§150); *lex orandi* (§§46-48).

**Level:** an encyclical, **rung 3** for its teaching; its disciplinary directions, **no rung**, and some have changed.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md); also [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md), [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Liturgical movement

The nineteenth- and twentieth-century revival behind *Mediator Dei* and SC: monks, a pope's watchword, and theologians of the mystery.

Prosper Guéranger (d. 1875) restored monastic life in France at Solesmes (*Directory on Popular Piety* §44). Pius X gave the watchword "active participation" in 1903 (*Tra le sollecitudini*; [`church-history` 9.3](../church-history/lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)). Lambert Beauduin (d. 1960), a monk of Mont César at Louvain, gave it a pastoral programme at the Malines congress of September 1909. Odo Casel (d. 1948) of Maria Laach built a theology of Christ's saving acts made present in worship (*Das christliche Kultmysterium*, 1932). Pius Parsch (d. 1954), an Augustinian canon of Klosterneuburg, brought Bible and liturgy into a parish church (St Gertrude's) from 1919 (DPP §46 n. 39). *Mediator Dei* §4 credits the revival particularly to Benedictine monasteries and §165 warns, naming no one, against a "vague and uncertain" presence of the mysteries. SC 43: a sign of the providential dispositions of God and a movement of the Holy Spirit.

**Level:** SC 43 praises the movement, **not any theory**: Casel's and the movement's theories have **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Lex orandi lex credendi

The rule of prayer and the rule of belief: the Church's universal prayer witnesses to her faith, and she regulates that prayer.

The tag condenses a fifth-century chapter of the *Indiculus* (the collection of papal authorities on grace transmitted with Celestine I's letter of 431, **usually attributed** to Prosper of Aquitaine; ch. 8, printed as "Cap. 11" in the old Denzinger): *ut legem credendi lex statuat supplicandi*, "that the law of supplication may establish the law of believing" (lesson's translation). Its argument is narrow and concerns grace: the Church's apostolic, universal intercessions for unbelievers, idolaters, Jews, heretics, schismatics, the lapsed and catechumens show that faith is God's gift. *Mediator Dei* §§46-48: the liturgy professes the faith and supplies weighty evidence (Pius IX argued from it for the Immaculate Conception), but it does not decide the faith "independently" of the magisterium, and in general the rule runs the other way too, *lex credendi legem statuat supplicandi*. CCC 1124. A proper locus ([`fundamental-theology` 5.5](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-05-the-loci-theologici.md)), not an independent court; and since the Church regulates her prayer, the *lex orandi* is law as well as evidence ([`trinity-and-god` 2.4](../trinity-and-god/lessons/02-04-from-confession-to-creed.md), [`trinity-and-god` 6.2](../trinity-and-god/lessons/06-02-the-trinity-in-the-economy-liturgy-and-prayer.md)). Summorum Pontificum and Traditionis Custodes use "lex orandi" juridically ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)).

**Level:** a rule of theological method, **no level of its own**.

**Does not license:** letting any liturgical text settle doctrine; quoting the four-word tag as the chapter's own wording.

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Active participation

SC 14's *plenam, consciam atque actuosam*: the baptized offering themselves with Christ through the rite, inwardly and outwardly; neither a job nor a mood.

SC 14: the Church desires all the faithful to be led to full, conscious and active participation, which the liturgy's nature demands and which is their right and duty by baptism (1 Pet 2:9); in the reform the aim to be considered "before all else", the "primary and indispensable source" of the Christian spirit (echoing Pius X, 1903). Content: internal and external (SC 19); minds attuned to voices (SC 11); acclamations, responses, songs, gestures, postures and, at the proper times, reverent silence (SC 30); each doing all and only his own part (SC 28); at the core, offering the Victim through the priest's hands and with him, and learning to offer oneself (SC 48; *Mediator Dei* §80). The Eucharistic Prayer is by nature the priest's alone; the people associate themselves in faith, silence and acclamations (GIRM 147); joint recitation of it by the faithful is an abuse (*Redemptionis Sacramentum* 52); the faithful have no priestly power (MD 82-84). *Mediator Dei* §108 allowed those unable to follow the rite to pray in harmony with it; once the rite is intelligible, whether that describes participation or only its floor is the strain ([6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md) Ex 2). *Ars celebrandi* is the best way to ensure *actuosa participatio* (*Sacramentum Caritatis* 38; [ars celebrandi](#ars-celebrandi)).

**Level:** **at least rung 3** (SC's doctrinal teaching); GIRM and RS, law and instruction, no rung.

**Does not license:** "participation means activity" or "a rota of jobs" (SC 30 counts silence; SC 28 limits each to his part), or "purely interior, so it means nothing".

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Source and summit

Two texts, two subjects: SC 10 calls the liturgy the summit and font of the Church's activity; LG 11 calls the Eucharistic sacrifice the fount and apex of the whole Christian life.

**SC 10** (of the **liturgy**): the summit (*culmen*) toward which the Church's activity tends and the font (*fons*) from which all her power flows; vatican.va English "summit ... font". **LG 11** (of the **Eucharistic sacrifice**): "fount and apex of the whole Christian life" (vatican.va English); "source and summit" is CCC 1324's rendering of LG 11. Around SC 10: the liturgy does not exhaust the Church's activity, since faith and conversion precede it and preaching, charity and the apostolate go alongside (SC 9); the spiritual life is not confined to it (SC 12); devotions derive from and lead to it (SC 13). A claim about direction, not a ranking that makes the rest optional.

**Level:** **at least rung 3**.

**Does not license:** "'source and summit' is SC 10's phrase about the liturgy".

*Lessons:* [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Liturgical year

One paschal mystery unfolded over time: Sunday first, then Easter, then everything arranged around Easter.

Each week, on the Lord's day, the Church keeps the Resurrection; once a year with the Passion at Easter; across the year the whole mystery of Christ, from the Incarnation to the waiting for his return (SC 102; *Universal Norms on the Liturgical Year*, GNLY, 1). Recalling them, the Church opens the riches of Christ's powers and merits, so that the mysteries are "in some way made present" in every age (SC 102); *Mediator Dei* §165: no cold record of the past but Christ living in his Church. **Sunday:** "in the spirit on the Lord's day" (Rev 1:10, DR); Ignatius on those "no longer observing the Sabbath, but living in the observance of the Lord's Day, on which also our life has sprung up again by Him" (*Magnesians* 9, shorter recension, ANF 1; the "queen and chief of all the days" is the longer, interpolated recension); Justin's two reasons, creation's first day and the Resurrection (*First Apology* 67); SC 106, the original feast day, every eighth day by apostolic tradition; *Dies Domini* (John Paul II, 31 May 1998) §19, "the weekly Easter". Only solemnities and feasts of the Lord displace a Sunday, and nothing a Sunday of Advent, Lent or Easter (GNLY 4-5). **The Mass obligation:** can. 1247; satisfied by a Mass in any Catholic rite, on the day or the evening before (can. 1248 §1); the 1917 Code first made it universal law; understood as grave (*Dies Domini* 47, citing the Catechism). Can. 1246 §1 adds ten holy days; §2 lets a conference, with Rome's prior approval, suppress some or move them to Sunday (GNLY 7: Epiphany, Ascension, Corpus Christi). The year begins on the First Sunday of Advent, the Sunday on or nearest 30 November (GNLY 40). Seasons: [temporal and sanctoral cycles](#temporal-and-sanctoral-cycles); readings: [lectionary](#lectionary).

**Level:** SC 102, 106, **at least rung 3**; *how* the mysteries are present, **theology, no magisterial level** (Casel's mystery-presence is one account: [how the one sacrifice is present](#how-the-one-sacrifice-is-present)); can. 1247, **law, no rung**, its gravity **rung 3**; the calendar, **law**.

**Does not license:** "the Sunday obligation is only a custom now" (understates), or "moving the Ascension to Sunday changed the faith" (overstates).

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Date of Easter

The Sunday after the paschal full moon: between 22 March and 25 April. Nicaea fixed one day for all, not a method.

The rule the West settled into is Alexandria's: the Sunday after the paschal (tabular) full moon, the first on or after 21 March, so Easter falls between 22 March and 25 April. **Nicaea** (325) did less than is usually said: its synodal letter to the Alexandrians announces that the Easterners who had followed the Jewish reckoning will keep Easter with Rome and Alexandria; Constantine's letter (Eusebius, *Life of Constantine* III.18-20; NPNF second series vol. 14) asks for one day for all and argues in openly anti-Jewish terms; neither gives a method of calculation, and Rome and Alexandria kept different Sundays in several years after 325 (Hefele's excursus, same volume). Dionysius Exiguus's 19-year table later aligned the West with Alexandria. Since the Gregorian reform of 1582 East and West usually keep different dates (CCC 1170, which compresses the later history into Nicaea; a historical summary in the Catechism adds no weight). The Quartodeciman quarrel: [`church-history` 1.2](../church-history/lessons/01-02-bishops-canon-and-the-parting-of-the-ways.md). SC's appendix: no objection to a fixed Sunday in the Gregorian calendar if the Churches not in communion with Rome assent.

| Derived date | Offset from Easter (E) |
|---|---|
| Ash Wednesday | E-46 |
| Ascension | E+39 (or the 7th Sunday of Easter, E+42) |
| Pentecost | E+49 |
| Trinity Sunday | E+56 |
| Corpus Christi | E+60 (or Sunday, E+63) |

Worked year ([6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)): Easter 5 April 2026; the liturgical year begun 30 November 2025.

**Level:** **discipline, no rung**; Nicaea's decision was discipline, defining nothing; Easter on a Sunday is older than any computus.

**Does not license:** "Nicaea fixed the full-moon rule".

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Temporal and sanctoral cycles

The Proper of Time runs the seasons; the feasts of Mary and the saints are laid over it, and the Proper of Time has precedence.

**Temporal:** Advent, Christmas Time, Ordinary Time, Lent, the [Paschal Triduum](#paschal-triduum), Easter Time, Ordinary Time again. **Sanctoral:** the feasts of Mary and the saints (SC 103-104, 108, 111). **Ranks** (GNLY 10-14): solemnity (begun at First Vespers the evening before), feast (within the day), memorial, obligatory or optional (obligatory memorials in Lent become optional). **Precedence:** the Table of Liturgical Days alone decides (GNLY 59): 1 the Triduum; 2 Christmas, Epiphany, Ascension, Pentecost, the Sundays of Advent, Lent and Easter, Ash Wednesday, Holy Week Monday to Thursday, the Easter Octave; 3 General Calendar solemnities; ... 9 Lenten weekdays; ... 13 other weekdays. An impeded solemnity moves to the nearest day not in nos. 1-8; an Annunciation in Holy Week goes to the Monday after the Second Sunday of Easter (GNLY 60). **The 1969 calendar:** Paul VI's motu proprio *Mysterii Paschalis* (14 February 1969; in force 1 January 1970) carried out SC 111: some saints left the General Calendar, others became optional with their cult returned to their regions, martyrs from later-evangelized lands were added. A calendar entry says who is kept everywhere, not who is a saint; particular calendars keep their own (GNLY 9, 49).

**Level:** **liturgical law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Paschal Triduum

The high point of the year: from the evening Mass of the Lord's Supper, centred on the Easter Vigil, to Vespers of Easter Sunday.

GNLY 18-19. Easter is to the year what Sunday is to the week (GNLY 18); the fifty days to Pentecost are one feast, "one great Sunday" (GNLY 22). Table no. 1. A sliver of the year and its axle: Lent leads into it, Easter Time flows out of it, Easter Sunday belongs to both. Pius XII restored the Vigil to Easter night (S.C. Rites, *Dominicae Resurrectionis*, 9 February 1951) and reformed Holy Week (*Maxima Redemptionis nostrae mysteria*, 16 November 1955), counted by the 1969 Missal as the reform's first stage ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)). Adults are baptized, confirmed and given communion at the Vigil where possible ([catechumenate](#catechumenate)).

**Level:** liturgical law, no rung.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Lectionary

Three readings each Sunday on a three-year cycle: a Sunday text now returns once in three years, not every year.

SC 51 asked for a more representative part of Scripture read over a set number of years. *Ordo Lectionum Missae* (1969; second edition 1981). **Sundays:** three readings (Old Testament, or Acts in Easter Time; apostle; gospel), a three-year cycle: Year A Matthew, B Mark, C Luke; John in late Lent and Easter Time; in Year B five Sundays of John 6 follow the sixteenth, John's feeding replacing Mark's (Introduction 66, 74, 105). Harmony and semicontinuous reading (66-68). A year divisible by three is Year C, and each cycle begins at Advent of the year before (note 102: 1980 C, 1981 A, 1982 B). **Weekdays:** a yearly gospel cycle and a two-year first reading, Year I in odd years, Year II in even (69). Psalm references follow the Neo-Vulgate (note 123; [psalm numbering](#psalm-numbering)). Worked: the Sunday gospels until Advent 2026 are Year A (2026 leaves remainder 1), the weekday first readings Year II. Part of the 1969-1971 reform ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)).

**Level:** **liturgical law, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Liturgy of the Hours

Christ's priestly prayer continued by his Body through the day, the whole Church's prayer: the clergy hold the obligation, not the prayer.

Through the Church Christ continues his priestly work, in the Eucharist and especially in the Office, the bride's voice to the bridegroom and the prayer Christ with his Body addresses to the Father (SC 83-84; *General Instruction of the Liturgy of the Hours*, GILH, 15); no private function but the whole Church's (GILH 20); it extends to the hours the praise and memorial of the Eucharist (GILH 12; CCC 1178). **Shape** (SC 89; GILH 34-92): the invitatory (Ps 94 [Heb 95]); the Office of Readings, the old Matins, sayable at any hour; Lauds and Vespers, "the two hinges" of the day; daytime prayer, Terce, Sext and None, one sufficing outside choir; Compline before sleep. Prime suppressed (SC 89).
