Paul VI's apostolic constitution *Laudis Canticum* (1 November 1970) promulgated the reformed books, spreading the psalter over four weeks instead of the one week Pius X's breviary of 1911 (*Divino Afflatu*) had restored. **Obligation:** priests and deacons aspiring to the presbyterate pray it daily, permanent deacons as their conference defines (can. 276 §2 3°); "the whole sequence of Hours", Lauds and Vespers not omitted without serious reason (GILH 29); religious by their constitutions (can. 1174 §1). **The laity:** earnestly invited "as an action of the Church" (can. 1174 §2); pastors should see that Vespers is celebrated in church on Sundays, and lay people may pray it with priests, among themselves or alone (SC 100; GILH 27); no obligation (*Mediator Dei* §150). Clergy are deputed so that the Church's prayer never lapses (GILH 28). Benedict: *Ergo nihil operi Dei praeponatur*, "Therefore let nothing be put before the Work of God" (*Rule* 43, lesson's translation); "Seven times a day I have given praise to thee" (*Rule* 16, Ps 118:164 [Heb 119:164]). GILH 131 omits three imprecatory psalms and some verses ([psalm numbering](#psalm-numbering)).

**Level:** what the Office *is* (SC 83-84, GILH 15), **at least rung 3**; its shape, the four-week psalter, the obligation and GILH 131, **law, no rung**; Benedict's *Rule*, no magisterial level.

**Does not license:** "the Office is the priest's private breviary".

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Psalm numbering

The Latin liturgical books number the psalms by the Greek and Vulgate, mostly one behind the Hebrew: convert before you look.

| Liturgical (Greek/Vulgate) reference | Hebrew numbering |
|---|---|
| GILH 34 invitatory "Ps 94" | Ps 95 |
| GILH 88 Compline Pss 4, 133, 90 | Pss 4, 134, 91 |
| GILH 130 Pss 77, 104, 105 | Pss 78, 105, 106 |
| Douay 118:164 (*Rule* 16) | 119:164 |
| Douay 115:12 | 116:12 (Heb 116 = Douay 114 vv. 1-9 + 115 vv. 10-19) |
| GILH 131 omits Douay 57, 82, 108 | Heb 58, 83, 109 |

The Lectionary's psalm references follow the Neo-Vulgate (Introduction, note 123). The full conversion table, the genres and GILH 131's omissions are [`old-testament` 5.1](../old-testament/lessons/05-01-the-psalms.md)'s. The 150 psalms are canonical (Trent, Session IV, **rung 1**); GILH 131's omissions are liturgical law.

*Lessons:* [6.2](lessons/06-02-the-liturgical-year-and-the-liturgy-of-the-hours.md)

### Rite

A rite is one church's whole patrimony of living the faith, not a rulebook: the tradition (a family several churches share) as one church lives it.

CCEO can. 28 §1 (lesson's translation): a rite is the liturgical, theological, spiritual and disciplinary patrimony, distinguished by the culture and history of peoples, expressed in each church *sui iuris*'s own manner of living the faith; §2: the Eastern rites arise from five traditions, Alexandrian, Antiochene, Armenian, Chaldean and Constantinopolitan. Keep apart **tradition** (Melkite and Ukrainian Catholics and the Orthodox of Greece and Russia all pray the Byzantine liturgies) and **rite** strictly (that tradition as one church lives it). The Latin Church lives principally the Roman Rite, with the Ambrosian at Milan, the Mozarabic at Toledo and some religious orders' rites (CCC 1203). **Equal dignity:** all lawfully recognized rites "of equal right and dignity" (SC 4); no church superior to another by reason of rite (OE 3; OE 1). The church *sui iuris* itself is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-02-the-local-and-the-universal-church.md)'s. A Roman Rite that absorbed Frankish and other usages is still Roman by that church's reception and authority, not by unmixed origin; the Missal speaks of its "supraregional character" (GIRM 397). Borrowing runs both ways: the Latin confirmation formula of 1971 is Byzantine ([2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md)).

**Level:** SC 4, OE 1 and 3, **at least rung 3**; CCEO cann. 27-28, **law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "an Eastern Catholic church is a rite of the Latin Church", or treating any rite as the norm the others deviate from.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

### Eastern rites

Five traditions, each shared by Catholic and other churches; twenty-three Eastern Catholic churches *sui iuris*.

| Tradition | Catholic churches | Others | Anaphoras |
|---|---|---|---|
| Constantinopolitan (Byzantine) | fourteen, the Ukrainian and Melkite among them | the Eastern Orthodox churches | St John Chrysostom ordinarily; St Basil about ten times a year (Hapgood 1906) |
| Alexandrian | Coptic, Ethiopian, Eritrean | Coptic and Ethiopian Orthodox | St Mark |
| Antiochene (West Syriac) | Maronite, Syriac, Syro-Malankara | Syriac Orthodox | St James |
| Armenian | Armenian Catholic | Armenian Apostolic | |
| Chaldean (East Syriac) | Chaldean, Syro-Malabar | Assyrian Church of the East | Addai and Mari, Theodore, Nestorius |

The count of twenty-three is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 3.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/03-02-the-local-and-the-universal-church.md)'s; "fourteen" Byzantine is computed from it, not checked against a current *Annuario*. Communion under both kinds is the usual form in the Eastern rites (CCC 1390); confirmation is chrismation by the baptizing priest ([2.3](lessons/02-03-confirmation.md)); the minister of marriage is the priest who crowns ([5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)). Equal dignity: [rite](#rite).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

### Anaphora

The eucharistic prayer, "a carrying up, an offering" (lesson's gloss): one shape all rites share, in three orders.

Justin's president prayed "according to his ability" ([3.1](lessons/03-01-the-institution-and-the-earliest-witness.md)): the shape was fixed before the words. By the fourth century the thanksgiving survives in written anaphoras. After the dialogue ("Lift up your hearts"), the constants GIRM 79 lists: thanksgiving (the preface), Sanctus, [epiclesis](#epiclesis), institution narrative, [anamnesis](#anamnesis), offering, intercessions, doxology.

| Family | Order |
|---|---|
| Antiochene (Byzantine, West Syriac, Armenian) | Sanctus, narrative, anamnesis, the Spirit invoked, then all the intercessions |
| Alexandrian (St Mark, ANF 7) | intercessions inside the preface, before the Sanctus; a petition to consecrate both before and after the narrative (Fortescue, 1912, App. II) |
| Roman | a variable preface, then a fixed Canon; intercessions split, the living before the narrative and the dead after; *Quam oblationem* before the narrative asks that the offering become Christ's Body and Blood, naming no Spirit |

The Byzantine Chrysostom anaphora (Hapgood): the anamnesis remembers the Cross, the grave, the Resurrection, the Ascension and "the Second and glorious Coming-again", a memorial that includes the future; "Thine own, of thine own, we offer unto thee"; the Spirit invoked; the departed and the saints commemorated before the living. Didache 9-10 has thanksgiving prayers with no narrative ([Addai and Mari](#addai-and-mari) is the test of what the form requires). A priest may not compose his own: GIRM 147 requires an approved Eucharistic Prayer (SC 22 §3; can. 846 §1); rewriting the preface and intercessions leaves a Mass valid if the consecratory words stand, and illicit ([6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md) Ex 1).

**Level:** the structure, liturgical law and description; the form, [epiclesis](#epiclesis).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Epiclesis

The invocation asking the Father to send the Spirit, or the power of his blessing, on the gifts: in Orthodox teaching the moment of the change; in Latin teaching the Lord's words are the form.

CCC 1353: the epiclesis asks the Father to send the Holy Spirit (or the power of his blessing) on the bread and wine; with the institution narrative, Christ's words and action and the Spirit's power make him present. **Byzantine** (Hapgood 1906), after narrative and anamnesis: "send thy Holy Spirit upon us, and upon these Gifts here spread forth", "Transmuting them by thy Holy Spirit". The verb is *metaballō*, "to change" (lesson's gloss). **The Latin teaching:** Florence's instruction for the Armenians (lesson's translation): "The form of this sacrament is the words of the Saviour by which he effected this sacrament; for the priest, speaking in the person of Christ, effects this sacrament." Trent: the body under the bread "by the force of the words" (XIII ch. 3). The Roman Canon's *Quam oblationem* asks for the change before the narrative and names no Spirit. The Orthodox account is stated in [Orthodox on the epiclesis](#orthodox-on-the-epiclesis), with no reply; the comparison of Ambrose and Cyril on the moment and the agent is the module's boss problem.

**Level:** Florence on the form, with Trent XIII ch. 3, **at least rung 3**; that the words of institution alone consecrate, at the instant they are completed, long the common teaching of Latin theology, **rung 4**, never defined; CCC 1353 restates. Orthodox texts, no Catholic level.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

### Roman Canon

The Roman Rite's fixed eucharistic prayer: a much-added-to form of a shape all rites share, defined free from error, not defined as old, sole or unchangeable.

**History:** *On the Sacraments* IV quotes the Canon's core, from the petition before the narrative through the offering after it: the earliest Latin witness, late fourth century, its ascription to Ambrose long doubted and now widely accepted ([`patristics` 4.1](../patristics/lessons/04-01-ambrose-of-milan.md)). Gregory I defends his own changes, among them the Lord's Prayer said "immediately after the canon" (*Ep.* IX.12, NPNF second series vol. 13; [`patristics` 5.3](../patristics/lessons/05-03-gregory-the-great.md)). Between 784 and 791 Pope Hadrian sent Charlemagne Gregory's sacramentary; the Franks supplemented it (Duchesne credited Alcuin; later scholarship, Benedict of Aniane), and the mixed book returned to Rome and displaced the pure one. Low Mass produced the single-volume missal; local uses (Sarum, Lyons) multiplied until 1570. The Creed entered the Roman Mass in 1014 at Henry II's request (by the usual account); the offertory prayers came from beyond the Alps (Fortescue). **Shape:** intercessions split around the narrative (living before, dead after); the *Communicantes* lists after Mary and the apostles only martyrs, all of Rome's own cult but Cyprian ("the local liturgy of the city of Rome", Fortescue); the current Missal also names St Joseph. **The offering:** in the anamnesis *Unde et memores*, *nos servi tui, sed et plebs tua sancta ... offerimus*, "we your servants, and your holy people too, ... offer" a pure, holy, spotless victim (lesson's translation; [sacrifice of the Mass](#sacrifice-of-the-mass)). **Trent XXII ch. 4:** instituted "many years ago", composed "out of the very words of the Lord, the traditions of the apostles, and the pious institutions also of holy pontiffs"; **can. 6:** anathema on saying it contains errors and should therefore be abrogated. The 1969 Missal kept it beside three new eucharistic prayers with the Lord's words identical in all ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)).

**Level:** XXII can. 6, **rung 1** that the Canon contains no error; **not** for its age, sole use or unchangeability (Trent's own chapter credits "holy pontiffs" with additions).

**Does not license:** "can. 6 made the Roman Canon the only lawful eucharistic prayer"; "the pure Mass of the city of Rome".

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

### Quo Primum

Pius V's bull of 1570 imposing the restored Missal: a law of uniformity with an exemption for old rites, not a doctrinal act.

Bull *Quo primum tempore*, dated *pridie Idus Julii*, 14 July 1570 (some copies, and the vatican.va English of *Missale Romanum* note 1, say 13 or 19 July). Trent's decrees had left the missal's publication and correction to the pope. It imposed the restored Missal wherever conventual Mass is celebrated "according to the rite of the Roman Church" (lesson's translation), so it does not touch the Eastern rites; exempted rites approved by the Apostolic See from their institution, or by custom, continuously observed for more than two hundred years (which may adopt the new Missal with the consent of bishop or prelate and the whole chapter); forbade adding, removing or changing; declared itself *perpetuo valitura*. Pius V's experts restored the Missal "to the original norm and rite of the holy Fathers" (lesson's translation) and kept the Frankish additions. Lyons, Dominicans, Carmelites, Carthusians, Milan and Toledo kept their rites (Fortescue). Revised by Clement VIII (*Cum Sanctissimum*, 7 July 1604) and Urban VIII (*Si quid est*, 2 September 1634), named on the title page (*Clementis VIII et Urbani VIII auctoritate recognitum*); Leo XIII edition 1884. The perpetuity clause bound Pius V's subjects, not his successors.

**Level:** **papal law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "*Quo Primum* made the 1570 Missal irreformable" (calling it a dogmatic act overstates its level).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md); the reform's sequel: [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Addai and Mari

The East Syriac anaphora of the Assyrian Church of the East, which as received has no institution narrative, judged valid by the CDF in 2001.

Prayed about two hundred days a year (Palm Sunday to the year's end); in its oldest manuscripts and unbroken use it has no coherent institution narrative (the explanatory article, §2). Brightman (1896) prints one only in brackets, from observed practice, as "obviously incoherent with the context"; ANF 7's rubric "Here follow the words of institution" is its editors'. **The act:** Pontifical Council for Promoting Christian Unity, *Guidelines for Admission to the Eucharist between the Chaldean Church and the Assyrian Church of the East* (Rome, 20 July 2001), issued "in agreement with" the CDF and the Congregation for the Oriental Churches; they report that on 17 January 2001 the CDF concluded the anaphora "can be considered valid", and John Paul II approved (the form of approval is not stated). **Three grounds:** great antiquity and the intention of continuing the Last Supper, its validity never officially contested; a true particular church with apostolic succession and full eucharistic faith (UR 15); the words of institution present "in a dispersed euchological way" through its thanksgiving, praise and intercession. **What it did:** judged one anaphora valid; let Assyrians in necessity, and Chaldeans who cannot reach a Catholic minister, receive at each other's altars; restated that the words of institution are constitutive and indispensable; "warmly invited" Assyrian priests to insert them when Chaldeans are present (the Assyrian synod had allowed this in 1978); called its considerations intended exclusively for this case; denied that this is full communion. **What it did not do:** draw a general rule about eucharistic form. The 1994 Common Christological Declaration with the Assyrian Church: [`christology` 6.3](../christology/lessons/06-03-the-churches-that-did-not-receive-chalcedon.md).

**Said plainly** ([6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)): since Florence the Latin Church has taught that the Saviour's words are this sacrament's form; in 2001 its doctrinal congregation, with the pope's approval, judged valid an anaphora that does not recite them as a narrative, as a whole church has prayed it "from time immemorial" (Guidelines §3).

**Level:** a CDF doctrinal judgment with papal approval, **rung 3**, neither definitive nor general; the admission provisions, **discipline, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "the narrative is now optional" (overstates) or "a pastoral tolerance" (understates: it is a doctrinal judgment of validity). Whether a narrative is required is the module's essay question, not answered here.

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

## The reform, the language, and the life around the liturgy

Module 7: the twentieth-century reform and the law of the older books; language, translation, music and celebrating well; and the signs and devotions the Church and her people add around the sacraments. Keep four questions apart: could the Church reform (settled), is the reformed rite valid and lawful (settled), was it done well (open), what freedom the older books have (law, which changes).

### Missal of Paul VI

The Roman Missal promulgated in 1969: valid and lawful, settled; the prudence of particular changes, freely disputed.

**Before the council:** Pius X's "active participation" (1903), frequent and early communion, and the psalter redistributed (*Divino Afflatu*, 1 November 1911); Pius XII's *Mediator Dei* (1947), the Easter Vigil restored to the night (1951) and Holy Week reformed (1955), which the 1969 constitution counts as the first stage of revision; John XXIII's new rubrics (*Rubricarum instructum*, 25 July 1960) and the 1962 Missal. **The reform:** a commission set up under *Sacram Liturgiam* (25 January 1964), the Consilium, under Cardinal Giacomo Lercaro with Annibale Bugnini as secretary, drafted the books. Paul VI's apostolic constitution *Missale Romanum* (Holy Thursday, 3 April 1969; in force 30 November 1969, Advent I; full Missal 1970): three new eucharistic prayers beside the Roman Canon, the Lord's words identical in all; duplications cut "above all" in the offertory and communion rites; homily, prayer of the faithful and penitential act restored; a three-year Sunday cycle with an Old Testament reading ([lectionary](#lectionary)). New rites of ordination (1968), confirmation (1971), anointing and adult initiation (1972). The 1969 Instruction's article 7 definition of the Mass was later rewritten (now GIRM 27: a priest acting in the person of Christ, the Eucharistic Sacrifice), and a preamble restating Trent opens the Instruction (GIRM 1-15). GIRM 6 claims Pius V's warrant: SC, like *Quo Primum*, restores rites to "the original norm of the holy Fathers". The Missal remains a Latin book that every vernacular edition translates ([liturgiam authenticam](#liturgiam-authenticam)).

**Two readings, each at full strength.** *Organic development* (Alcuin Reid, *The Organic Development of the Liturgy*, 2004; 2nd ed. 2005): the Roman Rite grows slowly, elements accrue, are pruned, are received; the council asked that new forms "grow organically from forms already existing" (SC 23); a missal composed by experts in a few years, with new eucharistic prayers and a rewritten offertory, broke that principle and exceeded the mandate; authority over the liturgy is a guardian's, not an author's. *Ressourcement and reception* (Massimo Faggioli, *True Reform*, 2012): SC is the council's first act and the key to its ecclesiology, the Church as God's people assembled, fed by the word, offering with the priest; the reform is that council received; it removed what SC 50 told it to remove, and what it restored, from the prayer of the faithful to the cup, was older than what it replaced; to reject the reform is to reject the ecclesiology.

**Said plainly** ([7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)): the reform was carried out faster and more extensively than most council fathers expected. They passed a constitution, 2,147 to 4, that preserved Latin and opened the vernacular first to the readings and the common prayer; less than six years later Paul VI told a general audience that Latin would no longer be the Mass's principal language, and called its loss *un grande sacrificio*. SC is silent on the celebrant's orientation (SC 128 orders only the revision of laws on building altars and churches).

| Settled | Open |
|---|---|
| The Church's authority to reform her rites, the substance untouched: at least rung 3 | Whether the books as executed followed SC, above all SC 23 |
| The reformed sacraments are valid: defined doctrine of matter and form applied ([1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)) | Whether particular changes were prudent: offertory, orientation, the vernacular's extent, new prayers, lectionary |
| The reformed Missal is lawful: it is the Roman Rite's law | The historiography: Reid against Faggioli, and the Consilium's pace |
| SC's doctrinal teaching: at least rung 3 | The future discipline of the 1962 books: law, changeable |

**Level:** *Missale Romanum*, an apostolic constitution, **law, no rung**; validity, settled; lawfulness, settled; prudence of particular changes, **freely disputed**; Reid and Faggioli, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not license:** "criticizing the reform is disloyal" (shrinks what is open) or "the reformed Missal's validity is debatable" (opens what is settled).

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Ottaviani Intervention

The 1969 *Short Critical Study* that two cardinals sent Paul VI, charging the new Order of Mass with a departure from Trent, and Paul VI's answer.

Cover letter of Cardinals Alfredo Ottaviani and Antonio Bacci, Rome, 25 September 1969, transmitting *A Short Critical Study of the New Order of Mass* (dated 5 June 1969, Corpus Christi; by an unnamed "group of Roman theologians"). **The charge:** a striking departure from the theology of Trent's Session XXII. **The evidence:** the 1969 Instruction's article 7 defined the Mass as the people assembled, a priest presiding, to celebrate the Lord's memorial, naming neither sacrifice nor real presence; offertory prayers stating the oblation (*Suscipe, sancta Trinitas*; *Veni, Sanctificator*) were cut; "The change in the formula reveals the change in doctrine". It begged to keep the Missal of Pius V. **The answer:** Paul VI's general audiences of 19 and 26 November 1969 (Italian): the change was the council's will; nothing in the substance of the traditional Mass had changed; a rite and its rubrics are not of themselves a dogmatic definition; *non diciamo dunque «nuova Messa», ma piuttosto «nuova epoca»*, not a new Mass but a new era; the loss of Latin "a great sacrifice". The article 7 definition was later rewritten and a doctrinal preamble added ([Missal of Paul VI](#missal-of-paul-vi)). The charge was doctrinal; the answer separated doctrine from rite and amended the text. That the Mass is a true sacrifice was affirmed by the critics and Paul VI alike (Trent XXII can. 1, **rung 1**).

**Level:** the *Study*, **no magisterial level**; the audiences, ordinary teaching at its lightest.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Ecclesia Dei

John Paul II's 1988 motu proprio after Lefebvre's consecrations, and the indult it widened; the 2026 consecrations and the DDF's decree.

**The 1984 indult:** *Quattuor abhinc annos* (Congregation for Divine Worship, circular letter, 3 October 1984): bishops may permit named groups the 1962 Missal if they do not question the 1970 Missal's legitimacy and doctrinal exactness; not in parish churches save extraordinary cases; in Latin; no mixing of Missals; a report to Rome. **Ecclesia Dei** (apostolic letter motu proprio, 2 July 1988), after Archbishop Lefebvre's consecrations of 30 June 1988: the act schismatic (§3), excommunication incurred; Tradition's living character (§4); a Commission for those wishing to remain in communion (§6a); "wide and generous application" of the 1984 indult (§6c). The 1988 events and the 2009 remission of the excommunications are [`church-history` 9.3](../church-history/lessons/09-03-after-the-council.md)'s.

**2026:** after Leo XIV's letter of 29 June 2026 to the Society of St Pius X's Superior General against an imminent "schismatic act", a decree of the Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith (2 July 2026; press.vatican.va) declared that the consecrating bishop (Alfonso de Galarreta) and four new bishops had incurred excommunication *latae sententiae* reserved to the Apostolic See (can. 1387), the co-consecrator (Bernard Fellay) excommunication under can. 1364 §1, and called the act schismatic. Its explanatory note calls the Society's confessions and the marriages its priests witness **invalid** (no faculty, [4.1](lessons/04-01-penance-the-sacrament-and-its-history.md); no canonical form, [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)) and its other sacraments **unlawful**. The consecrations are valid and unlawful ([5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md) Ex 2; can. 1387 is the 2021 number, formerly 1382). Francis's 2016 grant that Society confessions are valid and licit (*Misericordia et misera* 12) presupposed their orders ([faculties](#faculties)).

**Level:** *Quattuor abhinc annos*, *Ecclesia Dei* and the 2026 decree, **law or juridical acts, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Summorum Pontificum

Benedict XVI's 2007 motu proprio: the 1962 Missal never abrogated, two usages of the one Roman rite, freed for any Latin priest.

Apostolic letter motu proprio, 7 July 2007, in force 14 September 2007: the 1962 Missal "never abrogated"; the Missals of Paul VI and of 1962 the ordinary and extraordinary expressions of one *lex orandi*, "two usages of the one Roman rite" (art. 1); any Latin priest may use either without a congregation and without permission (art. 2); parish priests to accede to stable groups (art. 5 §1). Benedict's letter to the bishops: in liturgical history "growth and progress, but no rupture"; the "ordinary/extraordinary form" wording is the letter's. Applied by the Pontifical Commission Ecclesia Dei's instruction *Universae Ecclesiae* (30 April 2011). Superseded by [Traditionis Custodes](#traditionis-custodes) art. 8.

**Level:** **law, no rung**; "never abrogated" judged 1970, and is no permanent guarantee.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Traditionis Custodes

Francis's 2021 motu proprio: the reformed books "the unique expression" of the Roman Rite's *lex orandi*, the bishop's exclusive competence, earlier norms abrogated.

Apostolic letter motu proprio, 16 July 2021: the books promulgated by Paul VI and John Paul II "the unique expression" of the *lex orandi* of the Roman Rite (art. 1); the diocesan bishop's exclusive competence (art. 2); groups must not deny the validity and legitimacy of the reform (art. 3 §1); not in parish churches, no new personal parishes (art. 3 §2), no new groups (art. 3 §6); priests ordained after it must ask, and the bishop consult the Holy See (art. 4); non-conforming earlier norms abrogated (art. 8). The Congregation for Divine Worship's *Responsa ad dubia* (4 December 2021: for example, no pre-conciliar *Pontificale*; the 1952 *Rituale* only in personal parishes). A rescript *ex audientia* (20 February 2023) reserved dispensations from art. 3 §2 and art. 4 to the Holy See. As of October 2026 unchanged by Leo XIV, who on 29 June 2026 urged respect for the rites of Paul VI and John Paul II (letter to Cardinal Roche). Art. 3 §1 asks groups not to deny the reform's validity and legitimacy, not to think every change prudent.

**One expression or two?** SP art. 1 and TC art. 1 are both apostolic letters given motu proprio, law, no rung; in both the phrase names which books the law recognizes as the Roman Rite's prayer, and a later legislator may change that. SP's "never abrogated" judged 1970; TC abrogates norms from 2021. *Lex orandi* is also a theological source, and the 1962 Missal prayed the Church's faith for centuries; TC says nothing against that faith. Whether "unique expression" is purely juridical, Catholics dispute; no act settles it.

**Level:** TC, the *Responsa* and the rescript, **law or juridical acts, no rung**, changeable by the competent authority.

**Does not license:** reading "unique expression" as dogma.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md)

### Desiderio Desideravi

Francis's 2022 apostolic letter on the liturgical formation of the People of God: formation, *ars celebrandi*, and no going back to the unreformed rite.

Apostolic letter, 29 June 2022. Formation (§§27-47); *ars celebrandi* (§§48-60: not a rubrical mechanism nor creativity without rules, the rite itself a norm, §48; silence, §52; inadequate models of presiding in opposed pairs, "rigid austerity or an exasperating creativity", "rushed briskness or overemphasized slowness", rooted in a personalism that puts the celebrant at the centre, §54); §61: no going back to the ritual form the council fathers judged in need of reform; *Traditionis Custodes* restated. Leo XIV's letter to Cardinal Roche (29 June 2026) cites DD 34, 38, 54.

**Level:** its teaching, **rung 3**; its counsel on celebrating well, prudential, no rung.

*Lessons:* [7.1](lessons/07-01-the-twentieth-century-reform-and-the-disputes-over-it.md), [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Comme le prevoit

The Consilium's 1969 instruction on translation: communicate what the Church meant; the unit of meaning is the passage.

*Comme le prévoit*, 25 January 1969 (French original). A translation communicates to a people in its own language what the Church meant by the original (¶6); the unit of meaning is the passage, not the word (¶12); intelligibility may take precedence over verbal fidelity (¶29); the terse Roman collects may be rendered more freely, moderately amplified, "while conserving the original ideas" (¶34); Scripture never paraphrased (¶31); new texts may be composed, growing organically (¶43). "Dynamic equivalence" is the theorists' name, not the document's. Superseded by [*Liturgiam Authenticam*](#liturgiam-authenticam) (¶8). The 1973 English Missal ("Lord, I am not worthy to receive you") was made under it. Jerome's "sense for sense" is its ancestor ([`patristics` 4.2](../patristics/lessons/04-02-jerome.md)).

**Level:** an instruction, **no rung**, unable to derogate from law.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Liturgiam Authenticam

The 2001 instruction on vernacular translation: integral and exact, without paraphrase; never abrogated, now read in light of the amended can. 838.

Congregation for Divine Worship and the Discipline of the Sacraments, Fifth Instruction, dated 28 March 2001 (approved 20 March, in force 25 April, published 7 May). Translation "not so much a work of creative innovation" as faithful rendering, integral and exact as far as possible, without omissions, additions, paraphrases or glosses (¶20); always from the Latin *editio typica* (¶23); a sacred register (¶27); do not make explicit what the original leaves implicit (¶28); *consubstantialis* among the hard terms (¶53); *Et cum spiritu tuo* as literal as possible (¶56); keep relative clauses, parallelisms and causal particles (¶57); Rome's *recognitio* "not a mere formality", able to introduce even substantial changes (¶80); the Holy See may prepare translations itself (¶104). The English Missal of 2010-2011 ("and with your spirit", "consubstantial", "under my roof") was made under it; defenders heard fidelity and a register fit for worship, critics syntax hard to proclaim and a review become rewriting. Benedict XVI defended "for many" (*pro multis*) to the German bishops (letter to Archbishop Zollitsch, 14 April 2012): "for all" had been an interpretation as much as a translation; "for many" does not teach that Christ died only for some.

**Level:** an instruction, **no rung**; never abrogated, but read in light of can. 838 as amended in 2017 (Archbishop Roche's commentary), corrected where contrary by the decree of 22 October 2021, and unable to derogate from law (can. 34 §2).

**Does not license:** "LA still governs every word" or "*Magnum Principium* abolished it".

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Magnum Principium

Francis's 2017 motu proprio amending can. 838: conferences translate faithfully and Rome confirms; adaptations still need Rome's review.

Apostolic letter motu proprio, dated 3 September 2017 (published 9 September, in force 1 October). **New can. 838:** §3 the conferences "faithfully" prepare vernacular versions, approve and publish them after the Apostolic See's *confirmatio*; §2 the Apostolic See recognizes **adaptations** (*recognitio*), including SC 40's deeper ones. **Old (1983):** §2 Rome reviews translations; §3 conferences publish "after the prior review of the Holy See". The Congregation's note: *confirmatio* is an authoritative ratification of the bishops' approval, ordinarily granted on trust, with detailed review kept for sacramental forms, the Order of Mass, the Eucharistic Prayers and the ordination prayers. Francis's letter to Cardinal Sarah (dated 15 October, released 22 October 2017), known only as Catholic News Service reported it: translations need no longer conform to LA in every point. The decree *Postquam Summus Pontifex* (22 October 2021) ¶20: fidelity threefold, to the original text, to the receiving language, to the hearers' understanding. The conferences translate and Rome checks; what moved is how closely, and by which fidelity.

**Level:** a motu proprio, **law, no rung**; whether the change was wise, **freely disputed**; the letters, no rung.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Sacred music

The Church's musical tradition is a treasure; Gregorian chant has pride of place, other things being equal; the test is fitness to the rite, not style.

SC 112 (the musical tradition a treasure of inestimable value, above any other art, integral to solemn liturgy); SC 116 (Gregorian chant "specially suited to the Roman liturgy", "other things being equal" given "pride of place"; polyphony not excluded); 118 (the people's religious singing); 119 (mission lands' own music); 120 (the pipe organ; other instruments with the territorial authority's consent); 121 (sung texts drawn chiefly from Scripture and liturgical sources). Pius X, *Tra le sollecitudini* (motu proprio, 22 November 1903) §2: "sanctity and goodness of form, from which its other character of universality spontaneously springs" (PD English); §3 chant the supreme model. *Sacramentum Caritatis* (2007) §42: in liturgy one song is not as good as another; chant suitably esteemed; §62: the faithful taught the common prayers in Latin and parts of the liturgy in chant.

**Said plainly** ([7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)): the council gave chant pride of place and wanted congregations able to sing their parts of the Ordinary in Latin (SC 54, 116); in most parishes of the Roman Rite neither is what happens on a Sunday, and the vernacular hymn became the norm. The text did not change; practice went elsewhere.

**Level:** SC's norms on music, **law, no rung**; *Sacramentum Caritatis* where it teaches, rung 3.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Ars celebrandi

The art of proper celebration: serving a rite one did not write, between two failures, performance and archaeology.

*Sacramentum Caritatis* (post-synodal apostolic exhortation, 22 February 2007) 38: the best way to ensure *actuosa participatio*, the fruit of faithful adherence to the liturgical norms in all their richness; 40: word and music, gesture and silence, simplicity over contrived additions. *Desiderio Desideravi* (2022) 48: not a rubrical mechanism nor imaginative creativity without rules; the rite is itself a norm serving a higher reality; 52 silence; 54 inadequate models of presiding in opposed pairs, rooted in a personalism that makes the celebrant the centre. GIRM 45 (silence prescribed, not decorative); 31 ("very brief" introductions at four points, never during the Eucharistic Prayer); 24 and 352 (choices entrusted to the priest). The liturgy is "never anyone's private property", neither the celebrant's nor the community's (*Ecclesia de Eucharistia* 52). **The two flanks:** performance (DD 54) and archaeology (*Mediator Dei* 61-64: a usage not better merely because ancient; Pistoia's "exaggerated and senseless antiquarianism"). The rubrics are the condition of the assembly's freedom: they guarantee that what it joins is the Church's prayer, not one man's. No canon measures minutes; the law leaves the celebrant choices, and *ars celebrandi* lives in that room ([7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md) Ex 2).

**Level:** **rung 3** where these documents teach; their counsel on celebrating well, **prudential, no rung**; GIRM, law.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Redemptionis Sacramentum

The 2004 instruction on abuses in the Eucharist: its categories rank gravity, not validity.

Congregation for Divine Worship and the Discipline of the Sacraments, with the CDF, dated 25 March 2004 (approved 19 March, published 23 April). **Ch. 8:** *graviora delicta* (172: the species taken, kept or thrown away for sacrilegious ends; attempted or simulated celebration; forbidden concelebration with ministers of communities lacking apostolic succession; one species consecrated for sacrilegious ends; per the norms then in force, since revised); **grave matters** (173: anything against nn. 48-52, 56, 76-77, 79, 91-92, 94, 96, 101-102, 104, 106, 109, 111, 115, 117, 126, 131-133, 138, 153, 168, described as putting the Eucharist's validity and dignity at risk); **other abuses** (174), still to be corrected. Complaints to the bishop or the Holy See (184). **Useful paragraphs:** 11 (quotes EdE 52); 48, 50 (bread and wine); 51 (only approved Eucharistic Prayers); 52 (joint recitation of the Eucharistic Prayer by the faithful an abuse); 63 (the Gospel reserved to the ordained, "even a religious" excluded); 64-66 (the homily reserved to priest or deacon; can. 767 §1); 69 (no unapproved creeds); 94 (no self-communion or passing the chalice); 112 (a priest may always celebrate in Latin, except at Masses the authorities have scheduled in the vernacular); 126 (vestments; can. 929). A Eucharistic Prayer the priest wrote himself is a grave matter (RS 51), and with the Lord's words intact the Mass is valid.

**Level:** an instruction, **no rung**, unable to derogate from law; validity stays with matter, form, minister and intention ([1.2](lessons/01-02-matter-form-minister-intention.md)).

**Does not license:** "a grave matter is an invalid Mass".

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md), [6.1](lessons/06-01-what-the-liturgy-is.md)

### Inculturation

A culture may enter the Roman Rite; it may not found a new one.

**SC 37-40:** no rigid uniformity where faith and the common good are not at stake (37); variations saving "the substantial unity of the Roman rite" (38); adaptations within the typical editions by the territorial authority (39); deeper adaptations proposed by the conference and introduced with the Apostolic See's consent (40). ***Varietates Legitimae*** (Congregation for Divine Worship, Fourth Instruction, 25 January 1994): substantial unity, no new families of rites (36); authority alone, never a celebrant or congregation, and no region's concession extended elsewhere unauthorized (37); the SC 40 procedure, the books' options exhausted first, then proposal, supervised experiment and Rome's decree (63-68). **The Roman Missal for the dioceses of Zaire**, approved in 1988 (decree *Zairensium Dioecesium*, 30 April 1988, per secondary sources): an announcer, upright ancestors invoked with the saints, the penitential rite after the homily, dance; the best-known instance. Can. 838 §2 (2017) keeps *recognitio* for adaptations. The two governing principles (*Redemptoris Missio* 52-54, rung 3) are [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 2.3](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/02-03-catholic.md)'s; the Chinese rites are [`church-history` 7.2](../church-history/lessons/07-02-accommodation-in-asia.md)'s; Gregory's letter to Mellitus, popular piety's founding method, is [`church-history` 3.1](../church-history/lessons/03-01-gregory-the-great-and-the-conversion-of-the-peoples.md)'s.

**Level:** SC's norms and the instruction, **law and instruction, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [7.2](lessons/07-02-liturgical-language-and-ars-celebrandi.md)

### Sacramentals

Sacred signs the Church instituted, resembling the sacraments, which signify effects obtained through her intercession: a sacrament is Christ's act, a sacramental the Church's prayer.

SC 60, restated in can. 1166 and CCC 1667: sacred signs instituted by the Church, resembling the sacraments, signifying effects, chiefly spiritual, obtained through her intercession; they dispose people for the sacraments' chief effect and make life's occasions holy, drawing their power from the paschal mystery (SC 61). Aquinas: "Holy Water and other consecrated things are not called sacraments, because they do not produce the sacramental effect, which is the receiving of grace. They are, however, a kind of disposition to the sacraments" (ST III q.65 a.1 ad 6); the same reply keeps monastic profession out of the seven ([1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)).

| | Sacrament | Sacramental |
|---|---|---|
| Instituted by | Christ, all seven (Trent VII can. 1) | the Church; only the Apostolic See creates, interprets, changes or abolishes them (can. 1167 §1; SC 79 allowed new ones) |
| How it works | *ex opere operato* (VII can. 8) | [*ex opere operantis Ecclesiae*](#ex-opere-operantis): by the action of the Church, holy and united to her Head (*Mediator Dei* 27) |
| What it gives | the grace it signifies, to one who sets no obstacle | not grace as the sacraments give it; by the Church's prayer, a disposition to receive grace and cooperate with it (CCC 1670) |
| Minister | fixed for each sacrament | a cleric with the power; some lay people, as the liturgical books and the local ordinary allow (can. 1168; CCC 1669) |

Kinds: [blessings](#blessings) first, [exorcism](#exorcism); blessed objects (an indulgence attaches to the devout act, the object being its occasion, [4.2](lessons/04-02-indulgences-as-a-practice.md)). A blessed oil used outside the sacrament is at most a sacramental, and the non-ordained may not anoint the sick with any oil (*Ecclesiae de mysterio*, 1997, art. 9; [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)).

**Level:** that sacramentals are the Church's and work by her intercession, not *ex opere operato*, **at least rung 3** (SC 60-61; MD 27; CCC 1670); saying they confer grace *ex opere operato* is an exegetical error, graded strictly; cann. 1166-1172, law.

**Does not license:** "a sacramental is a small sacrament", or "not *ex opere operato*" read as "only as good as my feelings" (the prayer is the Church's, its power from the paschal mystery; the user's faith is the channel, not the source).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md), [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md)

### Blessings

The first of the sacramentals: praise of God and petition for his gifts, over persons, meals, objects and places; and *Fiducia Supplicans* on what a blessing is.

CCC 1671. Some blessings last, consecrating persons (an abbot, virgins, religious profession, ministries) or reserving objects and places for worship (dedication of a church or altar, the holy oils, vessels, bells) (CCC 1672); the manuals called these *constitutive*, against merely *invocative*, terms CCC 1672 does not use. The rites: *De Benedictionibus* (*Book of Blessings*, editio typica 1984). **Who:** only bishops, and presbyters permitted by law or grant, consecrate and dedicate, and here the Code says "validly" (can. 1169 §1); any presbyter gives unreserved blessings (§2); a deacon only those the law expressly permits (§3); lay people preside at some, fewer the nearer a blessing stands to sacramental life (CCC 1669); given first to Catholics, also to catechumens and non-Catholics (can. 1170). The priest's blessing at a wedding is, for Aquinas, a sacramental ([minister of matrimony](#minister-of-matrimony)).

***Fiducia Supplicans*** (Dicastery for the Doctrine of the Faith, declaration, 18 December 2023; approved by Francis in audience and, per its Presentation, signed by him; no *in forma specifica* clause): keeps the CDF's responsum of 22 February 2021 that the Church cannot bless same-sex unions (§§5, 11); distinguishes liturgical or ritual blessings, which require that what is blessed conform to God's will (§§9-11), from spontaneous pastoral blessings, brief gestures outside any rite that legitimize nothing (§§31-40); allows the latter for couples in irregular situations and same-sex couples, never linked to a civil ceremony or resembling a wedding (§39); "a real development" (Presentation). Defenders: it recovers blessing as the Church's prayer for anyone who asks. Critics: blessing two people because they come as a couple cannot in practice be told apart from blessing the relationship; some episcopal conferences declined to apply it. The DDF's press release of 4 January 2024: such statements are no doctrinal opposition; timing is left to each bishop; no "total or definitive denial" (§§1-2). On 11 January 2024 the Symposium of Episcopal Conferences of Africa and Madagascar declared that such blessings would not be given in Africa, while affirming communion with the pope.

**Level:** the canons, law; *Fiducia Supplicans*: its restatement of marriage doctrine keeps that doctrine's weight, its account of blessings **at most rung 3**, its permission **practical**; constitutive/invocative, **no level**.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Exorcism

The Church's authoritative request in Christ's name that a person or object be withdrawn from the Evil One's dominion: commanding a demon is a mandated priest's act, asking God for deliverance is everyone's.

CCC 1673. The simple exorcism is part of the rite of baptism (CCC 1237). A **major exorcism** needs the local ordinary's special and express permission, given only to a presbyter of piety, knowledge, prudence and integrity (can. 1172), who must first rule out illness (CCC 1673). CDF letter to ordinaries (29 September 1985): (1) observe can. 1172; (2) the faithful may not use the exorcism formula drawn from the one Leo XIII published, still less the whole text; (3) no one without the faculty may lead meetings that question demons directly; prayer for deliverance (Matt 6:13) stays open to all. The revised rite, *De exorcismis et supplicationibus quibusdam* (presented 26 January 1999): the major exorcism; public prayers by a priest with the bishop's permission against influence short of possession; private prayers for the faithful.

**Level:** **law and liturgical law, no rung**.

**Does not license:** "a lay deliverance group performs a minor exorcism".

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Popular piety

The faithful's own prayers, which the Church tests and prunes: a devotion is sound when it leads to the liturgy and expects its fruit from God, not from the procedure.

SC 13: devotions are highly commended if they harmonize with the liturgical seasons, derive from the liturgy and lead people to it, since the liturgy by its nature far surpasses them; CCC 1674-1676. **Terms** (*Directory on Popular Piety*): pious exercises (§7), devotions (§8), popular piety (§9), popular religiosity (§10). Sunday Mass binds, no devotion does (§11; can. 1247); the Gospel the measure, with a biblical, liturgical, ecumenical and anthropological spirit (§12); devotions neither mixed into the liturgy nor given liturgical form (§13); the local ordinary approves public devotional prayers (§21). **Relics:** Trent XXV (decree, no canons) teaches that the saints' bodies, once members of Christ and temples of the Spirit, are to be venerated, condemns those who deny it, and orders "every superstition shall be removed, all filthy lucre be abolished"; relics may not be sold (can. 1190 §1); pastors secure authenticity and prevent fraud, trafficking and superstition, no fragmenting or collecting, not on the altar's mensa, and may take relics to the sick (Directory §237); a seller's certificate is not the Church's authentication. **First Fridays:** revived frequent Communion; against mere credulity (§171). Medals are never talismans (§206). The *sensus fidei* lives in popular piety without being identical to it ([`fundamental-theology` 5.3](../fundamental-theology/lessons/05-03-the-sensus-fidei.md)). The Marian case is [`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.5](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-05-mary-in-the-economy-in-the-church-and-in-devotion.md)'s; what approving a private revelation means is [`fundamental-theology` 1.4](../fundamental-theology/lessons/01-04-public-and-private-revelation.md)'s. **The course's closing rule:** a devotion says about God, Christ and the Church only what the liturgy itself says.

**Level:** SC 13's ground (the liturgy surpasses every devotion), **at least rung 3**; relic veneration, Trent's teaching in a decree without canons, **no rung assigned** (as the ecclesiology card gives none to the same decree on invoking saints); the Directory's guidelines, **changeable direction**; the closing rule, **no magisterial level**.

**Does not license:** "relic veneration is merely tolerated" (Trent teaches it), or "the Directory's criteria are dogma" (pastoral direction resting on SC 13; approval binds no one to a devotion's promises).

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Directory on Popular Piety

The Congregation for Divine Worship's *Directory on Popular Piety and the Liturgy: Principles and Guidelines* (decree 17 December 2001; published 2002).

Papal approval by Secretariat of State letter of 14 December 2001. Principles §§11-21: Sunday Mass binds, no devotion does (§11); the Gospel the measure, with a biblical, liturgical, ecumenical and anthropological "spirit" (§12); devotion and liturgy not mixed (§13); public devotional prayers approved by the local ordinary (§21). Guéranger and the liturgical movement (§§44, 46). Dangers, including superstition and magic (§64). §75 restates the criteria, its note 93 citing *Marialis Cultus* 24-38: the four criteria originate in Paul VI's *Marialis Cultus* (2 February 1974) §§29-37 ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 6.5](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/06-05-mary-in-the-economy-in-the-church-and-in-devotion.md)). First Fridays (§171); medals (§206); relics (§237).

**Level:** a dicastery's practical document; its restatements of SC keep their weight; its guidelines, **changeable direction, no rung**.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

### Superstition

The named failure mode: worship offered to whom it ought not, or in a manner it ought not; faith expects the result from God, superstition from the procedure.

Aquinas, ST II-II q.92 a.1: "superstition is a vice contrary to religion by excess, not that it offers more to the divine worship than true religion, but because it offers divine worship either to whom it ought not, or in a manner it ought not". q.92 a.2, its species: undue worship of the true God, idolatry, divination, observances. q.93 a.2: the undue manner is worship of mere externals, cut off from interior worship. q.96 a.4: sacred words or relics worn about the neck are lawful, but superstitious if hope rests in the manner of writing or fastening; ad 3, if account is taken of a vain circumstance (a three-cornered casket). CCC 2110-2111: forbidden by the first commandment; crediting prayers or sacramental signs with an effect from mere external performance, apart from the dispositions they require. Trent XXV: "every superstition shall be removed"; XXII's reform decree banned "a fixed number of certain masses and of candles" as superstition ([Mass stipends](#mass-stipends)). The devout and the superstitious may perform the same act; the line runs through an expectation no pastor sees, so the Church regulates what she can see and catechesis does the rest. No moral-theology lesson treats superstition or the virtue of religion ([`moral-theology`](../moral-theology/syllabus.md)).

**Level:** its sinfulness, **at least rung 3** (CCC 2110-2111); Aquinas's analysis, **no magisterial level**.

*Lessons:* [7.3](lessons/07-03-sacramentals-and-popular-devotion.md)

## Other traditions in their own words

Each position as its own confessions and theologians state it, with their names and documents, and **no reply attached**: the arguing belongs to [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md), which takes this course as a prerequisite for exactly that reason. A Catholic reply volunteered inside one of these statements is a named wrong turn in this course's problems, and so is stating a position below its strength.

### Lutheran confessions on the sacraments

The sacraments are signs and testimonies of God's will toward us, to awaken and confirm faith: so they profit only when received in faith in the promise, and the schools' *ex opere operato* without a good disposition is rejected. Not bare symbols.

**Augsburg Confession** (1530), art. 13: the sacraments were instituted not only as marks of profession among men but chiefly as signs and testimonies of God's will toward us, to awaken and confirm faith, so they must be used with faith in the promises they offer; it condemns those who teach that they justify *ex opere operato* without requiring that faith (Schaff, *Creeds of Christendom* III). **Apology of the Augsburg Confession** (Melanchthon, 1531), art. 13 (Bente and Dau, *Triglot Concordia*, 1921): "we condemn the whole crowd of scholastic doctors, who teach that the Sacraments confer grace ex opere operato, without a good disposition on the part of the one using them, provided he do not place a hindrance in the way"; sacraments are signs and seals of God's promises, and a promise profits no one unless received by faith (the Latin tag *sine bono motu utentis* occurs in art. 24). **The number:** sacraments are rites with God's command and an added promise of grace, since no human authority can promise grace: baptism, the Lord's Supper and absolution; confirmation and extreme unction are rites received from the Fathers, without God's command; ordination, if it means the ministry of the Word, it will call a sacrament, and marriage too, if kept distinct from the New Testament's signs; "no prudent man will strive greatly concerning the number or the term", provided what has God's command and promise is kept. **Luther**, *The Babylonian Captivity of the Church* (1520, paraphrased): not baptism but faith in the word of promise, to which baptism is added, justifies; he denies seven, holds for the present to three (baptism, penance, the bread), and by the end strictly two, since only baptism and the bread have both a divinely instituted sign and the promise of forgiveness. **Small Catechism** (1529), Part IV: baptism works forgiveness, delivers from death and the devil, gives everlasting salvation to all who believe; "It is not water, indeed, that does it, but the Word of God which is with and in the water, and faith, which trusts in the Word of God in the water" (Schaff's translation).

*Lessons:* [1.3](lessons/01-03-ex-opere-operato-character-and-fruitfulness.md), [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md); the Catholic teaching it is set beside: [ex opere operato](#ex-opere-operato), [obex](#obex), [the number seven](#the-number-seven)

### Anglican Articles on the sacraments

Two sacraments ordained of Christ in the Gospel; five "commonly called Sacraments" that lack a visible sign ordained of God; the sacrifices of Masses rejected.

**Thirty-Nine Articles** (1571), art. 25: two sacraments ordained of Christ our Lord in the Gospel, baptism and the Supper of the Lord; the "five commonly called Sacraments" (confirmation, penance, orders, matrimony, extreme unction) are not to be counted for sacraments of the Gospel, having no visible sign or ceremony ordained of God. Art. 31: the offering of Christ once made is the perfect redemption, propitiation and satisfaction for all the sins of the whole world, and there is none other satisfaction for sin but that alone; so the sacrifices of Masses for the quick and the dead "were blasphemous fables, and dangerous deceits" ([Lutheran and Reformed on the sacrifice of the Mass](#lutheran-and-reformed-on-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass)). **On Anglican orders:** the archbishops of Canterbury and York, *Saepius Officio* (1897), answering *Apostolicae Curae*, point to the Ordinal's language of sacrifice and to changes in Rome's own rites (Schaff's summary, *Creeds* vol. 2). The Church of England's ordination of women: [communions that ordain women](#communions-that-ordain-women).

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md), [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md), [5.1](lessons/05-01-holy-orders-the-three-degrees.md)

### Orthodox on the number of mysteries

The early modern Orthodox confessions count seven; many Orthodox writers today add that no pan-Orthodox council made the number exclusive.

Pseudo-Dionysius, *On the Ecclesiastical Hierarchy* (around 500), describes six sacred rites (illumination, synaxis, consecration of the myron, priestly consecrations, monastic consecration, rites over the dead). Patriarch Jeremiah II's replies to Lutheran theologians (1570s) teach seven. Peter Mogila's *Orthodox Confession* (approved 1643), Q. 98, pairs the seven mysteries with the seven gifts of the Spirit. The Synod of Jerusalem (1672), Decree XV (lesson's translation of the Latin version, Schaff, *Creeds* vol. 2): "We believe that there are evangelical sacraments in the Church, and that they are seven. We admit in the Church neither a smaller nor a larger number of sacraments, since any number other than seven is the offspring of heretics' madness." Many Orthodox writers today add that no pan-Orthodox council has made the number exclusive, and that the systematic count came from Latin school theology (the lesson names none). The number was not among the issues at Lyons or Florence ([`church-history` 4.2](../church-history/lessons/04-02-east-and-west-the-long-estrangement.md)).

**Does not license:** "the Orthodox reject the seven".

*Lessons:* [1.4](lessons/01-04-why-seven.md)

### Anabaptist and Baptist on infant baptism

Baptism follows believing and is the believer's own confession, which no one can make for another: so it belongs to those who profess repentance and faith.

The objection is not the magisterial Reformers' (Luther, Zwingli and Calvin kept infant baptism) but the Anabaptists' and the Baptists'. The **Schleitheim articles** (24 February 1527; [`church-history` 6.2](../church-history/lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)), art. 1, paraphrased: baptism is for those who have learned repentance and amendment of life, believe that Christ has taken away their sins, and ask for it themselves; infant baptism is excluded. The **Baptist confession** printed in London in 1677 and reissued in 1689 calls baptism a sign of the believer's fellowship with Christ, then (ch. 29 §2, in Schaff, *Creeds of Christendom* vol. 3): "Those who do actually profess repentance towards God, faith in and obedience to our Lord Jesus, are the only proper subjects of this ordinance." **The argument, in its defenders' order:** in the New Testament baptism follows believing ("They therefore that received his word were baptized", Acts 2:41; Mark 16:16); it is the believer's own confession; no text records an infant's baptism, and the jailer's household heard the word first (Acts 16:32-33); of the new covenant God says "all shall know me" (Jer 31:34), so its sign belongs to those who know him.

**Does not license:** attributing the objection to "the Reformation traditions" at large.

*Lessons:* [2.2](lessons/02-02-necessity-infants-and-baptism-of-desire-and-blood.md); the Catholic teaching: [infant baptism](#infant-baptism)

### Luther Zwingli Calvin on the Supper

Three Reformation positions, all rejecting transubstantiation and differing from one another: Christ's body truly present with the unchanged bread (Lutheran); signified to faith (Zwingli); truly communicated by the Spirit across the distance (Calvin).

**The Lutheran confessions.** Augsburg Confession (1530), art. 10: the body and blood of Christ "are truly present, and are distributed to those who eat in the Supper of the Lord" (*Triglot*, 1921). Smalcald Articles (1537), III.6: the bread and wine "are the true body and blood of Christ", received "not only by the godly, but also by wicked Christians"; transubstantiation a "sophistical subtlety", for Paul still calls it bread (1 Cor 10:16). Formula of Concord (1577), Solid Declaration VII.35: "under the bread, with the bread, in the bread", to reject transubstantiation and mark the sacramental union of the bread's unchanged essence with Christ's body; "in, with and under" is the later shorthand.

**Zwingli.** "This is my body" means "this signifies my body" (*est* = *significat*), argued from John 6:63 (DR 6:64), "the flesh profiteth nothing" (*Commentary on True and False Religion*, 1525); the Supper is the community's thankful commemoration of Christ's death, and his ascended body is in heaven. His *Exposition of the Faith* for Francis I (1531) still affirms Christ truly present to the believing heart, his body eaten sacramentally and spiritually (Schaff, *History of the Christian Church* VII §111). At Marburg (1529) he and Luther agreed on fourteen articles and recorded their split on the fifteenth: whether Christ's body and blood are corporally present in the bread and wine ([`church-history` 6.2](../church-history/lessons/06-02-reformations-beyond-wittenberg.md)).

**Calvin**, *Institutes* IV.17 (Beveridge's translation, 1845): the Supper is not "a vain or empty sign" (10); Christ's body is "contained in heaven" until the judgment, neither enclosed under the elements nor everywhere (12); yet his flesh truly feeds us: "what our mind does not comprehend let faith conceive—viz. that the Spirit truly unites things separated by space" (10); Christ offers the thing signified to all at the table, believers alone receive it to their benefit (10); a "true and substantial communication" of the body and blood (19).

| | The bread's substance | Who receives the body | Where the body is |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lateran IV, Trent | converted | every communicant; fruitfully only the disposed | under the species |
| Luther | remains, in sacramental union | the mouth, godly and wicked alike | under, with, in the bread |
| Calvin | remains | believers, by the Spirit | in heaven |
| Zwingli | remains | the believing heart | in heaven |

No column records what still separates Calvin and Zwingli: whether the Supper *conveys* the body by the Spirit or *signifies* it to faith.

**Does not license:** sorting them by the ninth-century question, figure or truth (Augsburg says "truly present", Calvin "true and substantial", the late Zwingli "truly present" to faith).

**Argued in:** [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md) 6.4.

*Lessons:* [3.2](lessons/03-02-the-controversies-that-forced-precision.md)

### Orthodox on metousiosis

The Synod of Jerusalem (1672) used *metousiosis* for the change and refused to let the word explain the manner; later Orthodox writers differ over the word.

The Synod of Jerusalem (1672), whose decrees are called the Confession of Dositheus, answered the Calvinist-leaning *Confession* issued under Cyril Lucaris's name. Its **Decree 17** stacks four verbs: the bread is changed (*metaballesthai*), transubstantiated (*metousiousthai*), made over (*metapoieisthai*) and refashioned (*metarrhythmizesthai*) into the Lord's true body; places that body under the bread's accidents (*sumbebēkota*), where Trent says "species"; and limits its own key word (lesson's translation of the Greek): "By the word *metousiosis* we do not believe the manner is explained ... for that is wholly beyond grasp except to God himself ... but that the bread becomes truly, really and substantially the Lord's true body." Peter Mogila's *Orthodox Confession* (1643), Q. 107, uses *metousiosis* for the change at the epiclesis ([Orthodox on the epiclesis](#orthodox-on-the-epiclesis)). Later Orthodox writers differ: some use the word as Jerusalem did; others prefer the Byzantine epiclesis's verb, *metaballō*, "to change" (lesson's gloss), and decline any account of how (the lesson names none).

*Lessons:* [3.3](lessons/03-03-real-presence-and-transubstantiation.md)

### Lutheran and Reformed on the sacrifice of the Mass

Christ's death alone is truly a propitiatory sacrifice; the Mass may be a sacrifice of thanksgiving by the reconciled, not an offering that obtains forgiveness for others by the work done.

**Augsburg Confession** (1530), art. 24 (Schaff's translation): its churches have not abolished the Mass, which is kept with reverence; it rejects the opinion that Christ's passion satisfied for original sin and the Mass was instituted as an oblation for daily sins, taking away the sins of the living and the dead *ex opere operato*, "for the doing of the work"; that diminishes the glory of the passion, since "by one oblation" Christ has perfected the sanctified (Heb 10:14), and puts justification in the work of Masses, not in faith; the Mass exists so that faith recalls Christ's benefits and the fearful conscience is comforted; it complains of Masses "used for gain". **Apology** (1531), art. 24 (Bente and Dau): "One is the propitiatory sacrifice, i.e., ... one that reconciles God, or appeases God's wrath ... The other species is the eucharistic sacrifice, which does not merit the remission of sins or reconciliation, but is rendered by those who have been reconciled"; "The death of Christ alone is truly a propitiatory sacrifice"; prayer, thanksgiving, preaching and the saints' afflictions are of the second kind, which Malachi's "pure offering" (1:11) means; the Mass may be called a daily sacrifice if the whole Mass is meant, not a ceremony profiting others *ex opere operato*. **Heidelberg Catechism** (1563), Q. 80: the Supper witnesses full forgiveness by Christ's one sacrifice once accomplished, against a Mass that offers forgiveness only if Christ is "still daily offered" by priests: "a denial of the one sacrifice and passion of Jesus Christ". **Thirty-Nine Articles** (1571), art. 31: Christ's offering once made is the perfect redemption, propitiation and satisfaction, and there is no other; so the sacrifices of Masses "were blasphemous fables, and dangerous deceits".

*Lessons:* [3.4](lessons/03-04-the-sacrifice-of-the-mass.md); the Catholic teaching: [sacrifice of the Mass](#sacrifice-of-the-mass)

### Calvin on anointing

James's oil belonged to a temporary gift of healing, now ceased: no sacrament.

Calvin, *Institutes* IV.19.18-19 (paraphrased): the oil of James 5 belonged to the gift of healing, a temporary gift that has ceased. The Reformers counted no sacrament here; the Apology called extreme unction a rite received from the Fathers ([Lutheran confessions on the sacraments](#lutheran-confessions-on-the-sacraments)).

*Lessons:* [4.3](lessons/04-03-anointing-of-the-sick.md); the Catholic teaching: [anointing of the sick](#anointing-of-the-sick)

### Communions that ordain women

Two arguments carry most of the weight: from baptism (in Christ there is neither male nor female) and from the incarnation (what Christ assumed and saves is the humanity women share).

The Church of Sweden decided in 1958 to ordain women, and has since 1960; the US Episcopal Church's General Convention authorized it in 1976; the Church of England's General Synod voted for it in 1992, thirty-two women were ordained priests in 1994, and women bishops followed in 2014-2015. On 9 July 1975 Archbishop Donald Coggan told Paul VI of a slow but steady consensus growing in the Anglican Communion that there was no fundamental objection in principle (paraphrased). **From baptism:** "There is neither Jew nor Greek: there is neither bond nor free: there is neither male nor female. For you are all one in Christ Jesus" (Gal 3:28, DR). Krister Stendahl, a Lutheran exegete in the Swedish debate (*The Bible and the Role of Women*, English 1966), read the verse as the new creation breaking into the Church's own order, not only as equal standing before God. **From the incarnation:** "For that which He has not assumed He has not healed" (Gregory Nazianzen, Letter 101, NPNF; [`christology` 2.1](../christology/lessons/02-01-apollinaris-a-complete-humanity.md)). Richard A. Norris Jr., in a 1975 paper for the United States Anglican-Roman Catholic Consultation, argued that the Fathers took no christological interest in Jesus' maleness; the saving point is the humanity women share with him, so a priest represents Christ by that humanity, and requiring maleness adds a premise the tradition never used.

*Lessons:* [5.2](lessons/05-02-the-reservation-of-priestly-ordination.md); the Catholic teaching: [Ordinatio Sacerdotalis](#ordinatio-sacerdotalis), [Inter Insigniores](#inter-insigniores)

### Orthodox crowning

In the Byzantine rite the priest joins and crowns the couple: marriage is no juridical contract, and the crowned are witnesses and heirs of the Kingdom.

The Office of Holy Matrimony (Hapgood's *Service Book*, 1906): the priest asks God to "conjoin this thy servant, N., and this thy handmaid, N.; for by thee is the husband united unto the wife", then crowns each in the name of the Trinity and blesses them three times: "O Lord our God, crown them with glory and honour". The Orthodox Church in America's catechism *The Orthodox Faith* (vol. 2, *Worship*): marriage "is not a juridical contract"; the service "contains no vows or oaths"; by the Church's recognition and incorporation into Christ's Body the union becomes an image of God's unending love; the crowned are witnesses (martyrs) and heirs of the Kingdom. In Catholic teaching the Eastern minister is the priest who crowns (CCC 1623; [minister of matrimony](#minister-of-matrimony)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)

### Westminster Confession on marriage

Two sacraments only; marriage no sacrament; after adultery the innocent party may divorce and remarry.

Westminster Confession (1647): two sacraments only, baptism and the Lord's Supper (27.4). On marriage (24.5): "In the case of adultery after marriage, it is lawful for the innocent party to sue out a divorce, and after the divorce to marry another, as if the offending party were dead"; 24.6 adds wilful desertion that no remedy can cure. The dissolution reading of Matt 19:9 ([matrimony](#matrimony)).

*Lessons:* [5.3](lessons/05-03-matrimony-sacrament-contract-indissolubility.md)

### Oikonomia

"Stewardship" (lesson's gloss): the Orthodox Church's application of a rule with a view to salvation, against *akribeia*, "exactness"; by it second and even third marriages are permitted, penitentially.

The Holy and Great Council in Crete, *The Sacrament of Marriage and Its Impediments* (Pentecost 2016): the indissoluble union of man and woman is an icon of the unity of Christ and the Church (I.2); a marriage not completely dissolved or annulled, and a third marriage, are absolute impediments, and a fourth is condemned (II.2); *oikonomia* over impediments belongs to each autocephalous Church's synod (II.5.2). The Orthodox Church in America's pastoral answer (website): the Church sees marriage as lifelong and opposes divorce, but for its people's salvation permits a second and even a third marriage, performed by "economy" as an exception, in a service with a penitential character. Basil's canonical letters, received among the canons by the Council in Trullo (692, can. 2): a year's penance for a second marriage (Letter 188, can. 4); a deserted husband pardonable, the woman who lives with him not condemned (can. 9); Trullo can. 87. John Meyendorff, *Marriage: An Orthodox Perspective* (St Vladimir's Seminary Press, 1984), a fuller Orthodox account. At Trent, Venice's plea for the Greeks of its territories, who allowed remarriage after adultery, changed the wording of XXIV can. 7 ([Trent Session XXIV](#trent-session-xxiv)). In Catholic doctrine the Orthodox Churches are true particular Churches ([`ecclesiology-and-mariology` 5.2](../ecclesiology-and-mariology/lessons/05-02-subsistit-in-and-the-status-of-other-churches.md)).

**Level:** no Catholic level.

**Argued in:** [`apologetics-catholic-claims`](../apologetics-catholic-claims/syllabus.md).

*Lessons:* [5.4](lessons/05-04-nullity-dissolution-and-the-pastoral-debates.md)

### Orthodox on the epiclesis

The change happens by the working of the Spirit invoked in the epiclesis; the Lord's words are repeated as narrative. Orthodox texts differ on how to place the words.

The Byzantine anaphora, after narrative and anamnesis: "send thy Holy Spirit upon us, and upon these Gifts here spread forth", "Transmuting them by thy Holy Spirit" (Hapgood 1906). The *Orthodox Confession* drawn up under Peter Mogila and signed by the Eastern patriarchs (1643), Part I, Q. 107 (lesson's translation of the Latin in Schaff): "When the priest consecrates the gifts, he must hold that the very substance of bread and wine is changed into that of Christ's true body and blood by the working of the Holy Spirit, whose power he then implores"; as soon as those words are said the change (*metousiosis*) takes place. A note in the modern Greek service books (printed by Brightman, 1896) calls the deacon's pointing at the gifts during "This is my body" contrary to the right mind of the Eastern Church: there the Lord's words are repeated *diēgēmatikōs*, "as narrative" (lesson's gloss), referring to what Jesus took then. Philaret's Longer Catechism, approved by the Russian Holy Synod: "the most essential act" is Christ's words "and after this the invocation of the Holy Ghost" (Q. 338 in Schaff's numbering, Blackmore's translation).

**Level:** no Catholic level; the Latin teaching is [epiclesis](#epiclesis).

*Lessons:* [6.3](lessons/06-03-the-roman-rite-and-the-eastern-rites.md)

## Thinkers and texts

Every author and non-magisterial text the lessons rely on, grouped so the other side is never far from your own. Church acts are in [the levels table](#levels-of-authority-used-in-this-course). Years are as the lessons confirmed them. **PD** marks a public-domain text the lessons quote; everything else is paraphrased. None of these has a magisterial level: a Father, a Doctor, a commission or a historian is weighed by his or its argument.
